# Teaching paths: DL Notes 1001–1090

Brief: `docs/teaching-paths.md` (items 1–6) and `docs/NOTE-RULES.md` §13. Range: Notes 1001–1090. For 1067–1090 the 3Blue1Brown mapping in `docs/3b1b-transformers.md` is reused and only what it misses is added.

## Summary

### Coverage

- **Blocks:** 90 of 90 Notes have a block with all six parts (Sources, Teaching path, What the Note lacks, Animation ideas, CampusX vs the others, Contradictions).
- **CampusX source:** 1001–1084 each map to their CampusX video (English Whisper transcripts `transcripts/Dnnn.whisper-en.txt`; times marked "~" are estimated from word position × video length, because these transcripts have no timestamps). 1085–1090 have no CampusX video.
- **Outside videos opened (27 caption files in `transcripts/teaching-video/`, ID, title, channel and length confirmed with yt-dlp):** 3Blue1Brown ch 1–4 and "But what is a convolution?"; StatQuest neural networks main ideas, multiple inputs/outputs, cross entropy, argmax/softmax, backprop main ideas and details pt 1–2, chain rule, gradient descent, SGD, ridge, ReLU in action, GELU/SiLU/SwiGLU, CNN, RNN, LSTM, seq2seq, attention, transformer, transformer matrix math, word embedding, decoder-only transformer. The 3b1b transformer chapters (Ch 5, 6, 7, the LLM short, cross-entropy) come from `transcripts/3b1b/`.
- **Khan Academy:** no deep-learning videos; only neuron-biology videos (used as a side note in 1041).
- **Every beat** was checked against transcript text, not memory. Citations in clashes that were not opened in this session say so ("not opened here").

**Notes with no outside video (CampusX is the only video source):** 1001, 1005, 1011, 1013, 1021, 1022, 1023, 1024, 1025, 1029, 1030, 1031, 1032, 1033, 1034, 1035, 1036, 1037, 1038, 1039, 1041, 1045, 1048, 1049, 1050, 1051, 1053, 1054, 1064, 1065, 1066 (31 Notes). That is the whole optimiser block (1032–1038), initialisation and batch norm (1029–1031), regularisation (1022–1025), the CNN project and transfer-learning Notes (1048–1054), and GRU/stacked/bidirectional RNNs (1064–1066).

**Only a segment or one line from outside:** 1003, 1004, 1006, 1007, 1018, 1019, 1028, 1043, 1047, 1059, 1063, 1070, 1079 (one StatQuest sentence on layer norm, and it is wrong; see below).

**No CampusX video; 3b1b (and for 1086–1087, StatQuest) only:** 1085 (CampusX D080–D084 together), 1086, 1087, 1088, 1089, 1090.

### Top 10 animation ideas

| # | Note | Idea | Tool | Why it clicks |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1009 | StatQuest's squiggle rebuilt with his exact numbers: two softplus pieces, scaled, summed, shifted, landing on the three dosage groups | Plotly frames | Shows a hidden layer *building* a curve, piece by piece |
| 2 | 1015 | 3b1b nudges on the Note's own 2-2-1 network (ŷ = 0.32 must rise): arrows on weights sized by activations, then "wish arrows" passed back | Manim | Backprop as "who should change, and by how much", before any calculus |
| 3 | 1016 | Four number lines (w, z, a, C) with the Note's numbers; nudge w, watch each dot move; the three ratios multiply to the derivative | Manim | The chain rule seen as linked slopes |
| 4 | 1014 | p slides 0.99 → 0.01; −ln p vs (1 − p)² with tangent slopes printed | Plotly frames | Why cross-entropy keeps pushing when the answer is badly wrong |
| 5 | 1018 / 1060 | Gradient bar travelling back through 10 sigmoid layers shrinking by the Note's measured factors; wᵏ for w = 0.5, 1, 2 | Plotly frames | Vanishing and exploding as repeated multiplication |
| 6 | 1024 | A new random mask greys out nodes every training step; counter "sub-network #k of 2ⁿ" | Manim | Dropout is per step, not per epoch (fixes a CampusX error) |
| 7 | 1040 | StatQuest's 6 × 6 O/X through filter, ReLU, max-pool, dense; then the X shifted one pixel | Manim | The whole CNN pipeline in one toy, plus why it tolerates shifts |
| 8 | 1061 | The Note's Pratapgarh story as two lanes (short-term, long-term) with input and forget gates acting | Manim | The LSTM's two memories as a story a beginner can retell |
| 9 | 1073 | "money bank grows": dot products → softmax bars → weighted value arrows summing to the new "bank"; then StatQuest's 3-token matrices one operation per frame | Manim + Plotly | Self-attention invented step by step, then the same with every number |
| 10 | 1078 | Each sine–cosine pair as a clock hand; moving k positions turns every hand by a fixed angle | Manim | Why positional encoding uses sin and cos together (relative position = rotation) |

### Contradictions

**A. Where a Note itself needs a fix or more evidence**
- 1002: Note §5.5 line 276 "neural networks from the 1960s" repeats CampusX; McCulloch–Pitts 1943, Rosenblatt 1958 (Note 1003 already says 1958).
- 1072: the per-coordinate embedding picture (royalty, athletics) points the wrong way; meaning is in directions (3b1b doc §6 item 3; Note 1086 owns it).
- 1079: the only reason given for "batch norm fails in transformers" is padding; padding can be masked. Add evidence that batch statistics fluctuate in NLP (Shen et al. 2020, PowerNorm; not opened here).
- 1080: lines 270–271 can be read as "attention is linear"; the softmax weights depend non-linearly on the input. Add half a sentence.
- 1053: the low fine-tuning learning rate needs its stated reason (large updates wreck pretrained features), not "experience".
- 1021: check that the batch-size claims cite Keskar et al. 2017 and Goyal et al. 2017.
- 1052: do not reuse CampusX's photo of a real person.

**B. CampusX wrong, the Note right** (one line each; details in the blocks)
- History and facts: 1001 GANs promised but not in the playlist; 1002 "linear" data scaling (it is a power law); 1003 dates (1958, *Perceptrons* 1969, Cybenko 1989); 1041 Hubel–Wiesel 1959/1962; 1044 224 not 228; 1045 number (not size) of filters grows, US Postal Service zip codes; 1051 GoogLeNet 2014, ResNet 2015, ImageNet 14.2 M images; 1067 45 TB vs 570 GB, RLHF reward model; 1071 DALL·E 2 is diffusion not GAN, ChatGPT from GPT-3.5.
- Definitions: 1005 an epoch is a full pass, not one pick; 1006 perceptron loss is not hinge loss; 1009 one hidden layer suffices for universal approximation; 1023 standardised data is not inside ±1, min–max is the outlier-sensitive one; 1024 dropout mask per step not per epoch; 1028 ReLU is continuous, ELU α = 1; 1030 Xavier/He are derived, not "jugaad"; 1031 BN output is not in 0–1, Keras stores moving variance; 1036 AdaGrad converges on convex problems; 1037 RMSProp ρ = 0.9; 1038 Adam β₂ = 0.999, lr 0.001, square root in the step; 1039 the test set was used for tuning; 1060 ReLU slope is 0 or 1, LSTM is not a cure for exploding gradients; 1066 bidirectional RNNs leak in forecasting.
- Arithmetic and wording: 1014 and 1068 base-10 logs (frameworks use natural logs); 1017 −5 − 0.01 × (−50) = −4.5; 1018 sigmoid slope max 0.25; 1025 "10% more" is 13×; 1042 765 not 255; 1043 stride formula slip; 1062 3 biases not 4; 1075 10/√2 = 7.07 and softmax(7.07, 22.63) ≈ (0, 1), not (0.2, 0.8); y_bank lies between value vectors, not embeddings; 1078 "each next pair has higher frequency" (it is lower) and misread sine values.
- Transformer reasoning: 1074 a saturated softmax kills **every** gradient, not just the small scores; Var(q·k) = d_k exactly, not "roughly"; 1077 attention heads do not cleanly "capture one meaning" each; 1078 "adding saves 2× training time" has no source; 1080 "self-attention is linear" (softmax is non-linear) and "nobody knows why residuals" (He et al. 2016); 1081 "non-autoregressive generators don't exist" (Gu et al. 2018); 1083 training does not use argmax.

**C. StatQuest or 3Blue1Brown wrong or incomplete**
- 1077 StatQuest: the 8 heads are "stacked" (they run in parallel; layers are stacked).
- 1078 StatQuest: its positional values (−0.9, 0.4 for word 2) do not come from the Vaswani formula (sin 1 = 0.84, cos 1 = 0.54).
- 1079 / 1085 StatQuest: "normalize after positional encoding"; Vaswani applies dropout there, LayerNorm only after sub-layers.
- 1087 StatQuest decoder-only video: sine–cosine positions (GPT learns them); "12 cells" blurs 12 layers × 12 heads; masked attention called "autoregressive"; implies the encoder–decoder drops the mask at inference (CampusX D084 and Note 1084 keep it, and 1084 checks it).
- 1090 3b1b: 10 000 vectors in 100-d cannot all sit within 89–91°; the Note proves at least one pair is ≥ 5.7° off.
- 1074 3b1b "numerical stability" (incomplete), 1082 3b1b cross-attention omits values, 1088 3b1b separate W_U (GPT-2 ties it): all already in the 3b1b doc §7.
- 1061 StatQuest "LSTM avoids exploding gradients": it mitigates vanishing; clipping still handles explosions.

### Worst gaps

1. **31 Notes have no outside video**, including the whole optimiser block (1032–1038) and BN/initialisation (1029–1031). These Notes rely on CampusX plus their own figures, so their animations matter most.
2. **1079 layer norm:** the only outside line (StatQuest) is wrong, and the Note's main argument (padding) is not the full published reason.
3. **1080 / 1074 transformer "why" claims:** CampusX's reasoning is wrong in places (linear attention, which gradients die); the Notes are right but 1080 needs one clarifying sentence.
4. **1002 history date** repeated in the Note.

## Notes 1001–1090

Conventions used in every block:
- **CampusX timestamps** carry a "~". The DL transcripts (`transcripts/Dnnn.whisper-en.txt`, the English Whisper translation of `dl_map/transcripts/nnn.hi-orig.txt`) have no time codes, so the time is estimated from the word position in the transcript times the video length in `dl_map/playlist_durations.txt`. Expect an error of about ±1 min.
- **Outside-video timestamps** are exact, from the `.txt` files in `transcripts/teaching-video/` (one line per 30 s).
- CampusX video for Note 10NN is DL playlist Video NNN (`dl_map/playlist.txt`), linked as https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ID.

### 1001 DL scope and prerequisites

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "100 Days of Deep Learning | Course Announcement", 2dH_qjc9mFg, 18:32. Transcript `transcripts/D001.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video. This Note is a course map; StatQuest, 3Blue1Brown and Khan Academy have no "scope of a DL course" video. The prerequisite CampusX names, 3Blue1Brown's *Essence of Linear Algebra* ("watch its first 5 videos", ~13:30), is already mapped by the maths Notes (350–530).

**Teaching path** (CampusX order, the only source)
1. ~03:00 Four families in order: ANN (simplest), CNN (image data), RNN (text), then GANs/autoencoders and object detection/segmentation. Visual: a curriculum document scrolled on screen.
2. ~04:30 ANN part, about 30–35 of 100 days: perceptron → its problem → MLP → notation → prediction → training (backprop, gradient descent) → Keras on one regression and one classification problem → improving a network (vanishing/exploding gradients, dropout, regularization, optimizers, batch norm, tuning).
3. ~10:30 Software: TensorFlow + Keras ("industry"), PyTorch ("research").
4. ~12:00 Prerequisites: Python basics; the ML flow "how the model is trained, how the data is prepared, how it is served"; linear algebra (matrices, vectors, dot product).

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing in content. The Note adds derivatives as a fourth prerequisite (CampusX names three); the Note marks this with an Extra box and a link, which is correct.
- The roadmap and `prereq_neuron.png` figures are stills. A beginner gains from seeing *where* each prerequisite is used inside one prediction.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: one neuron σ(Wx + b) assembled piece by piece; as each piece lands, a label lights up the prerequisite it needs (vector x → linear algebra, W·x → dot product, σ → functions, gradient arrow → derivatives).
2. Plotly frames: the four families as four tiles that fill in as the Note numbers scroll 1004 → 1090 (one frame per family).

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source. CampusX adds the industry/research split for frameworks, which the Note keeps.

**Contradictions**
- Scope: CampusX promises GANs, autoencoders, object detection and segmentation; the actual playlist ends at transformers (Video 84). The Note's Extra box says these are not covered. **The Note is right**: `dl_map/playlist.txt` ends at "Transformer Inference".

### 1002 What is deep learning

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "What is Deep Learning? Deep Learning Vs Machine Learning", fHF22Wxuyw4, 66:58. `transcripts/D002.whisper-en.txt`.
2. 3Blue1Brown, "But what is a neural network? | Deep learning chapter 1", aircAruvnKk, 18:40. `transcripts/teaching-video/1002-3b1b-neural-network-ch1.txt`.
3. StatQuest, "The Essential Main Ideas of Neural Networks", CqOfi41LfDw, 18:54. `transcripts/teaching-video/1002-statquest-nn-main-ideas.txt` (main use is Note 1009/1010).
4. Khan Academy: none (searches return only biology "anatomy of a neuron" videos).

**Teaching path** (CampusX for the ML-vs-DL frame, 3b1b for "layers learn features")
1. CampusX ~03:00: nested circles AI ⊃ ML ⊃ DL. ML finds the input–output relationship with statistical techniques; DL with a neural network, "inspired by the human brain". Visual: Venn of the three terms.
2. CampusX ~06:00: one ANN diagram: circles = perceptrons, arrows = weights, input/hidden/output layers; "many hidden layers → deep". Visual: the labelled ANN.
3. 3b1b 00:00–00:30: a sloppy 28×28 "3"; three different "3"s whose pixels differ yet the brain sees one idea. Why it clicks: it states the problem a hand-written rule cannot solve.
4. CampusX ~15:00: dog-vs-cat classifier. ML: we hand-make features (size, colour); DL: give the image, the network finds features = **representation learning**.
5. 3b1b 05:30–07:30: layers of abstraction for digits: little edges → loop/line → digit (a 9 = loop on top + line on right; an 8 = two loops; a 4 = three lines). Visual: neurons lighting up layer by layer for one 9. 08:00: the same layering for speech (sounds → syllables → words → phrases). This is the clearest beginner picture of "higher layers find more complex features".
6. CampusX ~18:00 and ~27:00: the edges → shapes → face picture for images (the Note's `feature_learning.png`).
7. CampusX ~21:00–33:00: five ML-vs-DL differences with the "data vs performance" graph (ML plateaus, DL keeps rising), GPU vs CPU, training slow / prediction fast (KNN is slow at prediction), features (resume-placement project: 12th marks, 10th marks, achievements, courses, college quality need domain experts), interpretability (comment-ban example; logistic regression on CGPA and IQ gives readable w₁, w₂; a decision tree is a flow chart).
8. CampusX ~33:00: "needle vs sword" analogy: ML is not replaced by DL.
9. CampusX ~36:00–66:00: why now: datasets (smartphones + cheap data, e.g. Jio; MS COCO, YouTube-8M, SQuAD, AudioSet), hardware (Moore's law, GPU + CUDA, FPGA, ASIC/TPU, edge TPU, NPU), frameworks (DistBelief → TensorFlow 2015, Keras, PyTorch 2016, Caffe2), architectures + transfer learning (ResNet, BERT, U-Net, pix2pix, YOLO, WaveNet), community.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 3 (why a hand-written rule fails on three different "3"s) is missing: the Note asserts representation learning without first showing why we need it.
- Beat 5: the Note shows edges → shapes → face as a still image. There is no step that says *how* an edge neuron could work (3b1b 09:00–10:00: positive weights on a strip of pixels, negative weights around it). That one sentence turns "layers find features" from a claim into a mechanism. It belongs to Note 1009, with a one-line preview here.
- The "data vs performance" curve (CampusX ~21:00) has no figure in the Note (section 4.1 is text).
- The CampusX dataset examples (COCO, YouTube-8M, SQuAD, AudioSet) and the hardware ladder (CPU → GPU → TPU → edge TPU/NPU) have no figure; `why_now.png` lists only the five forces.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: a hand-drawn 9 enters a 4-layer net; edge neurons light, then "loop on top" and "vertical line" neurons, then the "9" output (3b1b's beat 5 rebuilt; use an MNIST 9 from Note 1012's data).
2. Plotly frames: the "data vs performance" curve: the x-axis (training-set size) grows frame by frame; the ML curve flattens and the DL curve keeps rising. Use real numbers: logistic regression vs a small MLP on MNIST subsets of 100, 1 000, 10 000 and 60 000 images.
3. Plotly frames: exponential data growth vs years (CampusX's "x, x, 2x, 4x" doubling, ~39:00) with the 2012 ImageNet moment marked.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Layers find features:** 3b1b is clearer. It decomposes one digit into named parts (loop, line, edges) and later shows how a weight grid detects an edge. CampusX states the edges → shapes → face idea from a slide, with no mechanism.
- **ML vs DL in practice:** only CampusX covers this (data, hardware, time, features, interpretability, why now). Keep CampusX as the main path; 3b1b and StatQuest do not compare with classical ML.
- **CampusX's new angles:** the resume-placement example for hand-made features, the comment-ban example for interpretability, the needle-vs-sword analogy, and India-specific context (Jio data prices). Keep all four.
- **StatQuest's angle:** "a neural network is a big fancy squiggle-fitting machine" (16:30). This is a useful one-line answer to "what does DL do", best placed in Note 1009.
- No clash between the sources.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX says DL performance grows "almost linearly" with data (~21:00). That is too strong: the deep-learning scaling literature shows a power law, so a straight line appears only on log–log axes (Hestness et al. 2017, "Deep Learning Scaling is Predictable, Empirically"). The Note says "keeps improving", which is the safe wording. **The Note is right.**
- CampusX says DL work began "in the 1960s from the time of Alan Turing" (~36:00). The perceptron is 1958 (Rosenblatt) and the McCulloch–Pitts neuron is 1943. The Note repeats it (section 5.5, line 276: "Researchers worked on neural networks from the 1960s"). **Both are wrong by date**: the McCulloch–Pitts neuron is 1943 and Rosenblatt's perceptron 1958; Note 1003 already says 1958. Fix to "since the 1940s and 1950s" and link Note 1003.

### 1003 Types of neural networks, history and applications

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Types of Neural Networks | History of Deep Learning | Applications of Deep Learning", fne_UE7hDn0, 33:16. `transcripts/D003.whisper-en.txt`.
2. Outside: none owns this topic. One supporting line: StatQuest, "The Essential Main Ideas of Neural Networks" (CqOfi41LfDw), 16:30–17:30: the name "neural network" comes from the 1940s–50s idea that nodes are "vaguely like neurons" and connections "like synapses"; "in theory neural networks can fit a green squiggle to just about any data set". This backs section 3.3 (universal approximation) with a picture.

**Teaching path** (CampusX order)
1. ~02:00–08:00 Five types, one picture each: MLP (layers of perceptrons; any supervised problem, non-linear relations), CNN (at least one convolutional layer; images, video; LeCun), RNN/LSTM (hidden output fed back; text, assistants), autoencoder (input and output layers equal, narrow middle: compress without losing quality), GAN (generator vs discriminator; faces of people who never existed).
2. ~08:00 Why history: the field is young and "feels like a Bollywood movie" (ups and downs).
3. ~08:00–10:00 Rosenblatt's perceptron and the media hype → 1969 Minsky: a perceptron cannot learn XOR → first AI winter.
4. ~10:00–12:00 Hinton (student, 1978–86) → 1986 backpropagation paper; many perceptrons in hidden layers can approximate non-linear functions; 1989 LeCun reads zip codes.
5. ~14:00 Second winter: four reasons (little labelled data, little compute, random weight initialisation, SVM and random forests win on small data).
6. ~14:00–16:00 2006 deep belief networks / unsupervised pre-training → rename to "deep learning" → 2012 ImageNet: error 28% (2010), 26% (2011), then ~16% with GPUs.
7. ~16:00–18:00 Afterwards: self-driving cars (2015), AlphaGo vs Lee Sedol (2016), GANs.
8. ~20:00–32:00 Applications: self-driving car sensors (cameras, LiDAR, GPS → computer → controls), AlphaGo, assistants; then colourising black-and-white films, sound for silent video, image captions, live sign translation, pixel restoration of old blurry photos, Google Photos grouping faces ("They grew up so fast"), GAN faces/music/handwriting/stories; medicine, finance (fraud, churn), education.

**What the Note lacks**
- Every type in section 2 has text but only one shared picture (`nn_types.png`). A beginner needs to see the **data flow** of each: feed-forward (MLP), sliding filter (CNN), a loop arrow (RNN), an hourglass (autoencoder), two players (GAN).
- The history beats have a timeline still (`timeline.png`) and an ImageNet bar chart: good. Missing: the XOR picture at the first winter (it is the reason for the winter). Link to Note 1007's XOR figure.
- Applications: CampusX's self-driving pipeline (sensors → network → steering) is the one application with a mechanism; the Note lists it without a diagram.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: "interest in neural networks" line drawn year by year 1943 → 2016 (use the Note's own `timeline.png` data); at each event the line pauses and a label appears (perceptron 1958, *Perceptrons* 1969, backprop 1986, DBN 2006, AlexNet 2012, AlphaGo 2016).
2. Manim: five mini-networks side by side, each animating its own data flow: a pulse going left→right (MLP), a 3×3 window sliding over a grid (CNN), a pulse looping back (RNN), a wide→narrow→wide hourglass rebuilding a digit (autoencoder), generator and discriminator passing an image back and forth (GAN).
3. Plotly frames: universal approximation: the Note's own figure (1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32 sigmoid neurons) as frames, so the fit visibly tightens.

**CampusX vs the others:** only CampusX covers types, history and applications as a whole. StatQuest adds one useful line ("neural networks are big fancy squiggle-fitting machines"), which the Note can use to introduce universal approximation in plain words. CampusX's angles to keep: "a Bollywood movie of ups and downs"; the four reasons for the second winter; old family photos restored.

**Contradictions** (CampusX vs records; the Note already sides with the records)
- "It all started in 1960s" (~08:00): Rosenblatt's perceptron is 1958. **The Note is right** (1958).
- Minsky "published a paper" (~10:00): it was the book *Perceptrons* (Minsky and Papert 1969). **The Note is right** (its Extra box).
- The 1986 backprop paper "says" universal approximation (~12:00): the theorem is Cybenko 1989 and Hornik 1991. **The Note is right** (its Extra box).
- AlexNet error "16%" (~16:00): 15.3% top-5 (Krizhevsky et al. 2012). **The Note is right.**
- AlphaGo beat Lee Sedol "in 5 straight games" then "4 times" (~16:00): 4–1. **The Note is right.** Lee Sedol retired in 2019, not 2018 (~22:00); the Note says November 2019, citing a Yonhap interview.

### 1004 Perceptron

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "What is a Perceptron? Perceptron Vs Neuron | Perceptron Geometric Intuition", X7iIKPoZ0Sw, 38:34. `transcripts/D004.whisper-en.txt`.
2. 3Blue1Brown, "But what is a neural network?" (aircAruvnKk), 09:00–11:30 only: one neuron as a weighted sum plus a bias through a squashing function. File `1002-3b1b-neural-network-ch1.txt`.
3. StatQuest and Khan Academy: no perceptron video (searched "StatQuest perceptron", "Khan Academy perceptron": no hit from either channel).

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~02:00–06:00: the diagram, part by part: inputs x₁, x₂; weights w₁, w₂; bias b on a constant input 1; summation block z = w₁x₁ + w₂x₂ + b ("dot product"); step function (z ≥ 0 → 1). Visual: the perceptron drawing, built left to right.
2. CampusX ~06:00–12:00: placement data (IQ, CGPA, placed), e.g. (78, 7.8, 1) and (69, 5.1, 0). Two stages, training (find w₁, w₂, b) and prediction. **Worked example:** w₁ = 1, w₂ = 2, b = 3; new student IQ 100, CGPA 5.1 → z = 100 + 10.2 + 3 = 113.2 ≥ 0 → placed.
3. CampusX ~12:00: a third input (state: Maharashtra, West Bengal, UP) adds x₃ and w₃; nothing else changes.
4. 3b1b 10:30–11:30: **what the bias means**: "maybe you only want it to be active when the sum is bigger than say 10 … add −10 … the bias tells you how high the weighted sum needs to be before the neuron starts getting meaningfully active." CampusX says "what does this bias do? … we will do it in the future" (~22:00) and never returns to it in this video. 3b1b's sentence fills the gap.
5. CampusX ~14:00–20:00: neuron vs perceptron: dendrites ≈ inputs, nucleus ≈ sum + step, axon ≈ output. Three differences: a neuron is complex; its processing is unknown electro-chemistry; neuroplasticity (connections thicken, thin, vanish, appear) while trained weights are fixed. "Weakly inspired, not a copy."
6. CampusX ~20:00–22:00: weights as feature importance: w₁ = 2 (IQ), w₂ = 4 (CGPA), b = 1 → CGPA counts twice as much.
7. CampusX ~22:00–32:00: geometric intuition: rename w₁, w₂, b → A, B, C and x₁, x₂ → x, y: z = 0 is Ax + By + C = 0, a line; z ≥ 0 and z < 0 are two regions → binary classifier. 3 inputs → a plane ("a sheet in your room"), 4+ → hyperplane. Limitation: only linear or "sort of linear" data; a ring-shaped placement set fails.
8. CampusX ~32:00–38:00: scikit-learn `Perceptron` on 100 students (CGPA, resume score, 0–10): coef ≈ 40, −36, intercept −25; `plot_decision_regions` (mlxtend) draws the line, "not that good" on unconfigured data.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 4: the Note says only "the bias lets the line sit anywhere, not only through the origin" (section 3.1). It never gives the threshold meaning (b = −10 means "fire only when the weighted sum passes 10"), which is the plain-words version a beginner can hold.
- The Note's prediction GIF uses standardized inputs (z = 5.82x₁ + 1.48x₂ + 1). CampusX's raw-number example (113.2) is easier to follow by hand and is missing as a first worked example.
- The neuron-vs-perceptron figure is a still; neuroplasticity (the third difference) has no picture.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: the bias as a threshold: a number line for the weighted sum, the step jumps at 0; drag b from 0 to −10 and the jump point slides to +10. Then the 2-D view: the same change shifts the line parallel to itself.
2. Plotly frames: the Note's placement data; w₁, w₂, b fixed at the trained values; one student at a time enters, z fills a bar, the point drops into the green or red region (the Note already has a version: keep it, add the raw-number student 100 / 5.1 first).
3. Manim: 2 inputs → line, 3 inputs → plane through a 3-D point cloud ("a sheet in a room"), rotating camera.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Parts of a perceptron:** CampusX is clearer for a beginner: it builds the diagram one block at a time and uses a placement example with numbers. 3b1b explains the same unit as one neuron of a digit network, which is better for "why" but heavier.
- **Bias:** 3b1b is clearer (a threshold the weighted sum must beat). Use it as the main explanation, keep CampusX's "line not through the origin" as the geometric second view.
- **CampusX's new angles:** the neuron-vs-perceptron comparison with neuroplasticity; weights as feature importance; the "sheet in your room" picture for a plane. None of the outside sources has these.
- No clash.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX reads weights as feature importance on raw IQ and CGPA (~22:00). On unscaled inputs that is misleading. **The Note is right** to add the standardization caveat (section 6 Extra), and its section 8 shows the raw weights even flip sign. Evidence: the Note's own notebook (section 8).
- Step at z = 0: CampusX uses "z ≥ 0 → 1" here (~06:00) and "z ≤ 0 → 0" at ~24:00 (overlapping). The Note's Extra box fixes the convention (z ≥ 0 here, z > 0 in Note 70). Fine.

### 1005 Perceptron trick

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Perceptron Trick | How to train a Perceptron | Perceptron Part 2", Lu2bruOHN6g, 51:45. `transcripts/D005.whisper-en.txt`.
2. Owner of the full derivation: ML Note 70 (perceptron trick). No StatQuest, 3b1b or Khan video on the perceptron trick.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~02:00 Data are linearly separable → we need A, B, C of Ax₁ + Bx₂ + C = 0.
2. ~04:00–10:00 The core loop as a dialogue: start with a random line; pick a random student; ask "are you on the right side?"; a correct point says "leave the line"; a wrong point says "come towards me". Stop after a fixed count (1 000) or at convergence (0 misclassified). Visual: the line jumping towards each complaining point.
3. ~10:00–14:00 Which side is positive: put the point in Ax + By + C (Desmos: 2x + 3y + 5 > 0 shaded).
4. ~14:00–18:00 What A, B, C do (Desmos): changing C shifts the line parallel (2x + 3y + 10 moves down, 2x + 3y + 0 moves up); changing A or B rotates it (4x + 3y + 5, x + 3y + 5; 2x + 6y + 5, 2x + y + 5).
5. ~18:00–24:00 The update by hand: line (2, 3, 5); negative point (4, 5) in the positive region → append 1 → subtract: (2, 3, 5) − (4, 5, 1) = (−2, −2, 4). Positive point (1, 3) in the negative region → add: (3, 6, 6). Desmos check with (5, 2) and (−3, 2).
6. ~24:00 Learning rate 0.01: subtract 0.01·(1, 3, 1), not the whole point.
7. ~26:00–34:00 Notation: x₀ = 1 column, Σ wᵢxᵢ = 0; two if-cases → one rule w ← w + η(yᵢ − ŷᵢ)xᵢ, checked on all four (y, ŷ) cases.
8. ~42:00–50:00 Code on `make_classification` (100 points, η = 0.1, 1 000 loops) and an animation of 200 loops where the line sits still whenever the picked point is already correct.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 4 (what A, B and C each do to the line) is missing. It is the reason "subtract the point" moves the line the right way. Needs a figure or animation.
- Beat 5's hand example (2, 3, 5) − (4, 5, 1) = (−2, −2, 4) is missing; the Note goes straight to the vector rule. One worked step with numbers is the bridge. ML Note 70 does not show it either (no "(4, 5, 1)" or Desmos-style coefficient demo there), so 1005 or 70 must add it once and the other link to it.
- The "line sits still when the picked point is correct" animation (CampusX ~48:00) is missing; the Note's `training_loop.png` is a flow chart.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: three sliders A, B, C on 2x + 3y + 5 = 0; moving C slides the line, moving A or B turns it.
2. Plotly frames: the trick on the Note's Iris pair (petal length/width, η = 0.1): one frame per pick; the picked point flashes; the line moves only when the point is wrong; a counter shows misclassified points (the data of `stopping_rules.png`).
3. Manim: one update step on the CampusX numbers: point (4, 5) on the wrong side; the coefficient vector (2, 3, 5) minus (4, 5, 1) → (−2, −2, 4); the line swings past the point.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source. CampusX's "point talks to the line" dialogue and the Desmos coefficient demo are the clearest beginner material available and should stay the main path.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX calls one pass of the loop (one random pick) an **epoch** (~10:00, "how many times this loop is running, this is called epoch"). An epoch is one full pass over the training set (Goodfellow et al. 2016, §8.1.3; Keras `fit(epochs=)` docs). **The Note is right** (section 5).
- CampusX says "if you increase C the line goes down" and then corrects itself on screen (~14:00–16:00: "I might have said it a little upside down"). For 2x + 3y + C = 0, y = −(2x + C)/3, so a larger C lowers the line. The final on-screen statement is right; the Note does not repeat the slip.

### 1006 Perceptron loss

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Perceptron Loss Function | Hinge Loss | Binary Cross Entropy | Sigmoid Function", 2_gCL5RAkHc, 59:13. `transcripts/D006.whisper-en.txt`.
2. Supporting (for section 8 only): StatQuest, "Neural Networks Part 6: Cross Entropy" (6ArSys5qHAU) and "Part 5: ArgMax and SoftMax" (KpKog-L9veg); see Note 1014. No outside video on the perceptron loss itself.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~06:00–10:00 Two flaws of the trick: it cannot say how good a line is (two runs, two lines, no way to compare); convergence is not guaranteed if the picks keep hitting correct points. "Jugaad … 99% of the time it works."
2. ~10:00–16:00 A loss gives every line one number: line 1 → 25, line 2 → 23, so line 2 is better. Find w₁, w₂, b with the smallest number.
3. ~16:00–18:00 Loss design 1: count mistakes (7 vs 5). Flaw: all mistakes cost the same.
4. ~18:00 Loss design 2: perpendicular distances (10 + 13).
5. ~20:00 Loss design 3: put the point in the line: line 2x + 3y + 4; point (4, 6) → 8 + 18 + 4 = 30; point (−2, −2) → −4 − 6 + 4 = −6 → |−6| = 6; total 36. Proportional to distance, needs only a dot product.
6. ~22:00–26:00 scikit-learn's SGD page: L = (1/n) Σ max(0, −yᵢ f(xᵢ)) + αR(w); drop the regularizer; argmin over w₁, w₂, b.
7. ~32:00–38:00 Four cases on four points with labels −1/+1: correct points give −(positive) → max = 0; wrong points give a positive number.
8. ~40:00–46:00 Gradient descent; chain rule ∂L/∂f · ∂f/∂w₁: 0 if yf ≥ 0, else −yᵢxᵢ₁; ∂L/∂b = −yᵢ.
9. ~46:00–49:00 Code on `make_classification`, η = 0.1, 1 000 epochs.
10. ~49:00–58:00 Flexibility table: step + perceptron loss → perceptron; sigmoid + BCE → logistic regression; softmax + categorical CE → softmax regression; linear + MSE → linear regression.

**What the Note lacks**
- The Note has every beat and the right figures (`distance_vs_value.png` uses CampusX's own line 2x + 3y + 4 = 0; `one_point_steps.gif`; `loss_training.gif`; `loss_landscape.png`).
- Beat 2's "every line gets a number, 25 vs 23" is told in words only. A two-line picture with the two loss numbers printed would help.
- Beat 7 (the four cases) has `point_loss.png` (loss vs s = yf). A 4-point picture with each point's contribution written next to it would make the max(0, ·) concrete.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: one line rotating through 36 angles over the Note's 100 points; beside it the three candidate losses (count, distance sum, value sum) drawn as the line turns. Shows that count is a staircase (flat, no gradient) and the other two are smooth.
2. Manim: the four-case board: four points, the line, and for each point the chain y → f(x) → −y·f(x) → max(0, ·) filling in.
3. Plotly frames: the Note's `loss_landscape.png` as a surface with the 12-update path drawn step by step.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source on this loss. For the section 8 flexibility table, StatQuest's cross-entropy video (Note 1014) gives a clearer *why* for BCE with numbers; CampusX gives the clearer *map* (one model, four activation–loss pairs). Keep both: CampusX table as main, link Note 1014 for the loss.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX calls the perceptron loss "hinge loss" and writes "step + hinge loss → perceptron" (~23:00, ~56:00). Hinge loss is max(0, 1 − yf), the perceptron loss is max(0, −yf); scikit-learn's SGD docs list them as two separate losses (`loss='hinge'` vs `loss='perceptron'`). **The Note is right** (section 6.2 Extra).
- CampusX writes the gradient step as "w − η·∂L/∂w", then says "the update rule has a plus here" (~46:00). Both are true once the sign of ∂L/∂w = −yx is put in: w − η(−yx) = w + ηyx. The Note derives this cleanly (section 7.2). No fix needed, but a rewrite should not copy the "plus" correction as a separate rule.

### 1007 Problem with perceptron

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Problem with Perceptron", Jp44b27VnOg, 7:39. `transcripts/D007.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No StatQuest, 3b1b or Khan video on XOR. StatQuest's "Essential Main Ideas" (CqOfi41LfDw, 02:00–03:00) has the same idea in another form: drug dosage low/medium/high = 0/1/0; "no matter how we rotate the straight line it can only accurately predict two of the three dosages". This is a 1-D XOR.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~02:00–04:00: AND, OR, XOR tables (4 rows each), plotted; scikit-learn `Perceptron` separates AND and OR, gives no boundary on XOR.
2. StatQuest 02:00–03:00: the dosage example: a straight line, rotated, always misses one of three groups. Why it clicks: it is one-dimensional, so the failure is visible with no geometry.
3. CampusX ~04:00–06:00: TensorFlow Playground: delete hidden layers to get one neuron; sigmoid, learning rate 0.01; linearly separable data → solved fast; XOR data → "no matter how many epochs", high error.

**What the Note lacks**
- The Note has the rotating-line GIF on XOR, the gates figure and a Playground-style figure. Missing: StatQuest's 1-D version (beat 2), which is the simplest possible picture of "a line cannot do this", and which sets up Note 1009 (a squiggle can).

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the 1-D dosage data (0, 1, 0); a straight line rotates through all slopes; the count of correct groups never reaches 3.
2. Plotly frames: the Note's sigmoid neuron trained on XOR-shaped data, frame per epoch: loss flat near 0.69, decision boundary wandering (the "however long it trains" claim shown).

**CampusX vs the others:** CampusX's code-plus-Playground route is clearer for "it fails in practice". StatQuest's dosage example is clearer for "why no line can work". Use CampusX as the main path and the dosage example as "another way to see it". No clash.

**Contradictions:** none found. CampusX says the perceptron "will never be able to converge" on XOR; that is the perceptron convergence theorem's converse (no separating line exists), and the Note's Extra box proves it with the four inequalities.

### 1008 MLP notation

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "MLP Notation", H0_3SJh4Rqs, 13:24. `transcripts/D008.whisper-en.txt`.
2. 3Blue1Brown, "But what is a neural network?" (aircAruvnKk), 11:30–15:00: counting 784 × 16 weights + 16 biases, "almost exactly 13,000" parameters; then the compact matrix form a⁽¹⁾ = σ(Wa⁽⁰⁾ + b) with **one row of W per receiving neuron**. File `1002-3b1b-neural-network-ch1.txt`.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–02:00: why notation first: backprop confuses beginners mostly because they cannot name the weights.
2. CampusX ~02:00: the 4-3-2-1 network; layers numbered 0 (input) to 3 (output); xᵢⱼ = column j of row i.
3. CampusX ~04:00–06:00: count parameters layer by layer with colour coding (all weights entering one node share a colour): 4×3 + 3 = 15, 3×2 + 2 = 8, 2×1 + 1 = 3 → 26.
4. 3b1b 11:30–12:30: the same count at scale: 784 × 16 + 16 for one layer; ~13 000 "knobs and dials" in the digit network. Why it clicks: it shows why we need a naming scheme at all.
5. CampusX ~06:00–08:00: biases bᵢⱼ (layer i, node j), outputs Oᵢⱼ (same indices); O₃₁ is the prediction.
6. CampusX ~08:00–12:00: weights Wᵢⱼᵏ: k = layer entered, i = node left, j = node entered; examples W¹₁₁, W¹₄₂, W¹₁₃, W²₂₂, W³₁₁.
7. 3b1b 13:30–15:00: the matrix view: activations as a column vector, weights as a matrix with one row per next-layer neuron, bias vector added, σ applied element-wise. This is the bridge to Note 1010.

**What the Note lacks**
- The Note covers beats 1–3, 5, 6 with good figures (`param_blocks.png`, `weight_index.png`). Beat 4 (the scale argument) appears only as an Extra with 101 770 parameters; fine.
- No animation that "walks" one weight: highlight a connection and spell out its three indices as the arrow lights up.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: the 4-3-2-1 network; a cursor moves over each connection; the label Wᵢⱼᵏ assembles: k pops in when the target layer lights, i when the source node lights, j when the target node lights.
2. Manim: parameter counter: each layer's weights fade in as a coloured block (the Note's `param_blocks.png` data), the running total ticks 15 → 23 → 26.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Naming scheme:** CampusX is clearer for hand calculation (three indices, read aloud). 3b1b skips indices and goes straight to the matrix form, which is clearer for "why a layer is one matrix product".
- **Clash in index order:** CampusX writes Wᵢⱼᵏ as *from i to j* (source first), so its weight matrix has one row per **input** node and needs a transpose in Note 1010 (Wᵀa + b). 3b1b (and Goodfellow et al. 2016, Nielsen 2015) put one row per **receiving** node, so no transpose (Wa + b). Both are correct conventions. The Note follows CampusX and says in an Extra box that books differ. **Keep it, but add the 3b1b form as "another way to see it" in Note 1010**, since every later library and paper uses Wa + b (PyTorch `nn.Linear` stores weight as out × in).

**Contradictions:** none (only the convention above).

### 1009 MLP intuition

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Multi Layer Perceptron | MLP Intuition", qw7wFGgNCSU, 37:46. `transcripts/D009.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "The Essential Main Ideas of Neural Networks", CqOfi41LfDw, 18:54. `1002-statquest-nn-main-ideas.txt`.
3. 3Blue1Brown, "But what is a neural network?" (aircAruvnKk), 05:30–10:00: what hidden neurons might detect.

**Teaching path**
1. StatQuest 02:00–03:00: the problem in 1-D: drug dosage low/medium/high → 0/1/0; a straight line fits only two of three. A network "can fit a squiggle".
2. CampusX ~02:00–08:00: one perceptron with a sigmoid gives a **probability map**: weights 5, 10, bias 3; student CGPA 8.7, IQ 87 → z → σ(z) ≈ 0.8 placed, 0.2 not; 0.5 on the line, 0.6 → 0.9 moving away on one side, 0.4 → 0.1 on the other. Visual: parallel probability contour lines.
3. CampusX ~08:00–12:00: two perceptrons, lines 2x + 3y + 6 = 0 and 5x + 4y + 3 = 0; **superimpose** the two maps and **smooth** → a curved decision boundary.
4. CampusX ~12:00–14:00: the arithmetic: one student gets 0.7 from P1 and 0.8 from P2; 0.7 + 0.8 = 1.5 is not a probability; σ(1.5) = 0.82.
5. CampusX ~14:00–18:00: weighted version: 10·0.7 + 5·0.8 + 3 → σ. "This whole thing is itself a perceptron": P1, P2 feed P3 → input, hidden, output layers.
6. StatQuest 07:30–15:30: the same mechanism in 1-D with exact numbers: dosage × (−34.4) + 2.14 → softplus → × (−1.3) gives the blue curve; dosage × (−2.52) + 1.29 → softplus → × 2.28 gives the orange curve; add, then −0.58 → the green squiggle; dosage 0.5 → 1.03 → "effective". Visual: the two bent curves are sliced, flipped, stretched, then added. Why it clicks: every step is a picture of a curve on one graph, with no 3-D.
7. StatQuest 16:30–17:30: "big fancy squiggle-fitting machines"; weights multiply, biases add.
8. CampusX ~22:00–30:00: four architecture changes: more hidden nodes (3 lines combined; z = 0.2·1 + 0.3·2 + 0.4·3 + 10), more inputs (planes), more outputs (dog/cat/human), more hidden layers (universal approximator).
9. 3b1b 05:30–07:30: why deeper layers help: edges → loops/lines → digits.
10. CampusX ~30:00–36:00: TensorFlow Playground: XOR with 2 hidden sigmoid nodes, then 4; circles; spiral fails with 4×4 sigmoid layers, works after switching to ReLU; first-layer boundaries are lines, later ones curves.

**What the Note lacks**
- StatQuest's 1-D squiggle (beats 1, 6, 7) is missing. It is the simplest picture of "add bent shapes to get any shape" and needs no probability maps. The Note jumps straight to 2-D probability maps.
- The Note's Figure (`prob_maps.png`) shows the three maps as stills; the **superimpose → smooth** step is not animated.
- Beat 9 (what deeper layers detect) is not in the Note; one line plus a link to Note 1052 (visualizing CNN filters) would do.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: StatQuest's squiggle rebuilt with his exact numbers: x ∈ [0, 1]; frame 1 softplus(−34.4x + 2.14); frame 2 scaled by −1.3; frame 3 softplus(−2.52x + 1.29); frame 4 scaled by 2.28; frame 5 sum; frame 6 −0.58; the three dosage groups overlaid.
2. Plotly frames: CampusX's two perceptrons (2x + 3y + 6, 5x + 4y + 3): frame 1 P1 map, frame 2 P2 map, frame 3 the plain sum (values up to 2), frame 4 σ(10·P1 + 5·P2 + 3), the black 0.5 contour bending. Use the Note's own `prob_maps.png` grid.
3. Plotly frames: XOR with two hidden nodes (the Note's 200 points): training epochs as frames; both hidden lines and the output strip move into place.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **How a hidden layer bends the boundary:** StatQuest is clearer for a first look (1-D, exact numbers, one graph). CampusX is clearer for classification (probability maps, the "combiner is a perceptron" step). Use StatQuest's squiggle as the opening picture, then CampusX's 2-D version as the main mechanism.
- **CampusX's new angle:** "superimpose, then smooth", and the observation that the combining step is itself a perceptron. StatQuest never says this.
- **StatQuest's new angle:** the activation's *portion* used: only the part of softplus inside the "red box" matters (11:00–11:30). This explains why weights stretch and shift the curve.
- No clash.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX: "given enough hidden **layers** … you can make any mathematical function" (~30:00). The universal approximation theorem needs only **one** hidden layer with enough units (Cybenko 1989; Hornik 1991). Depth helps efficiency, not possibility. **The Note is right**: section 4.4 says "with enough hidden nodes" (line 155) and links Note 1003.
- CampusX in the Playground switches to ReLU and the spiral trains (~34:00). The Note's Extra says ReLU is the modern default (Goodfellow §6.3): consistent.

### 1010 Forward propagation

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Forward Propagation | How a neural network predicts output?", 7MuiScUkboE, 15:31. `transcripts/D010.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "Neural Networks Pt. 4: Multiple Inputs and Outputs", 83LYR-1IcjA, 13:49. `transcripts/teaching-video/1010-statquest-nn-multiple-inputs-outputs.txt`.
3. 3Blue1Brown, "But what is a neural network?" (aircAruvnKk), 13:30–15:30: the layer as σ(Wa + b), "the entire network is just a function: 784 numbers in, 10 out".

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~02:00–04:00: forward vs backward propagation; "linear algebra handles everything".
2. CampusX ~04:00: the 4-3-2-1 network; 26 parameters; student (7.2, 72, 69, 81) enters the four input nodes.
3. StatQuest 03:30–07:30: one forward pass by hand with two inputs (Iris petal and sepal width, scaled 0–1): top hidden node: petal × (−2.5) + sepal × 0.6 + 1.6 = 1.6 at (0, 0) → ReLU 1.6; at petal 0.2 → 1.1; sweeping all inputs gives a **bent surface**; × (−0.1); bottom node gives an orange bent surface × 1.5; add (−0.16 + 1.05 = 0.89) → a **crinkled surface** for setosa. Visual: 3-D surfaces built point by point. Why it clicks: the forward pass is shown as a shape over the whole input space, not one number.
4. StatQuest 09:00–12:30: three outputs = three crinkled surfaces from the same two hidden nodes with different output weights (2.4, −5.2; −2.2, 3.7, bias 1); the iris (0.5, 0.37) → 0.09 setosa, 0.86 versicolor → versicolor. Points to argmax/softmax (Note 1014).
5. CampusX ~06:00–08:00: layer 1 as one matrix product: W¹ (4 × 3) transposed to 3 × 4, times a⁰ (4 × 1), plus b¹ (3 × 1) → 3 × 1 → σ → O₁₁, O₁₂, O₁₃.
6. CampusX ~08:00–10:00: layer 2 (3 × 2 → 2 × 3)·(3 × 1) + (2 × 1); layer 3 (1 × 2)·(2 × 1) + (1 × 1) → ŷ.
7. CampusX ~10:00–14:00: aˡ = σ(Wˡᵀaˡ⁻¹ + bˡ) and the nested formula ŷ = σ(W³ᵀσ(W²ᵀσ(W¹ᵀa⁰ + b¹) + b²) + b³).
8. 3b1b 15:00–15:30: "the whole network is just a function".

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 3/4 (the forward pass as a surface over all inputs, one surface per output) is missing. The Note shows the pass for one student only. A beginner then cannot connect "forward pass" to "decision boundary" (Note 1009).
- The Note has a good `forward_anim.gif`, `one_node.png`, `layer_product.png` and a scaling figure.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: StatQuest-style surfaces for the Note's own 4-3-2-1 network cut to 2 inputs (CGPA, IQ; the other two fixed at their means): frame per hidden node (bent surface), then the weighted sum, then σ: the output surface with the student's point marked.
2. Manim: matrix shapes sliding together: (3 × 4)(4 × 1) + (3 × 1) → (3 × 1), the inner 4s cancelling with a flash; repeat for layers 2 and 3.
3. Manim: the nested formula growing outward one σ(·) at a time as the pulse moves through each layer.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Matrix form:** CampusX is clearer for "how to compute it" (shapes, transposes, one layer at a time). StatQuest is clearer for "what the result looks like" (surfaces). 3b1b gives the one-line summary. Main path: CampusX; add the StatQuest surface as "another way to see it".
- **Convention clash:** CampusX's Wᵀa + b vs 3b1b's Wa + b (see Note 1008). Both correct; the Note uses Wᵀa + b with a 4 × 3 matrix. Add one sentence that libraries store the transposed shape.
- StatQuest uses ReLU in the hidden layer; CampusX and the Note use sigmoid everywhere. No clash; the Note's scaling Extra (sigmoid saturates on raw inputs) is a CampusX-independent point grounded in the Note's own numbers (20.1, −13.7, 12.5 → 1.000, 0.000, 1.000).

**Contradictions:** none.

### 1011 Customer churn ANN

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Customer Churn Prediction using ANN | Keras and Tensorflow", 9wmImImmgcI, 35:23. `transcripts/D011.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video: StatQuest's coding videos use PyTorch on toy data ("Introduction to Coding Neural Networks with PyTorch and Lightning", khMzi6xPbuM, seen in the playlist listing, not opened), not Keras on tabular data. The concepts used here (sigmoid output, cross-entropy) are mapped in Notes 1010 and 1014.

**Teaching path** (CampusX, code-first)
1. ~00:00 Why code before backprop: "see it once, then the theory connects".
2. ~03:00–09:00 Data: 10 000 × 14; drop RowNumber, CustomerId, Surname; no missing values or duplicates; Exited ≈ 8 000 stay / 2 000 leave ("a bit imbalanced"); Geography (France, Germany, Spain), Gender; `get_dummies(drop_first=True)`.
3. ~09:00–12:00 Why scale: "6-digit numbers next to single digits … the weights do not converge quickly"; split 80/20, `StandardScaler`.
4. ~12:00–17:00 `Sequential`, `Dense(3, sigmoid, input_dim=11)`, `Dense(1, sigmoid)`; `summary()` → 11×3 + 3 = 36, 3 + 1 = 4, total 40. Visual: the network drawn with every input reaching every node.
5. ~17:00–19:00 `compile(loss='binary_crossentropy', optimizer='adam')`; `fit(epochs=10)` → loss down to ~0.42; `layers[0].get_weights()` shows 33 weights + 3 biases.
6. ~19:00–22:00 `predict` gives probabilities → threshold 0.5 (`np.where`) → accuracy 81.45%.
7. ~22:00–29:00 Improve: ReLU hidden, 11 + 11 nodes, 100 epochs, `validation_split=0.2`, accuracy metric → train 87%, val 84.8% → test 85.75%.
8. ~29:00–33:00 `history.history` → plot loss vs val_loss and accuracy vs val_accuracy; the gap = overfitting.

**What the Note lacks**
- The Note covers every beat and adds the key point CampusX misses: 79.25% of test customers stayed, so the first network's accuracy is barely above "always predict stay" (section 6).
- Missing visual: a picture of what `fit` does per epoch (8 000 customers → loss → weight update → next epoch). The Note's `keras_workflow.png` is a flow chart of the code steps, not of the training loop.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the Note's own training history: one frame per epoch, loss and val_loss drawing themselves; the gap shaded once val_loss stops falling.
2. Plotly frames: the threshold slider: test probabilities as a strip of 2 000 dots coloured by true label; the 0.5 line moves from 0.1 to 0.9 and accuracy, precision and recall update (data from the Note's notebook).

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source. CampusX's angle (code first, theory after) is the reason Notes 1011–1013 sit before backprop; keep it.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX's first network scores 81.45% (~21:00); the Note's scores 79.25%. Different random seeds and Keras versions; the Note's number comes from its executed notebook (NOTE-RULES §4). Not a contradiction in principle.
- CampusX says the threshold should come from an "ROC-AUC curve" (~21:00). Correct in spirit (ML Note 78 owns it).

### 1012 MNIST ANN

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Handwritten Digit Classification using ANN | MNIST Dataset", 3xPT2Pk0Jds, 28:40. `transcripts/D012.whisper-en.txt`.
2. 3Blue1Brown, "But what is a neural network? | Deep learning chapter 1", aircAruvnKk, 18:40 (the same task: 784 inputs, 10 outputs). `1002-3b1b-neural-network-ch1.txt`.
3. 3Blue1Brown, "Gradient descent, how neural networks learn | Deep Learning Chapter 2", IHZwWFHWa-w, 20:33. `transcripts/teaching-video/1017-3b1b-gradient-descent-ch2.txt` (13:00–16:00: results and what the hidden layer really learned).

**Teaching path**
1. 3b1b ch1 00:00–01:00: three sloppy "3"s at 28 × 28 that a brain reads at once; "write a program" for that is hard. Motivates the dataset.
2. CampusX ~00:00–03:00: 70 000 images, 28 × 28 = 784 pixel values; architecture 784 → 128 → 10; one output node per class, the highest probability wins.
3. CampusX ~06:00–09:00: `keras.datasets.mnist.load_data()`; X_train shape (60 000, 28, 28), X_test (10 000, 28, 28); y_train 5, 0, 4…; `imshow` shows the 5.
4. CampusX ~09:00–12:00: divide by 255 so pixels lie in 0–1.
5. 3b1b ch1 02:30–03:30: each input neuron *holds* one pixel's greyscale value (0 black, 1 white), the "activation". The picture of 784 lit-up neurons makes Flatten concrete.
6. CampusX ~12:00–15:00: `Flatten(input_shape=(28, 28))` lays the 28 rows side by side; `Dense(128, relu)`; `Dense(10, softmax)`; params 784 × 128 + 128 = 100 480, 128 × 10 + 10 = 1 290.
7. CampusX ~15:00–18:00: sparse categorical vs categorical cross-entropy: same loss, sparse keeps integer labels.
8. CampusX ~18:00–21:00: `fit(epochs=10, validation_split=0.2)`; `predict` → 10 probabilities per image → `argmax(axis=1)` → 7, 2, 1…; accuracy ~97% ("random forest/SVM reached 95–96% with tuning").
9. CampusX ~22:00–27:00: add `Dense(32)`, 25 epochs → val loss rises → overfitting; training accuracy 100%, validation below 98%.
10. 3b1b ch2 13:00–15:30: the 784-16-16-10 net gets ~96% (98% with tweaks); **its hidden weights do not look like edges** ("almost random"), and it **confidently labels random noise as a 5**. Why it matters: a beginner learns that high accuracy does not mean the network learned the "edges → loops" story; it sets up why CNNs (Note 1040) exist.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 10 is missing: no figure of the trained hidden-layer weights as 28 × 28 images, and no "feed it noise" test. Both run on the Note's own model in a few lines.
- Beat 5: the Note's Flatten section has text and an architecture figure; an animation of the 28 rows sliding into one row of 784 would make Flatten click.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: Flatten: a 28 × 28 digit; rows peel off one by one and line up into a 784-long strip that then feeds the input nodes.
2. Plotly (still, small multiples): the first 16 of the Note's 128 hidden units, each weight vector reshaped to 28 × 28, green positive / red negative (3b1b's colour scheme). Then the same for random noise input: the 10 output bars, showing a confident wrong answer.
3. Plotly frames: one test digit; the 10 softmax bars filling in; argmax highlighted (the Note's `argmax.png` animated).

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Task and code:** CampusX is the main path (Keras steps, shapes, losses, overfitting curve).
- **What the network learned:** only 3b1b covers this. It adds the honest caveat that the network does not learn edges and is overconfident on noise. Keep it as "another way to see it" / Extra.
- No clash on numbers: 3b1b's 96–98% (two 16-unit layers, sigmoid) and CampusX's ~97% (128 ReLU) are different networks.

**Contradictions:** none.

### 1013 Graduate admission ANN

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Graduate Admission Prediction using ANN", RCmiPBiA4qg, 17:43. `transcripts/D013.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video (regression with a network in Keras). StatQuest's "Essential Main Ideas" (CqOfi41LfDw) is itself a regression network (output = a number, 1.03 for dosage 0.5, 15:30–16:30) and is the conceptual partner: a linear output can give any number.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–03:00 Data: GRE (max 340), TOEFL (max 120), university rating, SOP, LOR, CGPA, research → chance of admit (0–1); 500 rows (the updated file, not the 400-row one).
2. ~03:00–06:00 Drop serial number; no missing values or duplicates.
3. ~06:00 **Min-max scaling, not standard**, because every column has known upper and lower limits ("GRE can only be 340").
4. ~06:00–09:00 Architecture 7 → 7 → 1: 7×7 + 7 = 56, 7 + 1 = 8, total 64.
5. ~12:00 Output activation **linear** for regression; loss `mean_squared_error` (the "mean squared loss" name raises an error).
6. ~12:00–15:00 10 epochs → R² "very bad"; add a hidden layer of 7 and 100 epochs → R² 0.76.
7. ~15:00 Loss and val_loss curves: no overfitting, still decreasing.

**What the Note lacks**
- The Note covers all beats and has a predicted-vs-actual plot. Missing: one picture of *why* the output must be linear: a sigmoid or ReLU output squeezes or clips the prediction range. StatQuest's squiggle shows that the final step is "add, then shift" (no squash) for regression.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: predicted-vs-actual scatter for the 100 test students, one frame per 10 epochs of the second network: points migrate onto the diagonal; R² in the title.
2. Plotly frames: the output node's pre-activation values for the test set passed through linear, sigmoid and ReLU in turn, beside the true range 0.34–0.97.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source. CampusX's practical rule (min-max when the bounds are known) is a good angle; the Note keeps it.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX reaches R² 0.76; the Note reports −0.06 → 0.80. Seeds and versions differ; the Note's numbers come from its notebook. No principle at stake.

### 1014 Loss functions in deep learning

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Loss Functions in Deep Learning", gb5nm_3jBIo, 59:56. `transcripts/D014.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "Neural Networks Part 5: ArgMax and SoftMax", KpKog-L9veg, 14:03. `transcripts/teaching-video/1014-statquest-argmax-softmax.txt`.
3. StatQuest, "Neural Networks Part 6: Cross Entropy", 6ArSys5qHAU, 9:31. `transcripts/teaching-video/1014-statquest-cross-entropy.txt`.
4. 3Blue1Brown, "But what is cross-entropy?" (GlYgs6v2YfU, 33:51) is mapped in `docs/3b1b-transformers.md` (section CE); it is a 34-min information-theory route, too long for this Note's beginner path; cite it as Extra only.
5. 3Blue1Brown ch2 (IHZwWFHWa-w) 03:30–05:00: the cost of one image = sum of squared differences between the 10 outputs and the wanted one-hot; average over all images = cost. Same loss-vs-cost split as CampusX.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–03:00: definition ("how well your algorithm models the data") and the loss as a function of the parameters (m, b for a line).
2. CampusX ~03:00–06:00: "You can't improve what you can't measure" (Drucker); the loss is "the eye of the ML algorithm": random line → loss → move → loss again.
3. CampusX ~06:00–11:00: the loss inside a network: student (CGPA 7.1, IQ 83) forward → prediction 3.7 → MSE → gradient descent updates → next student → epochs.
4. CampusX ~11:00–14:00: map of losses: regression (MSE, MAE, Huber), classification (BCE, CCE, hinge), autoencoder (KL), GAN, object detection (focal), embeddings (triplet).
5. CampusX ~14:00–17:00 (and 3b1b ch2 03:30–05:00): loss = one observation, cost = average. Four students: (6.3 vs 6.1), (4.1 vs 4), (3.5 vs 3.7), (7.2 vs 7) → J = 0.0325.
6. CampusX ~17:00–30:00: MSE: why square (errors +0.2, +0.1, −0.2, +0.2 would cancel); 1 → 1, 2 → 4, 4 → 16; far points rotate the line more; outlier student (CGPA 8.1, IQ 100, 50 LPA vs predicted 8) → error 42² drags the line. Output node must be linear.
7. CampusX ~30:00–36:00: MAE (units of y, robust, not differentiable at 0); Huber for "25% outliers" between MSE and MAE.
8. StatQuest Part 5, 01:00–04:00: why softmax at all: raw outputs 1.43, −0.4, 0.23 are hard to read; argmax gives 1, 0, 0 but its slope is 0 everywhere, so backprop gets nothing. Visual: the argmax step plotted as two flat lines.
9. StatQuest Part 5, 04:30–08:00: softmax on 1.43, −0.4, 0.23 → 0.69, 0.10, 0.21: keeps the order, all in 0–1, sum to 1. 08:00–09:00: do not trust these "probabilities" too much; they depend on the random initial weights.
10. CampusX ~36:00–45:00: BCE: sigmoid output; student 1 (y = 1, ŷ = 0.73), student 2 (y = 0, ŷ = 0.25).
11. StatQuest Part 6, 02:00–05:30: cross-entropy for three iris rows: only the true class's predicted probability counts: −ln 0.57 = 0.56, −ln 0.58 = 0.54, −ln 0.52 = 0.65 → total 1.75.
12. StatQuest Part 6, 05:30–08:30: **why not squared error on probabilities**: plot both losses for p from 0 to 1; cross-entropy "explodes" near 0, so its slope for a bad prediction is much larger → a bigger backprop step. Visual: the two loss curves on one graph with tangent lines.
13. CampusX ~45:00–57:00: CCE with one-hot (1, 0, 0) and ŷ = (0.2, 0.3, 0.5) → −log 0.2; second student (0, 1, 0), (0.3, 0.6, 0.1) → −log 0.6; sparse CCE: same loss, integer labels, faster with many classes. Summary table: which loss when.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beats 8 and 12 are missing: the Note never says **why** we need softmax instead of argmax (zero slope), nor **why** cross-entropy beats squared error for probabilities (steeper slope for bad predictions). Both are short, both have a picture, and both are the mechanism (NOTE-RULES §10.1).
- Beat 9's caveat (softmax "probabilities" depend on initial weights) is missing.
- The Note's figures are good (`loss_shapes.png`, `outlier_drag.gif`, `outlier_fit.png`, `bce_curves.png`, `cce_bars.png`, `sparse_vs_onehot.png`). Add the "MSE vs CE on one probability" plot with tangent lines.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: p (predicted probability of the true class) slides from 0.99 down to 0.01; two curves, −ln p and (1 − p)², with a tangent line on each; the slope values printed. Data: the Note's own students (0.73, 0.25) as the starting points.
2. Plotly frames: argmax vs softmax: three raw outputs (1.43, −0.4, 0.23); one raw value slides; argmax bars jump 0↔1, softmax bars move smoothly.
3. Plotly frames: the Note's outlier GIF already exists; add a third panel with the Huber fit and a δ slider (δ = 0.5, 1, 2, 4).

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Loss vs cost, MSE, MAE, Huber:** CampusX is clearer and more complete (worked numbers, outlier student, units). StatQuest and 3b1b do not cover MAE or Huber.
- **Softmax and cross-entropy:** StatQuest is clearer on *why* (zero-slope argmax; exploding CE slope), with exact numbers. CampusX gives *how* (formula, worked students, sparse vs categorical). Use StatQuest's why as the main explanation of each, keep CampusX's worked students.
- **CampusX's new angles:** the whole-zoo map (KL, focal, triplet), the sparse-vs-categorical speed argument, "the loss is the eye of the algorithm".
- **Clash (log base):** CampusX computes −log 0.73 = 0.13 and −log 0.75 = 0.12 (~42:00), which are base-10 logs. Keras and StatQuest (02:30: "negative log base e") use natural logs: 0.315 and 0.288. **The Note is right** and already flags it in an Extra box.
- **Clash (minima):** CampusX says MSE has "only one local minimum" and BCE "can have multiple local minima" (~24:00, ~42:00). For a linear or logistic model both MSE and BCE are convex in the weights (ML Notes 590, 73); inside a neural network both are non-convex, because of the hidden layers, not the loss (Goodfellow et al. 2016, §8.2). **The Note is right** for MSE (section 5.3 says "one minimum" for a linear model only, with an Extra box for networks). It does not repeat the BCE "multiple minima" claim.

**Contradictions**
- See the two clashes above. Evidence for natural logs: Keras `binary_crossentropy` uses `log` (natural); the Note's notebook numbers match 0.315 and 0.288.

### 1015 Backpropagation: the what

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Backpropagation in Deep Learning | Part 1 | The What?", 6M1wWQmcUjQ, 54:19. `transcripts/D015.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "Neural Networks Pt. 2: Backpropagation Main Ideas", IN2XmBhILt4, 17:33. `transcripts/teaching-video/1015-statquest-backprop-main-ideas.txt`.
3. 3Blue1Brown, "Backpropagation, intuitively | Deep Learning Chapter 3", Ilg3gGewQ5U, 12:47. `transcripts/teaching-video/1015-3b1b-backprop-intuitively.txt`.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~03:00–06:00: plain definition: "an algorithm to train a neural network", i.e. find the weights and biases. Data: students (CGPA, IQ → LPA); 2-2-1 network, linear activations, 6 weights + 3 biases = 9 parameters.
2. CampusX ~09:00–15:00: step 0 initialise (weights 1, biases 0); step 1 pick one student, forward pass → ŷ = 18 vs y = 3; step 3 loss (3 − 18)² = 225.
3. CampusX ~15:00–21:00: **why "backward"**: we cannot change y, only ŷ; ŷ = O₂₁ depends on 5 things (W²₁₁, W²₂₁, b₂₁, O₁₁, O₁₂); O₁₁ itself depends on 5 more (two weights, a bias, two inputs). So the fix flows from the output back. Visual: the dependency tree. This is CampusX's strongest beat.
4. StatQuest 03:00–08:30: one parameter only (b₃) with everything else already trained: b₃ = 0 → SSR 20.4; b₃ = 1 → 7.8; 2 → 1.11; 3 → 0.46. Visual: the green squiggle sliding up as b₃ grows, and the pink SSR-vs-b₃ curve drawn point by point. Why it clicks: it shows "the loss is a function of one parameter" before any formula.
5. StatQuest 08:30–13:00: chain rule for that one parameter: dSSR/db₃ = dSSR/dPredicted × dPredicted/db₃ = Σ −2(observed − predicted) × 1.
6. StatQuest 13:30–16:00: gradient descent on b₃: slope −15.7 → step −1.57 (η = 0.1) → b₃ = 1.57; slope −6.26 → b₃ = 2.19; … stop at 2.61.
7. 3b1b 03:30–08:00: **nudges**: for one image of a "2", the output neurons should move (the "2" up, all others down, each in proportion to how far it is from its target). Three ways to raise the "2" neuron: raise its bias, raise weights from **bright** neurons (they matter most: "neurons that fire together wire together"), or change the previous layer's activations in proportion to the weights. Each output neuron's wishes for the previous layer are **added**; repeat backwards. Why it clicks: it explains what the gradient *means* without symbols.
8. 3b1b 08:00–09:00: each training example has its own wish list; average them = (proportional to) the negative gradient.
9. CampusX ~21:00–48:00: update rule w_new = w_old − η ∂L/∂w; the nine derivatives by the chain rule, e.g. ∂L/∂W²₁₁ = −2(y − ŷ)·O₁₁; ∂L/∂W¹₁₁ = −2(y − ŷ)·W²₁₁·x_{i1}; the shared factor −2(y − ŷ).
10. CampusX ~48:00–54:00: the full loop (students inside, epochs outside, until convergence).

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 7 (3b1b's nudges/wishes: three avenues, bright neurons matter most, wishes add up backwards) is missing. The Note gives the dependency tree and the nine derivatives, but no plain-words story of *why each derivative looks the way it does* (e.g. why ∂L/∂W²₁₁ has O₁₁ in it: "the weight from a brighter neuron matters more").
- Beat 4 (one parameter, loss curve drawn point by point) belongs to Note 1017, which has `lr_paths.png` and `slope_sign.png` on b₂₁; 1015 could preview it with one sentence.
- Beat 8 (one example's wishes vs the average over all examples) is missing; the Note updates per student (stochastic) without saying that the "true" gradient averages all students. Link Note 1020.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: 3b1b-style nudges on the Note's own 2-2-1 network: ŷ = 0.32 must rise to 4; an up-arrow on ŷ; arrows appear on W²₁₁, W²₂₁ sized by O₁₁, O₁₂; on b₂₁; then "wish arrows" on O₁₁, O₁₂ sized by the weights; then the same one layer back.
2. Plotly frames: StatQuest-style one-parameter search with the Note's student 1: b₂₁ from −5 to 10; left panel the prediction moving, right panel the loss point tracing the parabola (3.68 − b₂₁)².
3. Manim: the dependency tree (the Note's `dependency.png`) growing from L backwards, each edge labelled with its local derivative; then the products along each path light up to form the nine formulas.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why it goes backwards:** CampusX's dependency tree (you cannot change y; ŷ depends on 5 things; O₁₁ depends on 5 more) is clearer than StatQuest's "conceptually starts with the last parameter". Keep CampusX as main.
- **What the gradient means:** 3b1b's nudges are clearer and are missing from CampusX. Add as "another way to see it".
- **Chain rule on one parameter:** StatQuest is clearer (one parameter, curve, three gradient steps with numbers). CampusX goes straight to nine derivatives.
- **CampusX's new angle:** the what/how/why split itself, and the observation that every formula uses numbers already computed in the forward pass.
- **Clash in loss scope:** StatQuest's loss is the sum over all 3 observations (one step per pass); CampusX and the Note update after each student (stochastic). Both valid; Note 1020 owns the difference. No conflict to fix if the Note says it uses one observation per update.

**Contradictions**
- None between the Note and the sources. CampusX starts weights at 1 (~09:00); the Note starts at 0.1. The Note's choice gives the reported numbers (ŷ = 0.32, loss 13.54); not a contradiction.

### 1016 Backpropagation: the how

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Backpropagation Part 2 | The How", ma6hWrU-LaI, 59:56. `transcripts/D016.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "Backpropagation Details Pt. 1: Optimizing 3 parameters simultaneously.", iyn2zdALii8, 18:31. `transcripts/teaching-video/1016-statquest-backprop-details-pt1.txt`.
3. StatQuest, "Backpropagation Details Pt. 2: Going bonkers with The Chain Rule", GKZoOHXGcLo, 13:08. `transcripts/teaching-video/1016-statquest-backprop-details-pt2.txt`.
4. 3Blue1Brown, "Backpropagation calculus | Deep Learning Chapter 4", tIeHLnjs5U8, 10:17. `transcripts/teaching-video/1016-3b1b-backprop-calculus.txt`.

**Teaching path**
1. 3b1b 00:30–04:30: the smallest possible chain: one neuron per layer; C₀ = (a⁽ᴸ⁾ − y)²; z⁽ᴸ⁾ = w⁽ᴸ⁾a⁽ᴸ⁻¹⁾ + b⁽ᴸ⁾; a⁽ᴸ⁾ = σ(z⁽ᴸ⁾). **Each quantity on its own number line**; a nudge of 0.01 to w ripples to z, to a, to C. ∂C/∂w = ∂z/∂w · ∂a/∂z · ∂C/∂a = a⁽ᴸ⁻¹⁾ · σ′(z) · 2(a − y). Why it clicks: the chain rule becomes three linked number lines, not symbol pushing.
2. 3b1b 04:30–05:00: "∂z/∂w = a⁽ᴸ⁻¹⁾: the weight's effect depends on how strong the previous neuron is" (fire together, wire together).
3. 3b1b 05:30–06:30: the bias is the same chain with ∂z/∂b = 1; and ∂z/∂a⁽ᴸ⁻¹⁾ = w⁽ᴸ⁾ is how the error moves back a layer.
4. 3b1b 06:30–09:00: more neurons only add indices; a hidden neuron feeds several outputs, so **its derivative is a sum over paths**.
5. StatQuest Pt 1, 01:30–17:00: three parameters at once (w₃, w₄, b₃): the derivative already found for b₃ does not change; ∂SSR/∂w₃ = Σ −2(obs − pred)·y₁,ᵢ (the hidden output); random start w₃ = 0.36, w₄ = 0.63; 175 steps of the animated fit.
6. StatQuest Pt 2, 01:00–12:00: all seven parameters: SSR → predicted → y₁,ᵢ → x₁,ᵢ → w₁; softplus derivative eˣ/(1 + eˣ); "big fancy equations"; 450 steps.
7. CampusX ~03:00–06:00: the algorithm as two loops: epochs outside, 4 students inside; average loss per epoch.
8. CampusX ~06:00–32:00: code: `initialize_parameters([2, 2, 1])` (weights 0.1, biases 0), `linear_forward`, forward pass for student (8, 8) → A1 = 1.6, ŷ = 0.32 vs y = 4; `update_parameters` = the nine formulas (minus × minus → plus); η = 0.001; one student at a time, then the epoch loop: average loss 25 → 18 → 9.47 → … → 1.3; Keras with the same starting weights (`set_weights`) and η.
9. CampusX ~32:00–51:00: classification: sigmoid everywhere, BCE; derivation ∂L/∂ŷ · ∂ŷ/∂z = −(y − ŷ) (the sigmoid and the log cancel); hidden weights gain O(1 − O).
10. CampusX ~51:00–59:00: code; loss stuck at 0.69 for 550 epochs; Keras also stuck at 0.69 → "our code is right".

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 1's picture (number lines for w, z, a, C, with a nudge propagating) is missing. The Note has a chain diagram (`chain.png`) with numbers, but not the "nudge ripples through" view.
- Beat 4 (a hidden node's derivative sums over every path it feeds) does not arise in a 2-2-1 net with one output, so the Note never says it. One sentence plus a 2-2-2 example would prepare Notes 1018 and 1059.
- The Note explains the stuck classifier better than CampusX (symmetry, tiny η; section 8). Keep.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: four horizontal number lines (w, z, a, C) for student 1 of the classification net; w nudged by +0.01; a dot slides on each line in turn; the three ratios appear and multiply to the derivative (use the Note's −0.4585 and O(1 − O) numbers).
2. Plotly frames: the Note's `loss_grid.gif` already shows the loop; add a second panel with the three distinct weight values per update (the data of `weights_path.png`) animated in sync.
3. Manim: symmetry: the two hidden nodes drawn side by side, both starting at 0.1; each update paints both with the same colour change; the twins never separate. Then one weight set to 0.11: they separate.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Deriving the formulas:** CampusX is the most complete for this exact network (all nine, both tasks, with code and a Keras check). StatQuest gives the same chain idea more slowly with pictures of curves. 3b1b gives the cleanest general form. Keep CampusX as the main path; add 3b1b's number lines as the picture for "what a chain-rule factor means".
- **CampusX's new angles:** checking hand-written backprop against Keras with identical starting weights; the stuck classifier as a real-world surprise.
- **StatQuest's angle:** derivatives already found stay valid when more parameters are added (Pt 1, 06:00). This is why the shared factor −2(y − ŷ) appears everywhere; the Note states the shared factor but not this reason.
- **Clash:** CampusX says Keras "took 75 epochs to converge … loss 1.47 vs ours 1.34" (~30:00), suggesting a difference. The Note shows that with plain SGD, batch size 1 and no shuffling Keras matches to four digits (section 5, `keras_gap.png`). **The Note is right**: the difference in the video comes from settings, not the algorithm.
- **Clash (why stuck):** CampusX lists "less data, initialization, optimizer" as possible reasons (~57:00). The Note proves it is symmetric initialization plus a tiny learning rate (its notebook shows both columns of W¹ stay equal; Goodfellow et al. 2016, §8.4). **The Note is right.**

**Contradictions:** the two clashes above; both resolved in the Note's favour.

### 1017 Backpropagation: the why

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Backpropagation Part 3 | The Why", 6xO-x8y0YSY, 40:21. `transcripts/D017.whisper-en.txt`.
2. 3Blue1Brown, "Gradient descent, how neural networks learn | Deep Learning Chapter 2", IHZwWFHWa-w, 20:33. `transcripts/teaching-video/1017-3b1b-gradient-descent-ch2.txt`.
3. StatQuest, "The Chain Rule, Clearly Explained!!!", wl1myxrtQHQ, 18:23. `transcripts/teaching-video/1017-statquest-chain-rule.txt` (for the chain-rule picture used in 1015/1016).

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~03:00–09:00: the loss is a function of all 9 parameters: write ŷ out in full; x and y are constants; "a box with 9 knobs".
2. 3b1b 04:30–05:00: the same idea at scale: the cost "takes 13 000 weights and biases as input and spits out one number describing how bad they are".
3. CampusX ~09:00–12:00: gradient = derivative when there are several inputs (z = x² + y² → 2x, 2y).
4. CampusX ~12:00–18:00: a derivative is a rate of change; dy/dx = 2 means one unit of x gives two of y; sign and size both matter; y = x² + 2x at x = 5 → 11.
5. 3b1b 05:00–07:00: one-input function, "shift left if the slope is positive, right if negative"; the ball rolling downhill; several valleys (local minima); steps proportional to the slope shrink near the bottom so we do not overshoot. Then two inputs: a surface, −gradient = steepest downhill.
6. 3b1b 10:30–12:00: **the non-spatial view**: each gradient component's sign says up or down, its size says how much that weight matters; gradient (3, 1) means the first input is 3 times as important. Why it clicks: it gives the 9- or 13 000-dimensional gradient a meaning without a picture.
7. CampusX ~18:00–21:00: minima by setting derivatives to 0 (x² → x = 0; x² + y² → (0, 0)); a 9-dimensional loss cannot be solved like that.
8. CampusX ~21:00–30:00: freeze 8 parameters; L as a function of b₂₁ alone is a parabola; positive slope → decrease b₂₁, negative slope → increase; "the minus sign smartly handles both cases"; step size = slope.
9. CampusX ~30:00–36:00: learning rate: start at b = −5, slope −50 → jump to 45, zig-zag; η = 0.01 smooths it; Google's gradient-descent tool with η = 0.01 (slow), 0.1 (good), 1.10 (diverges).
10. CampusX ~36:00–38:00: convergence: W_new ≈ W_old when the slope is near 0; in practice a fixed number of epochs ("government-style logic").
11. 3b1b 09:30–10:00: why neurons use smooth activations (not on/off): the cost must be smooth so small steps downhill can find a minimum.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 6 (gradient components = relative importance of each parameter) is missing; it is the plain-words meaning of the gradient for many parameters.
- Beat 5's "several valleys" point (local minima depend on the starting point) is missing from the Note's 1-D picture; Note 590 owns non-convexity, so add one line and a link.
- Beat 11 (why smooth activations) is missing; it connects to Note 1006 (0-1 loss has no slope) and Note 1027.
- The Note covers beats 1, 3, 4, 7–10 with good figures (`knobs.png`, `tangent.gif`, `bowl.png`, `slope_sign.png`, `lr_paths.png`, `convergence.png`).
- The chain-rule picture (StatQuest 02:00–06:00: weight → height slope 2; height → shoe size slope ¼; weight → shoe size = 2 × ¼ = ½) belongs in Note 1015 or 1016, where the chain rule is first used. Neither has it.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: 1-D loss with two valleys (a quartic fitted to the Note's student data with b₂₁ and one weight free); three balls from three starts roll to different minima.
2. Plotly frames: the gradient as a bar chart of 9 components for student 1 (the Note's numbers: −11.78, −5.89, −0.736, −7.36 …), bars shrinking epoch by epoch as training converges.
3. Manim: StatQuest's chain rule: three lines (weight → height, height → shoe size, weight → shoe size); a nudge of 1 on weight lifts height by 2 and shoe size by ½.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why the minus sign, why a learning rate, when to stop:** CampusX is clearer and more concrete (frozen b₂₁, the two slope cases, the zig-zag numbers, a live tool). 3b1b covers the same with the ball and the surface. Keep CampusX as main.
- **What a gradient means in many dimensions:** 3b1b's "relative importance" reading is clearer and CampusX lacks it.
- **CampusX's new angle:** "convergence vs a fixed epoch count, government style"; freezing 8 of 9 knobs to see one.
- **Clash:** CampusX says "gradient is just a fancy word for derivative" (~09:00). 3b1b and the standard definition: the gradient is the **vector** of all partial derivatives (Goodfellow et al. 2016, §4.3). CampusX later says the same in effect ("you calculate 9 slopes"). The Note says "together they are the gradient" (section 4): **the Note is right**.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX's step example: from b = −5 with slope −50 and η = 1 the new b is −5 − (−50) = 45 (~33:00): arithmetic right. With η = 0.01 it says "maybe 5 … now you are going to −0.45" (~33:00): wrong; −5 − 0.01 × (−50) = −4.5. Do not copy those numbers; the Note uses its own (b₂₁: −5 ↔ 12.36 for η = 1).

### 1018 Vanishing and exploding gradients

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Vanishing Gradient Problem in ANN | Exploding Gradient Problem | Code Example", uCrevbBh0zM, 32:16. `transcripts/D018.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs), Clearly Explained!!!", AsNTP8Kwu80, 16:37, segment 11:00–15:30 only (the same problem shown on one repeated weight). `transcripts/teaching-video/1055-statquest-rnn.txt`.
3. No dedicated StatQuest/3b1b/Khan video on vanishing gradients in feed-forward nets (searched "StatQuest vanishing gradient": only the RNN and gradient-descent videos come up).

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–03:00: Wikipedia definition; three facts: (a) a product of many numbers below 1 is tiny, (b) only deep networks, (c) sigmoid/tanh.
