2. CampusX ~03:00–06:00: the chain for an early weight multiplies several sigmoid derivatives; weight 1, η = 0.01, gradient 0.0001 → 0.999999: no change. A 10-layer net is worse. History: a reason networks failed in the 1980s–90s.
3. StatQuest 12:30–15:30: **the cleanest picture**: one weight w₂ applied at every step: w₂ = 2 → the input is multiplied by 2⁴ = 16 after 4 copies, 2⁵⁰ after 50 (explodes; big steps bounce around); w₂ = 0.5 → 0.5⁵⁰ ≈ 0 (vanishes; steps too small, training runs out of steps). Why it clicks: one number raised to a power; both failures from the same mechanism.
4. CampusX ~09:00: how to recognise it: the loss stops changing; a weight-vs-epoch plot stays flat.
5. CampusX ~11:00–17:00: Keras demo on `make_moons`, 10 hidden sigmoid layers: first-layer weights before/after 1 epoch: gradient = (w_old − w_new)/η, changes ~0.00%; 100 epochs: loss stuck at 0.69.
6. CampusX ~17:00–21:00: fix 1, fewer layers (3 hidden): loss falls to 0.39, weights move.
7. CampusX ~21:00–27:00: fix 2, ReLU: slope 0 or 1, does not squash positives; dying ReLU caveat; loss reaches ~10⁻⁴; changes of 5–26%.
8. CampusX ~27:00: fixes 3–5 named (Glorot/Xavier init, batch norm, residual blocks).
9. CampusX ~27:00–30:00: exploding: derivatives above 1; gradient 1 000, η = 0.1 → a weight jumps by 100; mainly RNNs; gradient clipping later.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 3 (one weight to a power; 2⁵⁰ vs 0.5⁵⁰) is not in the Note. The Note has `products.png` (product of k equal factors), which is close; adding StatQuest's two numbers (16 after 4 steps, 2⁵⁰ after 50) to that figure ties 1018 to Note 1060.
- The Note's experiments are richer than CampusX's (17-million-fold gradient ratio, layer-by-layer gradient figure, clipping demo, residual block). Keep.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: a "gradient signal" bar travelling backwards through the Note's 10 sigmoid layers, shrinking at each layer by that layer's measured mean factor (Note's own numbers: 0.11 at the last layer down to 6 × 10⁻⁹ at the first); the same for 10 ReLU layers in a second row, where the bar keeps its size.
2. Plotly frames: w^k for w = 0.5, 1, 2 as k goes 1 → 50, log y-axis; a horizontal band marks "useful step sizes".
3. Plotly frames: first-layer weights (20 dots) over 100 epochs for the 10-sigmoid net (frozen) and the 10-ReLU net (moving), from the Note's notebook.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Mechanism:** both give "multiply many small numbers"; StatQuest's single-weight version is simpler for a beginner; CampusX's version (chain of sigmoid derivatives in a feed-forward net) is the one this Note needs. Main: CampusX; add StatQuest's 2⁵⁰ / 0.5⁵⁰ as "another way to see it".
- **CampusX's new angles:** measuring the gradient in Keras as (w_old − w_new)/η; the five-fixes checklist; the history link (1980s–90s failure).
- **Clash (sigmoid slope):** CampusX says the sigmoid's derivative "is always between 0 and 0.5" (~03:00). Its maximum is 0.25, at z = 0 (σ′ = σ(1 − σ) ≤ ¼). **The Note is right** (section 3.2, `slopes.png`).
- **Clash (exploding arithmetic):** CampusX: weight 1, gradient 1 000, η = 0.1 → "1 − 100 = 99" (~30:00). It is −99. **The Note is right** (section 7.1: "from 1 to −99").

**Contradictions:** the two clashes above.

### 1019 MLP memoization

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "MLP Memoization", rW0eeTXas4k, 25:24. `transcripts/D019.whisper-en.txt`.
2. 3Blue1Brown, "Backpropagation calculus" (tIeHLnjs5U8), 08:00–09:00: a neuron in layer L−1 "influences the cost through multiple different paths … you have to add those up"; then "repeat the process" backwards. File `1016-3b1b-backprop-calculus.txt`.
3. No StatQuest/Khan video on memoization in backprop.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–03:00: definition (Wikipedia): store the results of expensive calls and return the cached result; trade space for time; dynamic programming.
2. CampusX ~03:00–09:00: Fibonacci by recursion: fib(5) = 8, fib(10) = 89, fib(12) = 233; fib(36) 7 s, fib(38) 19 s, fib(40) ~1 min ("48 would take 3 hours"); the call tree for fib(5) recomputes fib(3) twice and fib(2) three times; O(2ⁿ).
3. CampusX ~09:00: dictionary version: fib(100) instantly.
4. CampusX ~09:00–15:00: a 3-3-2-1 network (12 + 8 + 3 = 23 parameters); derivatives of an output weight (one link), a middle weight (two links).
5. CampusX ~15:00–21:00: a first-layer weight: O₁₁ feeds O₂₁ **and** O₂₂, so ∂L/∂W¹₁₁ = ∂L/∂ŷ·∂ŷ/∂O₂₁·∂O₂₁/∂O₁₁·∂O₁₁/∂W¹₁₁ + ∂L/∂ŷ·∂ŷ/∂O₂₂·∂O₂₂/∂O₁₁·∂O₁₁/∂W¹₁₁ (the multivariable chain rule h(f(x), g(x))).
6. 3b1b 08:00–09:00: same point, general: sum over all paths.
7. CampusX ~21:00–24:00: the factors found for the later layer reappear in the earlier ones → store and reuse; "backprop = chain rule (maths) + memoization (computer science)".

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing in content: the Note has the Fibonacci tree, the two-paths figure, the call counts (59 vs 6; 2 345 610 vs 51).
- Missing visual: an animation of the backward pass filling a "∂L/∂O" table one node at a time, each value computed from already-stored ones.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: the fib(5) tree drawn call by call; with memoization, repeated subtrees grey out and a lookup arrow points to the stored value.
2. Manim: the 3-3-2-1 network; backward pass: each node gets its ∂L/∂O written inside as soon as all nodes it feeds are done; arrows from those stored values show the reuse.

**CampusX vs the others:** CampusX is the only full source and is clear (Fibonacci first, then the network). 3b1b adds nothing new beyond "sum over paths" but states it in one line, a good summary. No clash.

**Contradictions:** none. (CampusX's "100 would take 2 months" is a rough guess; the Note uses measured call counts instead, which is better evidence.)

### 1020 Gradient descent in neural networks (batch, stochastic, mini-batch)

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Gradient Descent in Neural Networks | Batch vs Stochastics vs Mini Batch Gradient Descent", 7z6yXpYk7sw, 37:53. `transcripts/D020.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "Stochastic Gradient Descent, Clearly Explained!!!", vMh0zPT0tLI, 10:53. `transcripts/teaching-video/1020-statquest-sgd.txt`.
3. StatQuest, "Gradient Descent, Step-by-Step", sDv4f4s2SB8, 23:54. `transcripts/teaching-video/1020-statquest-gradient-descent.txt`. Its content (intercept 0 → 0.57 → 0.8 → … → 0.95, step = slope × η, stop when step < 0.001 or after 1 000 steps) belongs to ML Note 57; link, do not repeat.
4. 3Blue1Brown, "Backpropagation, intuitively" (Ilg3gGewQ5U), 09:30–10:30: mini-batches of 100; "a drunk man stumbling aimlessly down a hill but taking quick steps" vs "a carefully calculating man". File `1015-3b1b-backprop-intuitively.txt`.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–06:00: definition (minimise J(θ) by stepping against the gradient; η sets the step); the three variants differ only in how much data feeds one update; trade-off: accuracy of the update vs time per update.
2. CampusX ~06:00–09:00: batch GD: no inner loop; all 50 rows → one dot product → 50 predictions → one loss → one update; updates = epochs.
3. CampusX ~09:00–15:00: SGD: shuffle; one row → predict → loss → update; 50 rows × 10 epochs = 500 updates.
4. StatQuest 03:00–04:30: **why SGD exists**: logistic regression with 23 000 genes and 1 million samples → 23 billion terms per step, 2.3 trillion for 1 000 steps; one sample per step divides that by a million.
5. StatQuest 05:00–07:00: SGD works because of **redundancy**: 12 points in 3 clusters; one point per step still reaches intercept 0.85, slope 0.68 (least squares: 0.87, 0.68). Visual: the line moving one random point at a time.
6. CampusX ~15:00–21:00: Keras `batch_size = n` (batch) vs `1` (stochastic) on Social Network Ads (400 rows, age and salary, standardized): 10 epochs: batch 0.95 s, SGD ~10 s; but val accuracy 0.65 (batch) vs 0.95 (SGD): "batch finishes epochs faster, SGD reaches the solution in fewer epochs".
7. CampusX ~21:00–27:00: loss curves: SGD spiky, batch smooth; 3-D bowl: batch walks down smoothly, SGD "as if someone has drunk alcohol" (same image as 3b1b 10:00); spikes can jump out of local minima but never settle exactly.
8. CampusX ~27:00–30:00: vectorization: the dot product replaces the loop (fast) but needs all rows in RAM ("10 crore rows will not fit").
9. CampusX ~30:00–33:00 + StatQuest 07:00–08:30: mini-batch: 320 rows, batch 32 → 10 updates/epoch; "best of both worlds"; StatQuest: 3 samples per step → intercept 0.86 (closer to 0.87).
10. StatQuest 08:30–09:30: SGD can take one more step when **new data arrives** (weight 1.1, height 2 → intercept 0.878, slope 0.7) without restarting.
11. CampusX ~33:00–37:00: why batch sizes are powers of 2 (hardware; not required); 400 rows with batch 150 → 150, 150, 100.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 4 (the cost argument with numbers: 23 billion terms per step) is missing; the Note argues from memory (section 9) and speed in seconds, not from the count of terms.
- Beat 5 (redundancy: many similar rows make one row a good stand-in) is missing; it is the reason SGD's noisy steps still head the right way.
- Beat 10 (online updates with new data) is missing; ML Note 05 (online learning) owns it; one line and a link.
- The Note has strong figures (`batches.png`, `sgd_loop.gif`, `batch_race.gif`, `loss_curves.png`, memory figure). The 2-D "drunk walk vs smooth walk" contour path is missing.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the loss contours of the Note's network restricted to two weights (as in Note 1017's two-knob figure); three paths drawn update by update: batch (smooth, few steps), mini-batch 32 (slightly wiggly), SGD (drunk walk, many steps).
2. Plotly frames: StatQuest's redundancy demo on the Note's Social Network Ads data: logistic boundary after each SGD step vs the full-batch boundary; the two converge.
3. Plotly frames: a counter race: three bars "updates done" vs "seconds elapsed" for batch, 32 and 1 (the Note's measured timings).

**CampusX vs the others**
- **The three variants and Keras's `batch_size`:** CampusX is clearer and code-backed; StatQuest and 3b1b cover the same in a few minutes.
- **Why SGD:** StatQuest's term-count and redundancy arguments are clearer than CampusX's "it converges in fewer epochs".
- **Analogy:** CampusX's "drunk person" and 3b1b's "drunk man stumbling quickly down a hill" are the same image: no clash, use it once.
- **CampusX's new angles:** "batch is faster per epoch, SGD is faster to the solution" (resolves a common confusion); the uneven last batch (150, 150, 100); the interview question on powers of 2.
- **Clash (wording):** CampusX's summary at ~33:00 says "the fastest is batch, the slowest is mini-batch and the slowest is stochastic", a slip: by time per epoch the order is batch < mini-batch < SGD, i.e. mini-batch is in the middle. The Note's section 10 says "middle ground": **the Note is right**.
- **Clash (powers of 2):** CampusX: RAM "is designed to handle binary values", "honestly I don't know what exactly is happening". The Note says powers of 2 are customary for hardware, not required (section 11.1). Keep the Note's softer claim; Goodfellow et al. 2016, §8.1.3 says the same ("some kinds of hardware achieve better runtime with specific sizes of arrays… power of 2").

**Contradictions:** the two wording clashes above.

### 1021 Improving a neural network

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "How to Improve the Performance of a Neural Network", Ue_6n1yT_R8, 30:24. `transcripts/D021.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video: this is a roadmap Note. StatQuest/3b1b/Khan have no "how to improve a network" overview (searched "StatQuest early stopping", "StatQuest dropout", "StatQuest batch normalization": no hit from these channels).

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–03:00: "anyone can reach 90% with Keras; who can take it to 92%?" Two routes: tune hyperparameters, fix problems.
2. ~03:00–06:00: seven hyperparameters (hidden layers, neurons per layer, learning rate, optimizer, batch size, activation, epochs); four problems (vanishing/exploding gradients, not enough data, slow training, overfitting).
3. ~09:00–12:00: hidden layers: 3 layers of 32 beat one of 512, because of representation learning (lines/edges → shapes → face); add layers until overfitting.
4. ~12:00: transfer learning preview: a human-face network's early layers reused for monkey faces.
5. ~15:00–18:00: neurons per layer: input and output fixed by the data; the old "pyramid" rule (64 → 32 → 16) works no better than equal widths in experiments; "sufficient" neurons: a 1-neuron bottleneck loses features that later layers cannot recover; start generous.
6. ~18:00–24:00: batch size: small (8–32) generalises better; large (up to 8 192, limited by GPU RAM) is fast but unstable; learning-rate warm-up fixes large batches.
7. ~24:00: epochs: set high, use early stopping (callbacks).
8. ~24:00–27:00: the fix list: vanishing/exploding → weight init, activation, batch norm, gradient clipping; data → transfer learning, unsupervised pre-training; slow → optimizers, LR scheduler; overfitting → L1/L2, dropout.

**What the Note lacks**
- All beats are in the Note, with `roadmap.png`, `layer_shapes.png`, `fix_notes.png`. Missing: the monkey-face transfer-learning picture (beat 4) as a small figure; one image makes "early layers are reusable" concrete. Link Note 1053.
- No experiment backs "3 × 32 beats 1 × 512" or "pyramid = no better". Either add a tiny notebook comparison (same parameter budget, one dataset) or soften to "often".

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the Note's `fix_notes.png` as a clickable roadmap: each problem node lights up its fixes one by one, with the Note number on each chip.
2. Plotly (experiment): validation accuracy for 1 × 512 vs 3 × 32 vs pyramid 64-32-16 on the churn data of Note 1011, 5 seeds averaged, as bars; this grounds beats 3 and 5.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source. CampusX's angles (monkey-face transfer, "sufficient" neurons, warm-up for large batches) are practitioner advice; keep them, mark experience-based claims as such unless the Note tests them.

**Contradictions**
- None between the Note and CampusX. Note that the batch-size claims need a source: Keskar et al. 2017 ("On Large-Batch Training…") for the generalisation gap; Goyal et al. 2017 for warm-up with 8 192-image batches. Check the Note's Sources list cites them.

### 1022 Early stopping

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Early Stopping In Neural Networks", Ygvskt5HadI, 12:00. `transcripts/D022.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video (StatQuest "early stopping" search returns only Lasso and pruning videos).

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–03:00: how many epochs? Too many → overfitting. Data: `make_circles`, 100 points; one hidden layer of 256 neurons; 3 500 epochs.
2. ~03:00: loss curves: validation loss falls until ~300–350, then rises; the train–validation gap widens = overfitting.
3. ~03:00–06:00: callbacks run after every epoch; `EarlyStopping` stops at epoch 327; the decision boundary at that point is as good; ~10% of the epochs.
4. ~06:00–09:00: parameters: `monitor` (val_loss or accuracy), `min_delta`, `patience` (wait 3 or 5 epochs), `verbose`, `mode` (auto/min/max), `baseline`, `restore_best_weights`.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing: the Note has all beats, a live "patience counter" figure and the decision boundaries. It also fixes CampusX's omission: with `restore_best_weights=False` the model keeps the weights of the **last** epoch (503), not the best (469).

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the Note's `EarlyStopping as it happens` figure already animates; add a second run with patience 5 vs 50 vs 200 so the effect of patience is visible.
2. Plotly frames: the decision boundary at epochs 50, 469, 503, 3 500 side by side, morphing (Note's own runs).

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions:** none. (CampusX stops at epoch 327, the Note at 503; different seeds.)

### 1023 Data scaling in ANN

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Data Scaling in Neural Network | Feature Scaling in ANN", mzRO0cVppQ0, 16:55. `transcripts/D023.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No StatQuest/3b1b/Khan video on input scaling for networks. CampusX shows Andrew Ng's elongated-vs-round contour picture (~06:00), from his Coursera course (not one of our channels; not opened). ML Notes 24 and 25 own standardization and normalization.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–03:00: age (0–80) vs salary (thousands to crores); different scales slow or break training.
2. ~03:00–06:00: Social Network Ads (age, estimated salary, purchased); 128 ReLU → sigmoid; 100 epochs; validation accuracy oscillates 40–60%, never converges.
3. ~06:00: why: the salary weight w₂ gets most of the update, w₁ (age) is ignored.
4. ~06:00–09:00: Andrew Ng's picture: unscaled → elongated, uneven loss contours, many oscillations; scaled → round contours, direct path.
5. ~09:00–12:00: the fix: standardize (mean 0, sd 1, "unit circle") or normalize (min–max, "unit box"); normalize when min and max are known (CGPA 0–10), standardize otherwise (salary).
6. ~12:00–15:00: `StandardScaler`; same scatter, new scale; validation accuracy climbs past 92%.
7. ~15:00: "always do this step; no harm".

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing in content. The Note adds the mechanism with a citation (gradient ∝ input, LeCun et al. 1998 §4.3; salary gradients ~2 000× larger) and its own contour figure. It is ahead of CampusX here.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the Note's contour figure as a race: gradient descent on raw inputs (zig-zag across the narrow valley) and on standardized inputs (straight in), one frame per step, same learning rate.
2. Plotly frames: the scatter of 320 users morphing from raw to standardized axes (the Note's `raw_vs_scaled.png` as two key frames with interpolation).

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source on our channel list.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX: standardized data "generally comes between −1 and +1" (~09:00). False: sd 1 means about 95% of a normal feature lies within ±2. **The Note is right** (section 5.1 Extra: age runs −1.95 to 2.17).
- CampusX: use normalization "when there are outliers" (~15:00). Min–max scaling is the one most distorted by outliers, because one extreme value sets the max (scikit-learn docs, "Compare the effect of different scalers on data with outliers"). The Note does not repeat this claim; a rewrite must not add it.

### 1024 Dropout

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Dropout Layer in Deep Learning | Dropouts in ANN", gyTlcHVeBjM, 27:51. `transcripts/D024.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No StatQuest/3b1b/Khan video on dropout (search "StatQuest dropout" returns only the Ridge video).

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–03:00: overfitting picture: a green decision boundary hugging every point vs a smooth black one.
2. ~03:00: four standard fixes (more data, simpler network, early stopping, L1/L2); dropout is the fifth (Srivastava, Hinton et al.).
3. ~06:00–09:00: 5 inputs, two hidden layers of 5, 1 output; at each step switch off random nodes (with their connections); a different sub-network every time. "How can breaking the network help?"
4. ~09:00–15:00: why it helps, two reasons: (a) fewer nodes → less capacity for tiny patterns; (b) a node cannot rely on one input that may vanish next step, so it spreads its weights.
5. ~15:00–18:00: **company analogy**: every morning a random 50% of employees are told to stay home; a coder can no longer lean on the testing team alone, so everyone learns to cover for others; the company becomes resilient.
6. ~18:00–24:00: **random-forest analogy**: column sampling builds 100 different trees that vote; dropout with 9 droppable nodes gives 2⁸ (CampusX's count) sub-networks, a different one each step; prediction combines them.
7. ~24:00–27:00: at test time all nodes are on; each weight × (1 − p): with p = 0.25 a node was present 75 times in 100, so its weight is scaled by 0.75; Keras handles this.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 5 (company analogy) is missing. It is the plain-words picture for "no node can rely on one input" (NOTE-RULES §5 "everyday analogy for the why"). The Note gives the mechanism (section 5.2) but no analogy.
- The Note has `dropout_steps.png`, a four-step figure and the train-vs-predict scaling figure; good. An animation of the masks changing step by step would show the "different network each time" claim directly.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: the 5-5-5-1 network; for 6 training steps a new random mask greys out nodes and their edges; a counter below shows "sub-network #k of 2ⁿ".
2. Plotly frames: one hidden node's 5 incoming weights as bars over training, without dropout (one bar grows dominant) and with p = 0.5 (bars stay balanced). Data: the Note's notebook network, logged weights.
3. Manim: inverted dropout: during training kept outputs × 1/(1 − p); at prediction nothing changes; side by side with the paper's version (weights × (1 − p) at prediction).

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source. CampusX's two analogies (company, random forest) are its best angle; keep both, the random forest as the main "why" (the Note already has it, section 5.3) and the company as a second short view.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX: a new set of nodes is dropped "for every epoch" (~06:00–09:00, ~21:00 "for every epoch you are training a unique neural network"). Dropout samples a new mask for **every training case / mini-batch**, not once per epoch (Srivastava et al. 2014, §4: "for each training case in a mini-batch, we sample a thinned network"). **The Note is right** ("at every training step").
- CampusX counts 2⁸ sub-networks for 9 droppable nodes (~21:00), saying the output is excluded; with 9 droppable nodes it is 2⁹. Minor; the Note does not use this count.

### 1025 Dropout code

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Dropout Layers in ANN | Code Example | Regression | Classification", tgIx04ML7-Y, 19:17. `transcripts/D025.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–03:00: regression data with a linear trend (code from a GitHub repo); 2 hidden layers of 128 ReLU, linear output, Adam (lr 0.01), 500 epochs; training MSE 0.003 vs test 0.04; the curve zig-zags through the black training points.
2. ~03:00–06:00: `Dropout(0.2)` after each hidden layer; training loss up (0.01), test down, gap smaller; the curve smooths. Exercise: try 0.5.
3. ~06:00–12:00: classification on two noisy clouds; training 92%, validation 74%; islands in the decision boundary; p = 0.2 not enough; p = 0.5 gives a clean boundary, validation 83%.
4. ~12:00–15:00: effect of p: 0 → overfit, 0.2 and 0.5 smoother, 0.75 underfit; stay within 0.2–0.5. "Dropout is regularization by randomization."
5. ~15:00: tips: raise p on overfitting, lower on underfitting; try the last hidden layer first; CNNs 40–50%, RNNs 20–30%, ANNs 10–50%.
6. ~15:00–18:00: drawbacks: slower convergence; the loss changes from step to step, so gradients are harder to check and debug.

**What the Note lacks**
- All beats present, with its own numbers and a dropout-rate sweep GIF averaged over 5 seeds. The CampusX rule of thumb "CNN 40–50%, RNN 20–30%" (beat 5) is not in the Note; it is experience-based and has no cited source, so leaving it out is right (NOTE-RULES §3).

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the Note's `dropout_sweep.gif` exists; add the classification boundary morphing across p = 0, 0.1, …, 0.8 for the two-cloud data.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX says test MSE 0.04 is "almost 10% more" than training MSE 0.003 (~03:00); it is about 13 times larger. The Note uses its own numbers (0.006 vs 0.186, "about 29 times"), which is correct.

### 1026 Regularization in deep learning

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Regularization in Deep Learning | L2 Regularization in ANN | L1 Regularization | Weight Decay in ANN", 4xRonrhtkzc, 35:57. `transcripts/D026.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "Regularization Part 1: Ridge (L2) Regression", Q81RR3yKn30, 20:26. `transcripts/teaching-video/1026-statquest-ridge.txt` (00:00–15:00 read). ML Note 63 owns ridge; use this only for the one picture below.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–03:00: overfitting = rote learning ("a student who memorised the book without the concepts").
2. CampusX ~03:00–09:00: **why networks overfit**: 1 neuron → a line; 10 → the line bends; 50 → a curve; 257 and 1 000 → cuts around every point. "Each neuron gives the capacity for one more line." Fix: push weights towards 0 so neurons effectively disappear.
3. CampusX ~09:00–12:00: two routes: more data (incl. augmentation: crop, flip a dog photo), simpler model (dropout, early stopping, regularization). L2 used "99%" of the time in DL.
4. StatQuest 02:00–07:00: **the penalty with numbers**: two training mice; least squares fits them exactly (SSR 0) with slope 1.3 → SSR + λ·slope² = 0 + 1.69 = 1.69; a flatter line (slope 0.8) has residuals 0.3 and 0.1 → 0.09 + 0.01 + 0.64 = 0.74, so the penalised cost prefers it, and it predicts the test mice better.
5. StatQuest 07:00–08:30: a smaller slope means the prediction is **less sensitive** to the input ("regularization is just another way to say desensitization").
6. StatQuest 08:30–10:00: λ = 0 → least squares; larger λ → slope towards 0; choose λ by cross-validation. 14:00: the penalty covers every parameter except the intercept (CampusX: biases are not penalised).
7. CampusX ~12:00–18:00: cost + λ/(2n) Σ w²; L1 uses |w|; layer-wise form Σₗ Σᵢ Σⱼ (wᵢⱼˡ)²; λ large → underfit; biases excluded.
8. CampusX ~18:00–24:00: derivation: w_new = (1 − ηλ)w_old − η ∂L/∂w: "weight decay"; L1 gives exact zeros (sparse), L2 only small weights.
9. CampusX ~24:00–33:00: Keras on `make_moons` (noise 0.25), 128-128, 2 000 epochs, `kernel_regularizer=L2(0.03)`: clean boundary; loss curves side by side; first-layer weights: max 1.75/min −2.7 without, 0.3/−0.5 with; box plot and density plot. L1 with λ = 0.001 overfits a bit more.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beats 4–5 (StatQuest's two-point example and "less sensitive to inputs") are missing. The Note explains *that* the weights shrink (weight decay) but not *why smaller weights generalise*. StatQuest's "a flatter slope reacts less to small changes in the input" is the plain-words reason; Note 63 already has it, so a one-line recap and a link suffice.
- The Note's `neurons.png` and hyperplane figure cover beat 2 well (better than CampusX: dashed hyperplanes per neuron).

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: λ slider 0 → 0.1 on the Note's moons network: decision boundary, loss curves and the first-layer weight histogram update together (the Note has the three end states; fill in 6–8 λ values).
2. Plotly frames: weight decay in one weight: w × (1 − ηλ) each step with no data gradient, then with it; the weight settles at a smaller value.
3. Plotly frames: StatQuest's two-point example: λ from 0 to 3; the fitted line flattens; the test points' residuals shrink then grow (underfit).

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why networks overfit:** CampusX is clearer (neurons as lines). StatQuest does not cover networks.
- **Why a penalty helps:** StatQuest is clearer ("desensitization", with numbers). CampusX says "push weights to 0 so nodes vanish", which explains less: L2 does not remove nodes (CampusX itself says weights only go *near* 0).
- **CampusX's new angles:** reading the weights out of Keras (box plot, min/max) to *see* the shrinkage; "L2 in DL 99% of the time".
- **Clash (weight decay):** CampusX: L2 "is called weight decay … although weight decay is a little different" (~21:00). For plain SGD the two are identical; for adaptive optimizers such as Adam they differ (Loshchilov and Hutter 2019, "Decoupled Weight Decay Regularization", which introduced AdamW). **The Note is right**: its section 6 Extra limits the equality to plain gradient descent and section 7 shows the Adam case with Loshchilov and Hutter 2019 cited.

**Contradictions:** the weight-decay subtlety above; otherwise none.

### 1027 Activation functions (sigmoid, tanh, ReLU)

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Activation Functions in Deep Learning | Sigmoid, Tanh and Relu Activation Function", 7LcUkgzx3AY, 44:52. `transcripts/D027.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "Neural Networks Pt. 3: ReLU In Action!!!", 68BZ5f7P94E, 8:58. `transcripts/teaching-video/1027-statquest-relu-in-action.txt`.
3. StatQuest, "The GELU, SiLU and SwiGLU activation functions, clearly explained!!!", 2FaI2Fen1mQ, 24:17. `transcripts/teaching-video/1027-statquest-gelu-silu-swiglu.txt`.
4. StatQuest, "The Essential Main Ideas of Neural Networks" (CqOfi41LfDw) 04:30–06:00: softplus, ReLU and sigmoid as the "bent or curved lines" that are the building blocks of the squiggle.
5. 3Blue1Brown ch1 (aircAruvnKk) 17:00–18:30: sigmoid is "old school"; ReLU "seems to be much easier to train" for very deep networks.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–03:00: definition: z = Σwx + b, a = g(z); "a gate deciding whether and how much the neuron is activated".
2. CampusX ~03:00–06:00: experiment: two hidden layers of 128 with linear activations on non-linear data → a single straight line; with ReLU → a curved boundary.
3. CampusX ~06:00–12:00: algebra: a₂ = W₂(W₁a₀ + b₁) + b₂ = W′a₀ + b′, "a polynomial of degree 1".
4. StatQuest ReLU 01:30–07:00: **how a bent line builds a shape**: dosage × 1.70 − 0.85 → ReLU → 0 until dosage ≈ 0.5, then rising; × (−40.8) → bent blue line; bottom node gives a straight orange line × 2.70; add → green wedge; −16 bias; final ReLU clips negatives → "green pointy thing" that fits low/medium/high. Why it clicks: it shows ReLU, which "seems weird because it's not curvy", still bends the output; it answers "how is max(0, z) non-linear?" visually.
5. CampusX ~12:00–18:00: five properties of an ideal activation: non-linear, differentiable, cheap, zero-centred, non-saturating.
6. CampusX ~18:00–36:00: sigmoid: shape (0.5 at 0), derivative peak 0.25, near 0 outside ±3; pros (probability, non-linear, differentiable); cons: saturates → vanishing gradient (large x from large weights); not zero-centred → all weights into a node move in the same direction ("Gurgaon to Delhi without a left turn": zig-zag, slow); exponential is slow.
7. CampusX ~36:00–39:00: tanh = stretched sigmoid, range −1 to 1, derivative 1 − tanh², peak 1; zero-centred (fixes the zig-zag) but still saturates and uses exponentials.
8. CampusX ~39:00–43:00: ReLU = max(0, z): non-linear (max(0, x + 1) − max(0, x − 1) makes a bend), non-saturating for z > 0, cheap, faster convergence; cons: not differentiable at 0 (use 0 or 1), not zero-centred (batch norm helps), dying ReLU (next Note).
9. StatQuest GELU 03:00–08:30 (history): sigmoids made large networks hard to train (tiny derivatives far from 0); ReLU (2010s, in the first transformer 2017) trains large nets but they tend to overfit. 11:00–17:00: GELU = x · Φ(x) built as "input-dependent dropout": at x = −2 keep-probability 0.02 → output −0.04; at −1 → −0.16; at 1 → 0.84; at 2 → 1.96. SiLU = x · σ(x). SwiGLU adds trainable weights.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 4 (StatQuest's ReLU wedge with numbers) is missing; the Note proves ReLU's non-linearity by experiment and algebra but has no picture of *how* two ReLUs and a sum make a bend. CampusX's max(0, x + 1) − max(0, x − 1) example (beat 8) is also missing; both fit in one small figure.
- Beat 9 (GELU/SiLU, used in modern transformers, Notes 1080 and 1087) is missing from both 1027 and 1028 (grep: no "GELU", "SiLU" or "Swish" in either Note). A short Extra with StatQuest's dropout-average derivation and the five numbers would close the gap before the transformer Notes.
- The zero-centred argument has its own figure (`(a) all-positive inputs … quadrants`); good. CampusX's "no left turn" analogy is not in the Note.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: StatQuest's ReLU wedge with his numbers: frame 1 ReLU(1.70x − 0.85); frame 2 × (−40.8); frame 3 the orange straight line × 2.70; frame 4 sum; frame 5 −16; frame 6 final ReLU; dosage points overlaid.
2. Plotly frames: two ReLU units max(0, x + 1) and −max(0, x − 1) added → a ramp that flattens on both sides; then three units → a bump. Shows "ReLUs build any piecewise-linear shape".
3. Plotly frames: GELU built point by point: for x = −2, −1, 0, 1, 2 the keep-probability Φ(x) bar and the weighted average dot appear; the dots join into the GELU curve, overlaid on ReLU.
4. Plotly frames: zero-centring: a 2-D weight update restricted to the "both up / both down" quadrants zig-zags to a target in the "one up, one down" quadrant (the Note's figure (b), animated).

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why non-linearity:** CampusX is clearer (experiment + algebra). StatQuest shows the same with shapes.
- **ReLU's non-linearity:** StatQuest's wedge is clearer than CampusX's one-line example; use both.
- **Sigmoid/tanh/ReLU pros and cons:** CampusX is the most complete (five properties, the zero-centred argument with the chain rule). StatQuest only covers the vanishing-derivative point.
- **CampusX's new angles:** the five-property checklist; the "no left turn" analogy; the max(0, x + 1) − max(0, x − 1) construction.
- **StatQuest's new angle:** GELU/SiLU as a smooth, dropout-averaged ReLU; "large ReLU networks overfit" as the motivation.
- **Clash:** CampusX calls ReLU "not completely differentiable at 0" (correct) and StatQuest says "define the slope at the bend as 0 or 1, it doesn't really matter" (07:30): consistent.

**Contradictions:** none between sources and Note.

### 1028 ReLU variants

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Relu Variants Explained | Leaky Relu | Parametric Relu | Elu | Selu | Activation Functions Part 2", 2OwWs7Hzr9g, 33:25. `transcripts/D028.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest GELU/SiLU/SwiGLU video (2FaI2Fen1mQ, see Note 1027) 00:30–02:00: GELU and SiLU look like ReLU with a small dip between −2 and 0 and a smooth bend; no StatQuest/3b1b/Khan video on Leaky ReLU, PReLU, ELU or SELU.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–03:00: dying ReLU: a dead neuron outputs 0 for every input and stays dead; >50% dead → the network cannot capture the patterns; 100% → no network.
2. ~03:00–09:00: mechanism: one hidden ReLU unit; if z₁ < 0 then a₁ = 0 and ∂a₁/∂z₁ = 0; that factor sits in ∂L/∂w₁ and ∂L/∂w₂, so neither updates.
3. ~09:00–15:00: two causes: a high learning rate throws w negative; a large negative bias. Why permanent: weights stop changing and scaled inputs (0–1) are too small to lift z above 0.
4. ~15:00–18:00: three fixes: lower learning rate; bias init 0.01; ReLU variants.
5. ~18:00–24:00: Leaky ReLU: 0.01z for z < 0; derivative 0.01, so "a small gradient keeps flowing"; non-saturating, cheap, no dying, close to zero-centred.
6. ~24:00: PReLU: slope a learned per node.
7. ~24:00–27:00: ELU: α(eᶻ − 1) for z < 0; continuous and differentiable; better generalisation in experiments; slower (exponential).
8. ~27:00–33:00: SELU = λ·ELU with fixed λ ≈ 1.0507, α ≈ 1.6733; self-normalising (mean 0, sd 1 per layer); "90+ page paper", little adoption.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing in content; the Note adds the experiment (learning rate 10 kills 41% and 69%; bias −1 kills 72% and 100%) and the SELU 30-layer figure, and corrects two CampusX slips (below).
- Missing visual: a dying-node animation: one node's z over training as its weights are thrown negative by a big step, then flat forever.
- GELU/SiLU: see Note 1027's gap; if 1028 is the "family of ReLU-like functions" Note, an Extra here is the natural home.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: one ReLU node during training with η = 10 (the Note's moons experiment): the histogram of its z over the training set slides left past 0 and stops moving; a twin Leaky ReLU node keeps moving.
2. Plotly frames: the four variants' curves morphing from ReLU as their negative-side parameter grows (Leaky slope 0 → 0.3; ELU α 0 → 1.5; SELU fixed).
3. Plotly frames: standard deviation of activations layer by layer (the Note's `selu_layers.png` data) drawn layer by layer for ReLU, ELU, SELU.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source on these four variants. CampusX's derivation of why a dead node stays dead is the main path.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX: "In ReLU there is a discontinuity at x = 0" (~24:00). ReLU is continuous; only its slope jumps. **The Note is right** (section 6.1 Extra).
- CampusX: ELU's α "generally between 0.1 and 0.3" (~24:00). The ELU paper and Keras use α = 1.0 by default (Clevert et al. 2016; Keras `elu(alpha=1.0)`). The Note uses α = 1. **The Note is right.**
- CampusX: SELU is self-normalising "because values come on both sides" (~30:00). The proof needs inputs with mean 0, variance 1 and LeCun-normal weights (Klambauer et al. 2017). **The Note is right** (section 6.2 Extra, `kernel_initializer="lecun_normal"`).

### 1029 Weight initialization: what not to do

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Weight Initialization Techniques | What not to do?", 2MSY0HwH5Ss, 49:24. `transcripts/D029.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No StatQuest/3b1b/Khan video on weight initialization (search "StatQuest weight initialization": no match). StatQuest Backprop Details Pt 2 (GKZoOHXGcLo, 09:30–10:00) only says weights are drawn from a standard normal and biases start at 0, "one of many ways".

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–03:00: initialisation is step 0 of training; bad starts cause vanishing gradients, exploding gradients, slow convergence; sigmoid + bad init stalled DL research (~2000s).
2. ~03:00–12:00: zeros with ReLU or tanh: CGPA/IQ → 2 hidden → 1 output; z = 0 → a = 0 → every gradient has a 0 factor → no update. Keras demo: weights stay 0, model predicts 1 for all (50%).
3. ~12:00–24:00: zeros with sigmoid: σ(0) = 0.5; the weights move but all weights leaving x₁ stay equal, all leaving x₂ stay equal → the layer acts as **one node** → linear boundary; demo with 10 hidden nodes.
4. ~24:00–30:00: any shared constant (0.5) → same symmetry for ReLU, tanh, sigmoid.
5. ~30:00–39:00: small random (0.01 × randn), 500 inputs × 3 layers of 500 tanh: activation histograms collapse to 0 layer by layer → vanishing gradients; sigmoid slower; ReLU: very slow convergence (Playground: ~1 500 epochs, 5 minutes).
6. ~42:00–48:00: large random (weights 0–1, sums 100–500): tanh/sigmoid saturate at ±1 / 0–1 (histograms at the ends) → slow or vanishing; ReLU passes big values → big gradients → unstable, exploding.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing in content; the Note has the symmetry figure, the activation-histogram GIF (10 layers, three spreads), the Keras loss curves. It also links back to the stuck classifier of Note 1016, which is the same symmetry problem: worth a one-line cross-reference if not present.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the Note's activation histograms layer by layer (exists as a GIF); add ReLU with 0.01 × randn, ×1 and He in a second row.
2. Manim: symmetry: 3 hidden nodes, all weights 0.5; each update paints the edges from x₁ the same colour and from x₂ the same colour; the three nodes merge visually into one.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source. CampusX's systematic "four things not to do" with a Keras check for each is the main path.

**Contradictions**
- None with the Note. CampusX's "large" weights are drawn in 0 to 1 (~42:00) while its next video calls randn × 1 (range about −3 to 3) "large"; the Note uses randn × 1 throughout, consistently.

### 1030 Xavier and He initialization

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Xavier/Glorat And He Weight Initialization in Deep Learning", nwVOSgcrbQI, 21:07. `transcripts/D030.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–03:00: recap of the four failures; we need random weights with a spread neither too small (0.01 × randn) nor too large (randn).
2. ~06:00–12:00: **intuition**: a node sums n products wᵢxᵢ; with 250 inputs, ±0.01 gives z ≈ 0 and ±3 gives z up to ±250. So the spread must depend on n: variance 1/n; many inputs → smaller weights; few inputs → bigger weights.
3. ~12:00: Xavier normal: randn × √(1/fan-in) (variant √(2/(fan-in + fan-out))). Keras demo with 4 tanh layers of 10: randn × √(1/2) for layer 1, √(1/10) after; trains well.
4. ~15:00: He normal: √(2/fan-in), for ReLU. Uniform versions: Xavier limit √(6/(fan-in + fan-out)); He limit √(6/fan-in).
5. ~18:00: Keras: `kernel_initializer="he_normal"` etc.; default `glorot_uniform`.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing in content; the Note adds the derivation of the factor 2 for ReLU and the 10-layer standard-deviation figure. Missing visual: a fan-in slider showing z's spread staying near 1 under Xavier while it grows or shrinks under a fixed scale.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: fan-in n from 2 to 1 000; histograms of z = Σwᵢxᵢ for fixed scale 0.01, fixed scale 1, and √(1/n): only the last stays the same width.
2. Plotly frames: the Note's 10-layer std-of-activations figure drawn layer by layer for tanh+Xavier and ReLU+He.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX calls Xavier and He "heuristics … Jugaad … found by experimentation" (~03:00, ~15:00). Both come from a variance-preservation derivation, then tested by experiment (Glorot and Bengio 2010, §4.2; He et al. 2015, §2.2). **The Note is right** to present the derivation (section 3.3 and the Extra on the 2).

### 1031 Batch normalization

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Batch Normalization in Deep Learning | Batch Learning in Keras", 2AscwXePInA, 43:39. `transcripts/D031.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No StatQuest/3b1b/Khan video on batch normalization (search "StatQuest batch normalization" returns an unrelated DESeq2 normalization video).

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–03:00: definition: faster, more stable training (2015); normalize hidden-layer activations with the current batch's mean and variance, before or after the activation.
2. ~03:00–06:00: reason 1: normalized inputs give round loss contours (CGPA vs IQ), allowing a higher learning rate; so normalize every layer's input.
3. ~06:00–09:00: covariate shift: same boundary, shifted inputs; the **rose example**: trained on red roses, tested on roses of every colour.
4. ~09:00–12:00: internal covariate shift (Ioffe and Szegedy's definition): each layer's input distribution changes as earlier weights change; **Chinese whispers picture**: "P" → "B" → "knees" → "cheese" → "fleece".
5. ~12:00–21:00: how it works: mini-batch only; layer by layer, optional; per node; z = w₁·CGPA + w₂·IQ + b; batch of 4 → 4 values of z per node → μ_B, σ_B → (z − μ)/√(σ² + ε).
6. ~21:00–27:00: scale and shift γẑ + β (γ = 1, β = 0 at start in Keras), learned per node; if γ = σ and β = μ the network can undo the normalization: flexibility.
7. ~27:00–30:00: in a deep net: a BN "layer" between layers; 2 learnable parameters per node, updated by backprop.
8. ~30:00–33:00: prediction: one query point has no batch → keep an exponentially weighted moving average of μ and σ during training; per node 4 numbers (γ, β learnable; moving μ, σ not); 3 nodes → 12 parameters, 6 non-trainable.
9. ~33:00–39:00: advantages: stable (wider hyperparameter range), faster (higher LR), mild regularization (batch noise), less sensitive to initialization.
10. ~39:00–43:00: Keras `BatchNormalization()` after each hidden layer; concentric circles: 60% accuracy after 20 epochs with BN vs 70 without.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 4's Chinese-whispers picture and beat 3's rose example are missing (the Note uses an abstract covariate-shift figure). Both are everyday analogies for the "why" (NOTE-RULES §5).
- The Note correctly adds Santurkar et al. 2018 (BN may work by smoothing the loss, not by reducing internal covariate shift). Keep both views, with the ICS story labelled as the original paper's motivation.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the Note's second-layer input distribution over training (exists as a still), with and without BN, one frame per 50 updates: the unnormalized cloud drifts, the normalized one stays put.
2. Plotly frames: the Note's 4-value batch z = 2, 4, 6, 8 through subtract-mean → divide-by-sd → ×γ = 1.5 → +β = 0.5, one step per frame on a number line.
3. Plotly frames: the moving mean converging to the true mean over the 2 600 batches (the Note's `moving_mean.png` animated).

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source. CampusX's analogies (red roses; Chinese whispers) are its best angle.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX: after normalizing, "all your activations will come between 0 and 1" (~21:00). Standardized values have mean 0 and sd 1; they are not bounded. **The Note is right** (it says mean 0, variance 1).
- CampusX: the layer stores a moving average of the **standard deviation** (~33:00). Keras stores `moving_mean` and `moving_variance` (Keras `BatchNormalization` docs). **The Note is right** (section 5: "moving mean and moving variance").
- Mechanism: CampusX gives internal covariate shift as the reason; Santurkar et al. (2018) show BN helps even when ICS is not reduced. The Note's Extra handles this.

### 1032 Optimizers in deep learning (overview)

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Optimizers in Deep Learning | Part 1", iCTTnQJn50E, 22:34. `transcripts/D032.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No StatQuest/3b1b/Khan video on momentum, AdaGrad, RMSProp or Adam (search "StatQuest Adam optimizer momentum": no hit). The nearest outside material, 3b1b ch2 05:30–06:30 (several valleys; steps proportional to slope), is mapped under Note 1017.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–04:00: training = optimization: find the 9 weights and biases that minimize the loss; a 10-D loss surface drawn as 3-D for two weights; start random, improve step by step.
2. ~04:00–08:00: recap of batch / stochastic / mini-batch (500 rows, 10 epochs → 10 / 5 000 / 50 updates with batch 100).
3. ~08:00–12:00: problem 1, the learning rate: Google's gradient-descent tool: 0.01 too slow ("taking too long to reach the minimum"), 0.5–1 unstable zig-zag, ~0.1 good.
4. ~12:00–16:00: problem 2, learning-rate schedules are fixed before training and do not adapt to the dataset.
5. ~16:00: problem 3, one learning rate for every direction ("walking down a hill at the same speed in every direction").
6. ~16:00–20:00: problems 4–5: local minima (SGD's zig-zag can escape, batch rarely) and saddle points (slope near 0 over a wide flat region → no update).
7. ~20:00: the plan: momentum, NAG, AdaGrad, RMSProp, Adam; all are small changes to gradient descent; next: EWMA.

**What the Note lacks**
- All five problems are in the Note. Missing visuals: a saddle-point surface (x² − y²) with its flat centre, and a one-learning-rate-for-all-directions contour picture (an elongated bowl). CampusX shows both with the Google tool and sketches.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly (3-D surface + path frames): plain gradient descent on z = x² − y² starting near the saddle: steps shrink to nothing; side panel the gradient norm on a log scale.
2. Plotly frames: plain gradient descent on an elongated bowl (scales 1 and 25) with one learning rate: zig-zag across, crawl along.
3. Plotly frames: learning rate 0.01 / 0.1 / 0.5 / 1.0 on the same 1-D parabola, four panels in sync.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions:** none.

### 1033 Exponentially weighted moving average

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Exponentially Weighted Moving Average or Exponential Weighted Average", jAqVuYJ8TP8, 18:51. `transcripts/D033.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No StatQuest/3b1b/Khan video (search "StatQuest exponentially weighted moving average" returns only maximum likelihood).

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–04:00: temperature by date: the plain mean (a flat red line) vs the EWMA (a black curve that follows the trend); uses: time-series forecasting, finance, signal processing, optimizers.
2. ~04:00: two rules: a newer point gets more weight than an older one; any point's weight shrinks as time passes.
3. ~04:00–08:00: Vₜ = βVₜ₋₁ + (1 − β)θₜ, β in 0–1; V₀ = 0 or θ₁; worked: β = 0.9, θ₁ = 13 → V₁ = 1.3; V₂ = 0.9 × 1.3 + 0.1 × 17.
4. ~08:00–12:00: "an average of the last 1/(1 − β) days": β = 0.9 → 10 days; 0.5 → 2 days; plots for four β values (0.98, 0.9, 0.5, 0.1). **Moody vs stable person analogy**: small β reacts to every day's mood, large β to how life went lately.
5. ~12:00–16:00: unroll V₄ = (1 − β)(β³θ₁ + β²θ₂ + βθ₃ + θ₄): older values multiply by higher powers of β.
6. ~16:00–18:00: pandas `ewm(alpha=…)` with α = 1 − β on Delhi daily climate; exercise: write it by hand.

**What the Note lacks**
- The moody/stable analogy (beat 4) is missing; the Note explains β by curves only.
- The Note covers the rest (V₀ choices, β effect, unrolled weights). An animation of the weights decaying backwards in time (bars βᵏ(1 − β)) would make "exponentially weighted" literal.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: Delhi daily temperature (the dataset CampusX uses; check `data/` size before adding) with the EWMA drawn day by day for β = 0.5, 0.9, 0.98 together.
2. Plotly frames: a bar chart of the weights on the last 30 days, (1 − β)βᵏ, as β slides from 0.5 to 0.98; the bars' total stays 1 − β³⁰.
3. Plotly frames: V₀ = 0 vs V₀ = θ₁: the first 20 days, showing the start-up dip (sets up Adam's bias correction in Note 1038).

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions:** none.

### 1034 SGD with momentum

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "SGD with Momentum Explained in Detail with Animations | Optimizers in Deep Learning Part 2", vVS4csXRlcQ, 38:25. `transcripts/D034.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video. 3b1b ch2 06:00 ("a ball rolling down a hill") gives the image but not momentum.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–08:00: three graphs: loss vs one weight (2-D), loss vs w and b (3-D), contour plot = the 3-D surface seen from above; a gallery of surfaces and their contours (pits, saddles, ridges); rings far apart = flat.
2. ~08:00–16:00: convex vs non-convex; three troubles: local minima, saddle points (slope changes slowly over a wide area), high curvature.
3. ~16:00–20:00: animations of batch (smooth), stochastic (erratic, leaves the curve), mini-batch (between) on a convex bowl; momentum targets high curvature, consistent small gradients, noisy gradients.
4. ~20:00–24:00: **car analogy**: driving from A to B without knowing the way; four people in a row point the same direction → you speed up; if two say forward and two say back → you go slowly. Physics: a ball gathering momentum downhill.
5. ~24:00–28:00: vₜ = βvₜ₋₁ + η∇L(wₜ), wₜ₊₁ = wₜ − vₜ; contour picture: SGD zig-zags vertically; momentum damps the vertical and speeds the horizontal.
6. ~28:00–32:00: β: 0 → plain SGD; ≈ average of the last 1/(1 − β) velocities (0.9 → 10); β = 1 → no decay, the ball circles forever ("dynamic equilibrium"); usual 0.5–0.99.
7. ~32:00–36:00: two-ball animation on a surface with a small and a big pit: plain GD gets stuck in the small one; momentum escapes and reaches the big one, but **overshoots and oscillates** before settling. "The biggest problem of momentum is momentum."
8. ~36:00: web tool (two minima): momentum's path from several starts.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beat 4 (car asking for directions) is missing; it is the plain-words picture for "consistent past gradients → speed".
- Beat 7 (two balls: escaping a small pit, then overshooting) should be checked against the Note's figures: the Note's section list covers the zig-zag fading but not the escape-from-a-local-minimum demo.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: two balls on a 1-D curve with a shallow local pit and a deep global pit (a quartic): GD stops in the pit; momentum (β = 0.9) rolls through, overshoots the global minimum and settles.
2. Plotly frames: contour of an elongated bowl; SGD vs momentum paths step by step; a side panel with the velocity vector's two components (vertical shrinking, horizontal growing).
3. Plotly frames: β = 0, 0.5, 0.9, 0.99 on the same start: from plain GD to wild oscillation.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source. CampusX's car analogy and two-ball animation are its strongest material.

**Contradictions:** none found. (CampusX's velocity form vₜ = βvₜ₋₁ + η∇L matches the Note's; some books put (1 − β) in front of the gradient instead; the Note should say which convention Keras uses: Keras `SGD(momentum=…)` uses v = βv − η∇L, the same up to sign.)

### 1035 Nesterov accelerated gradient

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Nesterov Accelerated Gradient (NAG) Explained in Detail | Animations", rKG9E6rce1c, 27:50. `transcripts/D035.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–04:00: animation on a linear-regression loss (m, b): batch GD needs ~25–30 epochs; momentum β = 0.9 reaches the minimum's neighbourhood in ~3 epochs but oscillates, ~50 epochs to settle; β = 0.8 → ~30; β = 0.5 → ~10.
2. ~04:00–08:00: momentum's problem is oscillation; NAG with the same β oscillates much less.
3. ~08:00–12:00: momentum's step = two pushes computed at the same point: βvₜ₋₁ (history) + η∇L(wₜ) (slope here).
4. ~12:00–16:00: NAG: first take the momentum jump to the **look-ahead** point w_la = wₜ − βvₜ₋₁, compute the gradient there, then correct: vₜ = βvₜ₋₁ + η∇L(w_la), wₜ₊₁ = wₜ − vₜ.
5. ~16:00–24:00: 1-D picture: momentum passes the minimum, U-turns, smaller U-turns; NAG: at the look-ahead point past the minimum the slope already points back, so it brakes one step early: "you already know what is going to happen in the future".
6. ~24:00: weakness: the damping also removes the push that lets momentum roll out of a small local minimum.
7. ~24:00–27:00: Keras: `SGD(momentum=0, nesterov=False)` = SGD; `momentum=0.9` = momentum; `momentum=0.9, nesterov=True` = NAG.

**What the Note lacks**
- All beats present. The β-sweep (0.9, 0.8, 0.5) of beat 1 is a useful animation missing from 1034/1035.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: 1-D parabola; at one step show three arrows: the momentum jump, the gradient at the current point (momentum) and at the look-ahead point (NAG); then the resulting step for each.
2. Plotly frames: the linear-regression (m, b) contour with momentum and NAG paths side by side, β = 0.9, one frame per epoch (CampusX's setup, rebuilt on the Note's students data).

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions:** none.

### 1036 AdaGrad

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "AdaGrad Explained in Detail with Animations | Optimizers in Deep Learning Part 4", nqL9xYmhEpg, 26:29. `transcripts/D036.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–04:00: Adaptive Gradient: the learning rate adapts per parameter. Two use cases: features on very different scales (CGPA vs salary; usually fixed by scaling instead) and **sparse features**: IQ, CGPA, "from IIT" (mostly 0, but important) → package.
2. ~04:00–08:00: a sparse feature makes an **elongated bowl** (loss over m and b): circular contours for a normal x, stretched for a sparse x.
3. ~08:00–12:00: batch GD first slides along b, then crawls along m; momentum overshoots along b, then turns; a direct diagonal path would be shorter.
4. ~12:00–16:00: why: one neuron, ∂L/∂m = Σ −2(y − ŷ)·x is 0 on every row where x = 0, so m's gradient is small; ∂L/∂b uses 1 on every row, so it is large; the step is the vector sum, so it goes mostly along b.
5. ~16:00–20:00: the fix: a different learning rate per parameter: big gradients → smaller rate, small gradients → bigger rate, so the two updates are comparable.
6. ~20:00–24:00: wₜ₊₁ = wₜ − η·∇/√(vₜ + ε), vₜ = vₜ₋₁ + (∇)²; ε only avoids division by 0; squares keep magnitude; animation: AdaGrad goes straight down the diagonal.
7. ~24:00: disadvantage: vₜ only grows, so η/√vₜ shrinks until updates stop near, not at, the minimum; rarely used for deep networks; its idea is reused by RMSProp and Adam.

**What the Note lacks**
- All beats present; the IIT example and elongated-bowl figures are in the Note (section 4.2 uses "error × IIT"). Nothing to add.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: contour of the Note's sparse-feature loss; GD, momentum and AdaGrad paths drawn together, one frame per epoch.
2. Plotly frames: the per-parameter learning rate η/√vₜ for m and b over epochs (two lines): b's rate drops fast, m's stays larger.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX: AdaGrad "can never converge to the solution no matter how many epochs" (~24:00). Too strong: on convex problems AdaGrad has convergence guarantees (Duchi et al. 2011), and CampusX's own next video says AdaGrad "works perfectly in convex optimization problems". The Note's wording (section 5 of 1037: "on a simple convex bowl, AdaGrad can do fine"; the dying rate hurts in non-convex deep networks) is **right**.

### 1037 RMSProp

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "RMSProp Explained in Detail with Animations | Optimizers in Deep Learning Part 5", p0wSmKslWi0, 12:38. `transcripts/D037.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–04:00: recap: AdaGrad fixes the elongated bowl but its learning rate decays too far, so it stops short.
2. ~04:00: why: vₜ sums every past squared gradient; b (non-sparse) builds a huge vₜ, so b can no longer move.
3. ~04:00–08:00: fix: vₜ = βvₜ₋₁ + (1 − β)(∇)², an exponentially decaying average (CampusX: β ≈ 0.95); unrolled: v₃ weights epoch 3 most and epoch 1 least.
4. ~08:00–12:00: on the convex linear-regression demo AdaGrad and RMSProp look alike; the difference shows on non-convex neural-network losses; "before Adam, RMSProp was the default; still competes with Adam".

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing; the Note adds the stack-of-blocks figure (AdaGrad keeps every block, RMSProp lets old blocks fade), a worked example (9, 4, 1 → 0.9, 1.21, 1.189) and an MNIST comparison (AdaGrad 0.24 vs RMSProp 0.0001 after 30 epochs). It is ahead of CampusX.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the Note's stack-of-blocks figure animated step by step (blocks pile up for AdaGrad; shrink and drop off for RMSProp).
2. Plotly frames: MNIST training-loss curves of AdaGrad and RMSProp drawn epoch by epoch (Note's own runs).

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX: β "generally taken to be 0.95" (~04:00). Hinton's lecture 6e suggests 0.9 and Keras `RMSprop(rho=0.9)` uses 0.9. **The Note is right** (β usually 0.9, Hinton 2012).
- CampusX: RMSProp "has no disadvantages" (~08:00–12:00). It still needs a learning rate and has no momentum term; the Note's section 6 ("strengths and limits") is the safer wording.

### 1038 Adam

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Adam Optimizer Explained in Detail with Animations | Optimizers in Deep Learning Part 5", N5AynalXD9g, 12:39. `transcripts/D038.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–04:00: Adaptive Moment Estimation; the story so far with animations: batch GD (calm, slow) → momentum (fast, oscillates) → NAG (smaller circles) → AdaGrad (straight down a sparse bowl, stops short) → RMSProp (reaches the minimum).
2. ~04:00: two ideas: momentum (momentum, NAG) and learning-rate decay (AdaGrad, RMSProp); Adam merges them.
3. ~04:00–08:00: mₜ = β₁mₜ₋₁ + (1 − β₁)∇; vₜ = β₂vₜ₋₁ + (1 − β₂)∇²; bias correction m̂ = m/(1 − β₁ᵗ), v̂ = v/(1 − β₂ᵗ) because m₀ = v₀ = 0; wₜ₊₁ = wₜ − η·m̂/(√v̂ + ε).
4. ~08:00–12:00: animation on sparse data: Adam goes down the middle and curls like momentum; for non-convex networks it shines.
5. ~12:00: advice: start with Adam; if unhappy try RMSProp, sometimes momentum; tune.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing in content; the Note has the bias-correction table (1 − 0.999ᵗ = 0.001, 0.010, 0.095, 0.632) and a worked two-step example, plus the students-data and MNIST comparisons.
- One possible addition: a "family tree" figure of the six optimizers (two ideas merging into Adam), which is CampusX's organising picture (beat 2).

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: the family tree: momentum → NAG on one branch, AdaGrad → RMSProp on the other, merging into Adam; each node shows its update rule, the shared terms highlighted.
2. Plotly frames: m̂ₜ and v̂ₜ vs raw mₜ and vₜ for the first 50 steps of a constant gradient 2 (the Note's example), showing the correction lifting the early values.
3. Plotly frames: all six optimizers racing on one contour (the Note's students data), one frame per epoch.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX: β₂ "generally 0.99" (~08:00). The Adam paper and Keras use β₂ = 0.999 (Kingma and Ba 2015, Algorithm 1). **The Note is right.**
- CampusX: "start with a learning rate of 0.1 or 0.01" (~08:00). The paper's default is 0.001 (Keras default 0.001). **The Note is right.**
- CampusX says the step divides "by Vt plus epsilon" (~04:00), dropping the square root; the formula on screen and in the Note has √v̂. Do not copy the spoken version.

### 1039 Keras Tuner

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Keras Tuner | Hyperparameter Tuning a Neural Network", oYnyNLj8RMA, 65:34. `transcripts/D039.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video (StatQuest has no Keras Tuner video; its PyTorch videos were not opened).

**Teaching path** (CampusX, code-first)
1. ~00:00–05:00: the problem: layers, nodes, activation, batch size, optimizer are all guesses. Pima diabetes data (768 patients, 8 features, 23 KB); correlations with the outcome.
2. ~05:00–10:00: hand-made baseline: Dense(32, relu) → Dense(1, sigmoid), Adam, 100 epochs → validation accuracy ~0.81.
3. ~10:00–25:00: three steps: `build_model(hp)` with `hp.Choice('optimizer', values=[…])`; `kt.RandomSearch(build_model, objective='val_accuracy', max_trials=5)`; `tuner.search(…)`. Results: Adadelta 0.53, Adam 0.75, SGD 0.74, RMSProp 0.79; `get_best_hyperparameters()[0].values`; `get_best_models(num_models=1)[0]`; continue training with `initial_epoch`.
4. ~25:00–40:00: units: `hp.Int('units', 8, 128, step=8)`; directory and project name (trial JSON files); best 120, then 72 with step 1.
5. ~40:00–50:00: number of layers: `for i in range(hp.Int('num_layers', 1, 10))`; best 6.
6. ~50:00–65:00: all together: per-layer units and activation (`'units' + str(i)`), optimizer, and dropout per layer (0.1–0.9); the 7-layer winner overfits; with dropout it stops overfitting (~0.77).

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing in the code path. The Note fixes CampusX's leakage (below) with a train/validation/test split and reports its own numbers (baseline 0.760; RMSProp 0.747; Adadelta 0.338).
- Missing visual: the search itself, e.g. a parallel-coordinates or scatter plot of trials (units vs layers coloured by validation accuracy).

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: one frame per trial of the Note's full search: a dot appears at (layers, units) with colour = validation accuracy; the best-so-far is ringed.
2. Plotly frames: validation curves of the top 3 trials drawn epoch by epoch, plus the baseline.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX passes the **test set** as `validation_data` to `tuner.search` and then reports accuracy on the same set (~20:00, ~35:00, ~60:00). The chosen hyperparameters are then fitted to the test set, so the reported score is optimistic (Goodfellow et al. 2016, §5.3: "the validation set is used to tune hyperparameters… the test set is used only once"). **The Note is right** (section 4.2: separate validation and test sets).

### 1040 CNN intuition

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "What is Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) | CNN Intution", hDVFXf74P-U, 27:10. `transcripts/D040.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "Neural Networks Part 8: Image Classification with Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs)", HGwBXDKFk9I, 15:23. `transcripts/teaching-video/1040-statquest-cnn.txt`.
3. 3Blue1Brown ch1 (aircAruvnKk) 05:30–07:30 (digit = loop + line; edges → parts) and ch2 (IHZwWFHWa-w) 14:00–15:30 (a plain MLP's hidden weights do **not** look like edges).

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–04:00: definition: networks for grid-like data (1-D time series, 2-D images); a CNN has convolution, pooling and fully connected layers; history: LeCun's cheque reader (1998), Microsoft OCR, face recognition, self-driving.
2. StatQuest 00:30–02:00: the tiny task: tic-tac-toe X vs O on a 6 × 6 grid of 0/1 pixels.
3. StatQuest 02:00–04:00 + CampusX ~04:00–12:00: why not an ANN: (a) weights explode: 6 × 6 → 36 per hidden node; 100 × 100 → 10 000 (StatQuest); 40 × 40 with 100 hidden → 160 000; 1 000 × 1 000 with 500 hidden (CampusX); (b) overfitting; (c) flattening loses the 2-D arrangement (distance between a monkey's eyes and nose); StatQuest adds (d) **a 1-pixel shift** may break the ANN, and (e) neighbouring pixels are correlated (teddy-bear browns sit together).
4. StatQuest 04:00–04:30: a CNN does three things: fewer inputs, tolerates small shifts, uses local correlation.
5. CampusX ~12:00–16:00: how we read a 9: a circle + a vertical line (+ a short horizontal), whatever the handwriting; a CNN finds edges, then semicircles, then the circle, then the 9; for a cat: edges → ears/eyes → face → cat.
6. StatQuest 05:00–09:30: the whole pipeline on the O: a 3 × 3 filter (values learned by backprop) overlaid, multiply-and-add = 3 (a dot product), + bias → feature map; slide by 1; ReLU; 2 × 2 max pooling "selects the spot where the filter matched best"; flatten 4 values → small dense net → O = 1, X = 0.
7. StatQuest 12:30–13:30: payoffs: 36 inputs → 4; shift the X one pixel right → output X = 1.23 vs O = −0.2: still X.
8. CampusX ~16:00–24:00: applications: classification, localisation, detection, face recognition, segmentation, super-resolution, colourisation, pose estimation; course plan.

**What the Note lacks**
- StatQuest's end-to-end toy CNN (beat 6) is missing: the Note explains the intuition and the ANN problems with its own figures (`digit_grid.png`, `flatten.png`, the scrambled-pixels experiment), but no single picture follows one image through filter → feature map → ReLU → pool → dense → answer. That one figure is the map for Notes 1042–1045.
- The 1-pixel shift test (beat 7) is missing here; Note 1044 discusses shift in words only (section 3.2).

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: StatQuest's 6 × 6 O/X: the 3 × 3 filter slides, the feature map fills, ReLU zeroes negatives, 2 × 2 max pool picks four numbers, they drop into a 4-1-2 dense net, the O/X bars light up. Then the X shifted one pixel: the same numbers mostly survive pooling.
2. Plotly frames: the Note's scrambled-pixels experiment as a two-panel animation: original vs permuted digits, ANN accuracy identical (shows the ANN ignores 2-D layout), then a small CNN's accuracy drops on the permuted ones.
3. Plotly frames: weight count vs image side (28 → 1 000 pixels) for one dense layer of 100 nodes vs one conv layer of 32 3 × 3 filters (log y).

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why CNNs:** StatQuest is clearer and adds two reasons CampusX lacks (shift tolerance, local correlation) with a worked toy example. CampusX's three reasons (cost, overfitting, lost layout) are all valid and come with bigger, realistic numbers. Merge both lists.
- **How a CNN builds features:** CampusX's "9 = circle + lines" and "cat = edges → ears → face" is the clearer beginner story; StatQuest shows the mechanics. 3b1b ch2 adds a caution: a *plain MLP* does not learn edges; that is a reason to use convolution.
- **CampusX's new angles:** the application gallery; the monkey-face distance example.
- No clash.

**Contradictions:** none.

### 1041 CNN vs visual cortex

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "CNN Vs Visual Cortex | The Famous Cat Experiment | History of CNN", aslTGS9ef98, 15:02. `transcripts/D041.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No StatQuest/3b1b video; Khan Academy has biology videos on neurons ("Anatomy of a neuron", ob5U8zPbAX4; "Overview of neuron structure", 1h4kW8RX-6k, seen in search results, not opened) but none on Hubel and Wiesel or V1. Not used.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–04:00: the visual pathway: retina (a 2-D sheet) → optic nerve → lateral geniculate nucleus in the thalamus (pre-processing) → primary visual cortex V1 → deeper areas; shown on a diagram and a real brain section.
2. ~04:00–08:00: Hubel and Wiesel (around 1960): an anaesthetised cat, an electrode on one cell, bars on a screen; a cell responds only when the bar reaches one orientation (vertical) and fades as it rotates away; other cells prefer horizontal or slanted bars.
3. ~08:00–12:00: simple cells: small receptive field, one preferred orientation, edge detectors ("feature detectors"); complex cells: larger receptive field, combine simple-cell outputs into higher features (edges → a hexagon).
4. ~12:00–15:00: Fukushima's Neocognitron (S and C cells) → LeCun's CNN with backprop for cheques (1990s) → AlexNet wins ImageNet (2012).

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing; the Note has the pathway figure, the timeline, and a section mapping simple cells → filter and complex cells → max. The rotating-bar response curve (beat 2) would be clearer animated than described.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: a bar rotating 0° → 180° on the left; on the right a "firing rate" curve drawn as it rotates, peaking at 90° (a cosine-shaped tuning curve; label it as an illustration, not Hubel and Wiesel's data).
2. Manim: a vertical-edge 3 × 3 filter applied to a rotating bar image; its maximum response traced against angle: the same tuning shape, linking the cell to the filter.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source.

**Contradictions:** none found. (CampusX dates the experiment "around 1960s"; Hubel and Wiesel 1959 and 1962. The Note's timeline should carry the exact years.)

### 1042 Convolution operation

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "CNN Part 3 | Convolution Operation", cgJx3GvQ5y8, 29:14. `transcripts/D042.whisper-en.txt`.
2. 3Blue1Brown, "But what is a convolution?", KuXjwB4LzSA, 23:01. `transcripts/teaching-video/1042-3b1b-convolution.txt` (00:00–01:30, 05:30–13:30 read; the probability and FFT parts are not needed).
3. StatQuest CNN (HGwBXDKFk9I) 05:00–07:00: the 3 × 3 filter, dot product = 3, + bias, slide by 1, feature map.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–04:00: recap: early layers find edges, later layers combine them (face: edges → eyes, nose → face).
2. CampusX ~04:00–08:00: greyscale = one 28 × 28 grid of 0–255 (or 0–1); RGB = three stacked channels (height × width × 3).
3. 3b1b 05:30–07:30: **1-D first**: [1, 2, 3] * [4, 5, 6]: flip one list, slide, multiply aligned pairs, add: 4, 13, 28, 27, 18. Then a moving average: a window of five 1/5s smooths a noisy list. Why it clicks: the sliding-window idea is seen on a list before a grid.
4. 3b1b 08:30–10:30: the 2-D version: a 3 × 3 grid of 1/9 → blur (each pixel averaged with its neighbours); a 5 × 5 Gaussian grid → a softer, lens-like blur.
5. 3b1b 10:30–12:30: a kernel with positive values on the left and negative on the right → **vertical-edge detector**: zero on flat patches (the kernel sums to 0), large where brightness changes left to right; rotate it → horizontal edges. "In a CNN the data decide what the kernels should be."
6. CampusX ~08:00–16:00: edges = changes in intensity; the 6 × 6 image (top half 0, bottom half 255) with a 3 × 3 horizontal-edge filter (−1 row, 0 row, +1 row): slide, multiply cell by cell, add; feature map 0 / large / large / 0 (4 × 4). Vertical-edge filter by transposing.
7. CampusX ~16:00–20:00: filters are not hand-made in a CNN; they start random and are learned by backprop like ANN weights. TensorSpace-style web demo on MNIST (left/right/top/bottom edge filters): red = positive (left edge), blue = negative (right edge); ReLU keeps only the red.
8. CampusX ~20:00–24:00: output size n − f + 1 (6 → 4; 28 → 26; 64 → 62).
9. CampusX ~24:00–29:00: RGB: the filter is 3 × 3 × 3 (27 numbers) and gives a **single-channel** map; k filters → k channels (4 × 4 × 10 for 10 filters).
10. 3b1b 09:30–10:00, 13:00–13:30: strictly, convolution flips the kernel 180°; CS practice often does not.

**What the Note lacks**
- Beats 3–4 (1-D sliding window, blur as averaging) are missing. They give the beginner a reason the operation is useful before the edge example. The Note jumps from "edges are intensity changes" to the 2-D edge filter.
- Beat 5's "a kernel that sums to 0 gives 0 on flat regions" is the one-line reason the edge filter works; the Note shows the arithmetic but does not state the rule.
- The Note already has the cross-correlation Extra (matching beat 10), the sliding GIF, the real-photo edge figure, and RGB/multi-filter shapes.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: 3b1b's 1-D example: [1, 2, 3] and flipped [6, 5, 4] sliding; each output 4, 13, 28, 27, 18 appears as the windows align.
2. Plotly frames: a real greyscale photo (the Note's railing photo) convolved with: 1/9 box blur, Gaussian 5 × 5, vertical edge, horizontal edge; one kernel per frame with the kernel drawn beside the result.
3. Manim: a 3 × 3 × 3 filter sliding through an RGB volume, summing 27 products to one cell of a single-channel map; then a second filter adds a second map behind it.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **What convolution is:** 3b1b is clearer for the idea (1-D, then blur, then edges); CampusX is clearer for the CNN mechanics (exact 6 × 6 example, output size, RGB depth, number of filters = number of channels). Main path: CampusX, opened by 3b1b's blur and edge pictures.
- **CampusX's new angles:** red/blue = left/right edge and ReLU keeping one sign; the RGB filter depth rule.
- **3b1b's new angles:** the 180° flip (true convolution) vs what CNNs compute; kernels summing to 1 (blur) vs 0 (edges).
- **Clash (flip):** CampusX calls the operation convolution with no flip; 3b1b says true convolution flips the kernel. Both describe correctly what each field does; the Note's Extra (deep-learning "convolution" is cross-correlation; Goodfellow et al. 2016 §9.1) resolves it.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX: in the 6 × 6 example the value is 255 because "the biggest number here can be 255" (~12:00). The sum of three 255 × 1 products is **765**; nothing clips it (no activation has been applied yet). **The Note is right** (section 5.2: 765).

### 1043 Padding and strides

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Padding & Strides in CNN | CNN Lecture 4", btWE6SsdDZA, 24:26. `transcripts/D043.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest CNN (HGwBXDKFk9I) 06:00–06:30: "in this example we slide the filter over 1 pixel; other CNNs might move over two or more" (the only outside mention). No 3b1b/Khan coverage.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
1. ~00:00–04:00: two problems of plain convolution: the map shrinks (5 × 5 → 3 × 3; repeated layers shrink more, losing information); border pixels take part in fewer convolutions than centre pixels, so they count less.
2. ~04:00–08:00: want n − f + 1 = n → pad: one ring of zeros turns 5 × 5 into 7 × 7, and a 3 × 3 filter gives 5 × 5; formula n + 2p − f + 1; Keras `padding="valid"` (none) vs `"same"` (keep the size).
3. ~08:00–12:00: Keras demo on MNIST, three Conv2D(32, 3 × 3): valid → 26, 24, 22; same → 28, 28, 28.
4. ~12:00–16:00: strides: 7 × 7 image, 3 × 3 filter; stride 1 moves one pixel; stride 2 jumps two; size ⌊(n + 2p − f)/s⌋ + 1: (7 − 3)/2 + 1 = 3; with p = 1: (7 + 2 − 3)/2 + 1 = 4.
5. ~16:00: special case 6 × 7 image, stride 2: (6 − 3)/2 = 1.5 → floor → 2 rows, 3 columns: the last window does not fit and is dropped.
6. ~16:00–20:00: why strides: keep only high-level features; save compute (less important now).
7. ~20:00–24:00: Keras `strides=(2, 2)` with same padding: 28 → 14 → 7 → 4; check 14 = ⌊(28 + 2 − 3)/2⌋ + 1.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing in content; the Note has the padded sliding GIF and the "how often each pixel is covered" heat map (5 × 5: corner once, centre 9 times), which beats CampusX's verbal version.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: coverage count per pixel for a 3 × 3 filter on a 7 × 7 image with p = 0, 1 and s = 1, 2 (four heat maps morphing).
2. Manim: the 6 × 7, stride-2 special case: the window steps, the last step does not fit, the floor in the formula lights up.

**CampusX vs the others:** no outside source beyond StatQuest's one line.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX states the stride formula as "N + 2P − F divided by 2 plus 1" (~12:00), saying 2 where s belongs; the worked numbers use s correctly. The Note uses s. Do not copy the spoken slip.

### 1044 Pooling

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Pooling Layer in CNN | MaxPooling in Convolutional Neural Network", DwmGefkowCU, 27:54. `transcripts/D044.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest CNN (HGwBXDKFk9I) 07:30–09:30 and 12:30–13:30: max pooling "selects the spot where the filter did the best job matching the input"; average pooling as the alternative; a 1-pixel shift of the X still gives "X" (1.23 vs −0.2).

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–04:00: two problems after convolution: **memory** (an RGB image through 100 filters of 3 × 3 × 3 → 100 maps; ~19 MB per image at 32-bit, ~1.5 GB for a batch of 100) and **translation variance** (a cat's ear on the left lights the left of the map; for classification only "is there an ear" matters).
2. CampusX ~04:00–08:00: pooling = down-sampling after conv + ReLU; three settings: window size (2 × 2), stride (2), type (max, min, average, L2, global).
3. CampusX ~08:00–12:00: worked 4 × 4 map → 2 × 2 (maxima 5, 3, 7, 4); intuition: keep the most dominant feature in each small receptive field, drop low-level detail.
4. StatQuest 08:00–09:30: why max: the largest value marks where the filter matched best (the upper-left corner of the O).
5. CampusX ~12:00–16:00: deeplizard web demo on MNIST; volumes: pool each map separately (4 × 4 × 2 → 2 × 2 × 2); Keras `MaxPooling2D(pool_size, strides)`: 26 → 13, 11 → 5; 0 trainable parameters.
6. CampusX ~16:00–20:00: four advantages: smaller maps; translation invariance (two slightly shifted 8s give the same pooled map); enhanced (brighter) features (max only); no training.
7. StatQuest 12:30–13:30: the shifted-X test (numbers above).
8. CampusX ~20:00–24:00: average pooling; global max/average pooling: one number per map (4 × 4 × 3 → 3); global average pooling as a replacement for Flatten, to reduce overfitting.
9. CampusX ~24:00–27:00: disadvantages: location is lost (bad for segmentation); 75% of the values are discarded; some papers drop pooling entirely.

**What the Note lacks**
- The shifted-digit demonstration (beats 6–7: shift an 8 or an X by one pixel; the pooled maps match) is described but not drawn. It is the one picture that proves "translation invariance" (the Note's Extra correctly says *approximate*, local invariance; Goodfellow §9.3).
- Global pooling (beat 8) and the "drop pooling" caveat (beat 9) should be checked against the Note's later sections (not in the outline excerpt above).

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: an MNIST 8 shifted 0, 1, 2, 3 pixels right; row 1 the conv feature map (shifts with it), row 2 the 2 × 2 max-pooled map (changes little for 1-pixel shifts, more for 2–3): makes "local" invariance visible.
2. Plotly frames: the 4 × 4 → 2 × 2 worked example with the 2 × 2 window stepping and the max lighting up (the Note's `maxpool_slide.gif` exists; add average pooling as a second row).
3. Plotly bars: memory per image and per batch for conv output vs after 2 × 2 pooling (the Note's 19.7 MB / 1.97 GB numbers and their quarters).

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why pooling:** CampusX is clearer and broader (memory with numbers, translation, advantages and disadvantages, global pooling). StatQuest's "keeps where the filter matched best" is the cleanest one-line meaning of the max; use it as the definition's plain words.
- No clash.

**Contradictions**
- CampusX: "228 × 228 is the common input size of good CNN architectures" (~00:00). The common size is **224 × 224** (AlexNet, VGG, ResNet; CS231n). **The Note is right** (224, citing CS231n).
- CampusX: ~1.5 GB for a batch of 100 (~00:00); 226 × 226 × 100 × 4 bytes × 100 ≈ 2.0 GB. **The Note is right** (1.97 GB for 222 × 222 maps).

### 1045 LeNet-5

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "CNN Architecture | LeNet -5 Architecture", ewsvsJQOuTI, 20:00. `transcripts/D045.whisper-en.txt`.
2. No outside video.

**Teaching path** (CampusX)
