1. CampusX, "What is Self Attention | Transformers Part 2", XnGGmvpDLA0, 23:21. `transcripts/D072.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest, "Word Embedding and Word2Vec" (viZrOnJclY0), 02:00–04:00 (random numbers vs learned numbers; one word may need several numbers for positive vs sarcastic "great") and StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 12:30–14:00 ("the pizza came out of the oven and it tasted good": "it" must be linked to pizza).
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 5 15:09–17:44 and Ch 6 01:32: mapped in `docs/3b1b-transformers.md` §4 ("Directions carry meaning", "Vectors soak in context") and §6 item 3.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–08:00: every NLP application first turns words into numbers: one-hot ("mat cat mat", "cat rat rat"), bag of words, TF-IDF, then **word embeddings**, which capture meaning.
2. CampusX ~08:00: embeddings are trained on a big corpus; each word becomes an n-d vector; similar words have similar vectors (king, queen vs cricketer); CampusX's illustration labels dimensions (royalty, athletics, human).
3. 3b1b Ch 5 15:09–17:44 (from the doc): meaning lives in **directions** (woman − man; plural), read by dot products, not in single labelled coordinates.
4. CampusX ~08:00–16:00: **static embeddings hold an average meaning**: "apple" trained on 9 000 fruit sentences and 1 000 company sentences gets a vector tilted to taste; flip the counts and it tilts to technology (a 2-d taste/technology picture).
5. CampusX ~16:00–20:00: in "Apple launched a new phone while I was eating an orange" the static vector is wrong for this sentence; we need **contextual** embeddings that shift with the words around (launched, phone), without being fooled by "orange".
6. StatQuest Transformer 12:30–14:00: the same need for relations: "it" ↔ "pizza", not "oven".
7. CampusX ~20:00–23:00: **self-attention = a function: static embeddings in, contextual embeddings out**, one output per input word.

**What the Note lacks**
- The Note has the average-meaning calculation (section 4.1) and a real-text demonstration (4.2) and the static-vs-contextual comparison: it is ahead of CampusX.
- The 3b1b doc flags that section 3's per-coordinate example ("first number = royalty") points slightly the wrong way; Note 1086 now owns "meaning as direction". 1072 §3 should recap with a direction picture and link 1086 (as the doc recommends).

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the Note's "apple" example on the taste/technology plane: the static vector drawn as the weighted average of fruit and company uses, sliding as the 9 000/1 000 split changes.
2. Manim: "Apple launched a new phone": the static apple arrow, then arrows from "launched" and "phone" pull it towards the technology direction (preview of 1073/1075).

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why contextual embeddings:** CampusX's apple-average argument is the clearest beginner motivation; StatQuest and 3b1b give one-line versions ("it"/pizza; "bank").
- **Clash (what an embedding dimension means):** CampusX labels individual coordinates (royalty, athletics, human) and says "every dimension has a meaning". 3b1b: meaning is carried by directions, and individual coordinates are generally not interpretable. CampusX itself adds "we can't understand because the network doesn't tell us". The 3b1b doc (§6 item 3, §7) judges the per-coordinate picture as pointing slightly the wrong way; **3b1b's direction view is the standard one** (Mikolov et al. 2013 analogies are directions). Keep CampusX's picture only with an explicit "illustration" label, or replace it with directions.

**Contradictions:** the dimension-labelling point above (the Note already carries CampusX's caveat; see the 3b1b doc for the fix).

### 1073 Self-attention step by step

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Self Attention in Transformers | Simple Explanation with Code!", -tCKPl_8Xb8, 83:24. `transcripts/D073.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 14:30–21:00 (query, keys, dot products 11.7 and −2.6, softmax, values; shared weights; parallel) and StatQuest "The matrix math behind transformer neural networks, one step at a time!!!" (KphmOJnLAdI, 23:42; `transcripts/teaching-video/1073-statquest-transformer-matrix-math.txt`) 04:30–12:00 (Q = XW_Q, QKᵀ, scale, row softmax, × V, residual, all as matrices with numbers).
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 6: mapped in the 3b1b doc (§4 rows "Q/K as question and answer", "Attention pattern grid", "Value = what to add"; §6 items 1 and 4).

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–10:00: recap: static → contextual; toy sentences "money bank grows" and "river bank flows"; idea: write each word as a mix of the sentence's words: bank = 0.25·money + 0.7·bank + 0.05·grows in one sentence, a different mix in the other.
2. CampusX ~10:00–20:00: replace words by embeddings; the mixing weights are **similarities = dot products** (e.g. (6, 1)·(4, 2) = 26 vs (6, 1)·(1, 5) = 11); normalise with softmax; weighted sum → new embedding; draw it for all three words.
3. CampusX ~20:00–30:00: (a) everything is **parallel**: E Eᵀ → softmax → × E as three matrix operations (3 × n), any length; but order is lost (fixed later by positional encoding). (b) **no learnable parameters**: the result is general, not task-specific ("piece of cake" → literal; "break a leg").
4. CampusX ~30:00–50:00: each embedding plays three roles: **query** (asks), **key** (answers), **value** (is mixed in), like a Python dict lookup d['a'].
5. CampusX ~50:00–62:00: **matrimonial-site analogy**: an author's autobiography (the embedding) should not be pasted whole as his search (query), his profile (key) and his first conversation (value); each needs its own version, tuned by experience (data).
6. 3b1b Ch 6 06:22–09:32 (from the doc): the adjective→noun toy: a noun's query asks "any adjectives before me?", an adjective's key answers; a big dot product = a match.
7. CampusX ~62:00–74:00: make q, k, v by **linear transformations**: Wq, Wk, Wv, initialised randomly and learned by backprop; the same three matrices for every word.
8. StatQuest Transformer 14:30–19:00 + matrix-math video 04:30–12:00: the same with numbers: query of "let's" · key of "let's" = 11.7, · key of "go" = −2.6 → softmax 100% / 0% → values; then as matrices Q = XW_Qᵀ, QKᵀ (−4.49, 2.05, −27.4 …), scale, row softmax (SOS: 1% / 99% / 0%), × V; plus the residual.
9. CampusX ~74:00–83:00: the full matrix pipeline: E → Q, K, V → QKᵀ → softmax → × V → contextual embeddings; works for 3 or 3 000 words in parallel.

**What the Note lacks**
- The Note has the mixing idea, dot-product similarity, softmax, matrices and the "nothing to learn" problem, plus the dictionary and matrimonial analogies. Per the 3b1b doc (§6 item 4): add 3b1b's adjective→noun Q/K toy and redraw the weight figure as a dot-size grid.
- StatQuest's numeric matrix walk-through (beat 8) is a ready-made worked example with every intermediate number; one 3-token example with numbers would help the Note's section 5 (matrices).

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: "money bank grows": three embedding arrows; for "bank", three dot products, softmax bars, then the weighted arrows adding up to the new bank arrow; repeat for "river bank flows" with a different result.
2. Plotly frames: StatQuest's 3-token matrices: X → Q, K, V → QKᵀ grid fills → scaled → row softmax (colours) → × V, one operation per frame.
3. Plotly dot-size grid (the doc's proposal) of real attention weights from the Note's figure.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Building self-attention:** CampusX's "invent it from first principles" route (mix → dot product → softmax → no parameters → three roles → learned projections) is the clearest *why*; StatQuest gives the clearest *numbers*; 3b1b gives the clearest *meaning of Q and K*. Keep all three: CampusX main, StatQuest numbers as the worked example, 3b1b toy for Q/K.
- **CampusX's new angles:** "piece of cake"/"break a leg" for task-specific embeddings; the matrimonial analogy; the dictionary analogy.
- **3b1b's angle:** attention output as an added change Δe (doc §6 item 1), not a replacement.
- **Clash (convention):** 3b1b puts queries in columns and softmaxes per column; CampusX, StatQuest and Vaswani put queries in rows (doc §7 item 3). Keep rows.

**Contradictions:** none beyond the convention note.

### 1074 Scaled dot-product attention

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Scaled Dot Product Attention | Why do we scale Self Attention?", r7mAt0iVqwo, 50:42. `transcripts/D074.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest matrix-math video (KphmOJnLAdI) 09:00–10:00: scale QKᵀ by √2; "scaling by just the square root of the number of values per token doesn't scale the dot products in any systematic way"; range still 5.01 to −66.2; "the original authors said it improved performance". StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 35:00: divided by √(number of embedding values per token), "helped encode and decode long and complicated phrases".
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 6 10:35 ("for numerical stability"): in the 3b1b doc §7 item 1.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–10:00: recap of softmax(QKᵀ)V; the paper's only change is ÷√d_k ("scaled dot-product attention"); d_k = length of a key vector (3 in the toy, 512 in practice).
2. CampusX ~10:00–20:00: QKᵀ is 9 dot products, so it has a mean and a variance; **longer vectors → larger variance**. Code: 1 000 pairs of random vectors, histogram of their dot products: d = 3 spans about −2 to 2, d = 100 about −10 to 10, d = 1 000 about −30 to 30.
3. CampusX ~20:00–28:00: softmax of widely spread numbers gives ~99% / ~1%; with backprop the "training focuses on the big numbers" and the small ones get vanishing gradients. **Analogy: a class where tall children's raised hands are the only ones the teacher sees.** Low-d embeddings would fix it but hold too little information.
4. CampusX ~30:00: reduce variance by scaling: 10, 20, …, 70 has variance 400; ÷10 → 1…7, variance 4.
5. CampusX ~32:00–45:00: which factor? 1-d vectors: Var(X); 2-d: "roughly 2·Var(X), I cannot prove this"; d-d: d·Var(X). Rule Var(cX) = c²Var(X), so ÷√d brings it back to Var(X). Hence ÷√d_k.
6. StatQuest matrix math 09:00–10:00: the same step on numbers, with the honest caveat that the scaled scores are still widely spread (5.01 to −66.2).

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: the Note proves Var(q·k) = d_k (Vaswani footnote 4), measures the variance growth (about 350× from 3 to 1 000), shows softmax saturation and its gradient, a single query collapsing as d grows, real-sentence weights with/without scaling, and checks the formula against Keras. It also has CampusX's classroom analogy (line 95). The 3b1b doc rates 1074 "ours stronger".
- Possible addition: StatQuest's caveat that √d_k is not a guarantee (their trained toy still spans 5.01 to −66.2), which matches the Note's Extra at line 137.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the Note's histograms of 1 000 dot products as d grows 3 → 1 000, with a toggle to the ÷√d version, which stays fixed in width.
2. Plotly frames: 10 softmax bars for one query as d grows (the Note's Figure "one random query against 10 random keys"); the bars collapse onto one key without scaling and stay spread with it.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why scale:** CampusX is the clearest beginner route (histograms → softmax extremes → classroom analogy → variance rule). 3b1b gives one phrase ("numerical stability"), StatQuest one sentence. CampusX adds the derivation; the others add nothing that CampusX lacks except StatQuest's caveat.
- **Clash 1 (where the gradient dies):** CampusX says the training "focuses on the big numbers" and the *small* ones get vanishing gradients. For a saturated softmax the gradient ∂pᵢ/∂sⱼ = pᵢ(δᵢⱼ − pⱼ) is near 0 for **every** score, the big one included (p ≈ 1 gives 1·(1 − 1) = 0). Vaswani §3.2.1: "extremely small gradients". **The Note (line 85) is right**; CampusX's "big ones still learn" picture is wrong. The classroom analogy is fine as intuition for "only one score matters", not for the gradient.
- **Clash 2 (how exact):** CampusX: Var grows "roughly" like d, "I cannot prove this". For independent entries with mean 0, variance 1: Var(q·k) = d_k exactly (Vaswani footnote 4). **The Note's proof is right**; CampusX's hedge is unnecessary.
- **3b1b "numerical stability":** incomplete, not wrong (3b1b doc §7 item 1).

**Contradictions:** the two clashes above (CampusX only; the Note is right on both).

### 1075 Self-attention: geometric intuition

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Self Attention Geometric Intuition | How to Visualize Self Attention", 5ZgGuujZSbs, 20:52. `transcripts/D075.whisper-en.txt`.
2. 3Blue1Brown Ch 6 13:12–15:44 (value vectors as what to add; Δe added to the embedding): cited in the Note and in the 3b1b doc §4 and §6 item 1.
3. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 18:30–19:30: values scaled by the softmax percentages and added (numbers, not geometry).

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–05:00: recap flow for "money bank": embeddings → W_Q, W_K, W_V → six vectors → scores → ÷√d_k → softmax → weighted sum.
2. CampusX ~05:00: a real word2vec projector (PCA of 200-d vectors): chloride, magnesium, sodium sit together; formal words sit together. Words are arrows.
3. CampusX ~06:00–10:00: 2-d toy, hypothetical numbers; multiplying an embedding by a matrix is a **linear transformation**: one arrow becomes three (q, k, v).
4. CampusX ~10:00–13:00: for bank: q_bank·k_money is small (big angle), q_bank·k_bank large (small angle), assumed 10 and 32; ÷√2; softmax assumed 0.2 / 0.8.
5. CampusX ~13:00–17:00: scale v_money by 0.2 and v_bank by 0.8, add by the parallelogram rule → y_bank, which ends up closer to money: **"self-attention acts like gravity"**. In "river bank", bank would be pulled towards river.
6. 3b1b Ch 6 13:12–15:44: the output is a change Δe added to the word's own vector (the Note's §7.3 already does this, with a GPT-2 measurement).

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: real computed numbers instead of "assumed" ones, stills for each step, the "between the two value vectors" fact (§7.1), the same word in two sentences (§7.2), and the 3b1b Δe step with a GPT-2 measurement (§7.3). The gravity analogy is in (line 117).
- CampusX's real word2vec projector view (beat 2) is not in the Note; one PCA plot of real embeddings (the Note's own GloVe/word2vec if loaded elsewhere; check 1086 for reuse) would make "words are arrows" concrete. Note 1086 owns meaning-as-direction, so link rather than duplicate.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim (the Note's Figure 1 is already a GIF): add a slider for the weight w so y_bank slides along the dotted line from v_bank to v_money.
2. Manim: the same "bank" arrow in "money bank" and "river bank", drawn side by side, ending 2.37 apart (the Note's numbers).

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Geometry of attention:** CampusX's step-by-step arrows plus the gravity image are the clearest beginner picture; 3b1b adds the Δe view (edit, not replace), which CampusX lacks; StatQuest has no geometry here.
- **Clash 1 (CampusX numbers):** 10/√2 = 7.07 and 32/√2 = 22.63 (CampusX says 7.09 and 22.69), and softmax(7.07, 22.63) = (≈0.0000002, ≈1.0), not 0.2 / 0.8. CampusX labels all values "hypothetical", but the softmax pair cannot come from those scores. **The Note, using computed numbers (weights 0.603/0.397), is right.**
- **Clash 2 (which space):** CampusX compares y_bank with the original *embeddings* e_bank and e_money and says it moved "very close to money". y_bank is a weighted average of the **value** vectors, so it lies between v_bank and v_money (the Note §7.1), not between the embeddings. **The Note is right**; CampusX's picture mixes two spaces.
- **3b1b vs Note §7.1:** "output stays between the values" vs "Δe is added" are both right at different levels (3b1b doc §7 item 5); the Note already bridges them.

**Contradictions:** the two CampusX slips above.

### 1076 Why self-attention is attention, and why "self"

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Why is Self Attention called 'Self'? | Self Attention Vs Luong Attention", 22:35. `transcripts/D076.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 28:30–30:30: encoder–decoder attention (queries from the decoder, keys and values from the encoder) next to self-attention; only partly on this question.
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 6 18:27 (cross-attention: "the key and query maps act on different data sets"); doc §7 item 11.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–08:00: recap of encoder–decoder LSTMs; one context vector fails beyond ~30 words; attention gives each decoder step its own context vector cᵢ = Σⱼ αᵢⱼ hⱼ; 4 × 4 = 16 alphas.
2. CampusX ~08:00–11:00: αᵢⱼ = softmax(eᵢⱼ); Luong's eᵢⱼ = sᵢ · hⱼ. Three equations.
3. CampusX ~11:00–18:00: write "turn off the lights" with its queries and keys underneath; y_turn = Σ w₁ⱼ v_j, with w from softmax(q_turn · kⱼ). Side by side: decoder state sᵢ ↔ query, encoder state hⱼ ↔ key **and** value (in Luong the same hⱼ plays both). Same three equations → it is attention.
4. CampusX ~19:00–22:00: Luong works between two sequences (inter-sequence); self-attention within one (intra-sequence) → "self".
5. StatQuest Transformer 28:30–30:30: encoder–decoder attention as the inter-sequence version inside the transformer, with its own weights (bridge to 1082).

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing: the Note has the three equations side by side with coloured substitutions, a check that one Keras layer computes both, weight-matrix shapes (3 × 4 vs 4 × 4), and the Extra on why self-attention needs W_Q/W_K (a word's own dot product dominates otherwise), which CampusX lacks.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: the three Luong equations morph into the self-attention equations: sᵢ turns blue (query), hⱼ splits into orange key and green value; the French row of words slides away and the English sentence appears on both axes.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Is it attention?** CampusX's side-by-side equations are the clearest (only CampusX and the Note pose this question). StatQuest and 3b1b just call both "attention".
- **The Note's extra angle:** without projections, a word's query and key are the same vector, and q·q is usually the largest score, so a word attends mostly to itself. Neither CampusX nor the others say this.
- **Clash:** none. 3b1b's cross-attention wording omits values (doc §7 item 11); CampusX and the Note are precise.

**Contradictions:** none.

### 1077 Multi-head attention

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Multi-head Attention in Transformers", 38:27. `transcripts/D077.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 21:30–22:00: "we can create a stack of self-attention cells … they stacked eight … and they called this multi-head attention. Why eight instead of 12 or 16? I have no idea."
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 6 19:28–23:08 (many heads, W_O as value-ups stapled, heads' Δe summed): doc §4 rows and §6 item 2/8. The Note §5.4 already uses this.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–06:00: recap static → contextual, the self-attention block.
2. CampusX ~06:00–10:00: **"the man saw the astronomer with a telescope"** has two readings (man–telescope vs astronomer–telescope); one self-attention gives one table of weights, so one perspective. Also: a summariser should read a document from several angles.
3. CampusX ~10:00–20:00: two sets W_Q¹, W_K¹, W_V¹ and W_Q², W_K², W_V² → two q, k, v per word → two outputs per word (y_money¹, y_money²).
4. CampusX ~20:00–27:00: matrix form with 4-d toy: X (2 × 4) → Q₁…V₂ → Z₁, Z₂ (2 × 4 each) → concatenate (2 × 8) → × W_O (8 × 4) → 2 × 4, same shape as input; W_O "decides the importance of each perspective".
5. CampusX ~27:00–31:00: the paper: d_model = 512, 8 heads, W's are 512 × 64, 8 × 64 = 512, W_O 512 × 512; **why 64: same computation as one 512 head**, "best of both worlds".
6. 3b1b Ch 6 20:31–23:08: W_O can be cut per head; each head adds its own change; the outputs are summed (Note §5.3 GIF, §5.4).
7. CampusX ~31:00–38:00: BERT layer 0 via a bertviz Colab: head 0 ties man ↔ telescope, another head man ↔ astronomer and astronomer ↔ telescope.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: the Note has the two readings, the 2-head toy, matrix form, per-head W_O with a GIF, low-rank value maps checked on BERT (singular values drop after 64), parameter counts (1 × 512 = 8 × 64), Vaswani Table 3 rows A (answers StatQuest's "why eight? no idea"), a NumPy vs Keras check, and real BERT heads with an interpretation caveat.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: shape-blocks: X (2 × 512) splits into 8 thin 64-wide Q/K/V stripes, 8 attention grids light up in parallel, the stripes concatenate and W_O mixes them; a counter shows the weight count equal to one 512 head.
2. Plotly slider over BERT layer-1 heads on the telescope sentence (the Note's 12-head figure), with "man" and "astronomer" rows highlighted.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why several heads:** CampusX's ambiguous-sentence example plus a live BERT demo is the clearest beginner reason; StatQuest gives none (just "stack of cells"); 3b1b gives "many kinds of update".
- **Why 64 per head:** CampusX explains the cost argument; StatQuest skips it; 3b1b gives the low-rank value map. Clearest together: CampusX for the cost, 3b1b for "each head writes its own change".
- **Clash 1 ("stack"):** StatQuest says the 8 cells are "stacked". In Vaswani §3.2.2 the heads run **in parallel** on the same input and are concatenated; stacking is what the 6 layers do. CampusX ("parallel") and the Note are right; StatQuest's word can mislead a beginner.
- **Clash 2 (reading heads as meanings):** CampusX says each head "captures" one reading. The Note's Extra (line 264) shows head 1 also ties astronomer–telescope (0.44); attention weights show where a head looks, not what it concludes (Jain & Wallace 2019, "Attention is not Explanation"; not opened here). **The Note's caveat is right**; CampusX overstates.

**Contradictions:** the two clashes above.

### 1078 Positional encoding

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Positional Encoding in Transformers | Deep Learning", 73:15. `transcripts/D078.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 08:00–12:30: "Squatch eats pizza" vs "pizza eats Squatch"; alternating sine and cosine squiggles, wider for later embedding positions; add to the embeddings.
3. 3Blue1Brown: not covered in Ch 5–7 beyond "position is encoded" (the 3b1b doc has no 1078 row).

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–08:00: self-attention is parallel (10 000 words at once, unlike an RNN's 10 000 steps) but **blind to order**: "Nitish killed lion" = "lion killed Nitish".
2. CampusX ~08:00–20:00: first idea: append the count 1, 2, 3, 4 as a 513th number. Problems: (a) unbounded (a 500-page book → 100 000); dividing by length makes position 2 = 1.0 in "thank you" but 0.5 in a 4-word sentence; (b) discrete; (c) no relative position.
3. CampusX ~20:00–27:00: want **bounded, continuous, periodic** → sin(pos): bounded in [−1, 1], smooth, but it repeats, so two positions can collide.
4. CampusX ~27:00–33:00: add cos(pos) → a 2-number vector; then sin(pos/2), cos(pos/2) → 4 numbers; keep adding slower pairs until collisions are very unlikely.
5. StatQuest 08:00–11:30: the same with squiggles of growing width; reversing the words changes the sums for the moved words only.
6. CampusX ~33:00–40:00: the paper: PE has d_model numbers and is **added**, not concatenated (concatenation would enlarge every weight matrix).
7. CampusX ~45:00–55:00: the formula PE(pos, 2i) = sin(pos/10000^{2i/d}), PE(pos, 2i+1) = cos(…), worked by hand for "river bank" with d_model = 6: position 0 → [0, 1, 0, 1, 0, 1].
8. CampusX ~55:00–62:00: the 50 × 128 heatmap: left dimensions change fast, right ones barely; **analogy: binary counting** (bit 0 flips every step, bit 3 every 8), but continuous.
9. CampusX ~62:00–72:00: relative position: for every distance k one matrix maps PE(p) to PE(p + k) for all p (Timo Denk's blog); this is why sine and cosine come in pairs.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: the Note has all of CampusX's steps with correct values, the formula worked by hand, the build-up GIF, the heatmap, the binary comparison, the rotation matrix with a worked example (0.540, 0.841 → sin 2, cos 2), and the dot-product-vs-distance figure, plus learned PE (Vaswani Table 3 row E).
- StatQuest's "reverse the words: only the moved words' sums change" (11:30) is a one-picture proof that order now matters; the Note's §3 shows shuffling without PE but not the before/after with PE.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: one sine-cosine pair as a point on a circle; moving k positions rotates it by a fixed angle wherever it starts (the Note's §8 rotation), with 4 pairs turning at different speeds like clock hands.
2. Plotly frames: "Rahul killed the lion" vs "the lion killed Rahul": embeddings + PE per word, showing that without PE the two sets of vectors are the same multiset and with PE they differ.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why positions are needed and why sin/cos:** CampusX's first-principles build-up (count → normalise → sine → sine+cosine → more pairs → formula → binary → rotation) is far clearer than StatQuest's 4-minute squiggle sketch; 3b1b does not cover it. StatQuest adds the "swap the words" demo.
- **Clash 1 (frequency direction):** CampusX at ~33:00 summarises "the frequency of every next pair is more"; at ~45:00 and in the formula each next pair has **lower** frequency (ωᵢ = 1/10000^{2i/d}). The formula is right; the ~33:00 line is a slip. The Note is right.
- **Clash 2 (hand values):** CampusX reads sin 2 ≈ 0.87, sin 3 ≈ 0.19, sin 4 ≈ −0.90, cos 1 ≈ 0.5 from the graph; the true values are 0.909, 0.141, −0.757, 0.540. The Note's table (line 77) is right.
- **Clash 3 (why add):** CampusX: concatenation would double the parameters and "training time will be up by a factor of 2". Vaswani §3.5 says only that PE has the same dimension "so that the two can be summed"; no reason or timing is given. The Note's reason (more rows in every input matrix) is a fair argument; CampusX's "factor of 2 training time" has no source.
- **Clash 4 (StatQuest numbers):** StatQuest gives "go" (second word) the values −0.9 and 0.4; under the Vaswani formula position 1 gives sin 1 = 0.84, cos 1 = 0.54. StatQuest's squiggles are illustrative, not the formula. Use the Note's values.

**Contradictions:** the four clashes above (the Note is right in each).

### 1079 Layer normalization

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Layer Normalization in Transformers | Layer Norm Vs Batch Norm", 46:57. `transcripts/D079.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 34:30: one line ("they had to normalize the values after every step … after positional encoding and after self-attention"). No StatQuest, 3b1b or Khan video teaches layer norm (searched `ytsearch5:"layer normalization" statquest|3blue1brown`; none). **No full outside video.**

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–08:00: normalisation = mean 0, variance 1 (standardisation, min-max); applied to inputs and hidden activations; benefits: stable, faster training, internal covariate shift, slight regularisation (recap of Note 1031).
2. CampusX ~08:00–20:00: batch norm recapped as a table: 5 rows × nodes z₁, z₂, z₃ (7, 5, 4 / 2, 3, 4 …), one μ and σ **per column**, then γ, β per node.
3. CampusX ~20:00–35:00: batch norm on self-attention outputs: sentences "Hi Nitish" and "How are you today" (embedding 3), padded to 4 rows with zero vectors → tensor (2, 4, 3); stack and normalise each column: the padding zeros distort μ and σ; with batch 32 and longest sentence 100, most rows are zeros.
4. CampusX ~35:00–40:00: layer norm on the same table: one μ, σ **per row** (per observation), γ and β still per feature.
5. CampusX ~40:00–46:00: on the padded sentences: each word normalised by its own 3 numbers; padding rows do not affect real words.

**What the Note lacks**
- The Note goes beyond CampusX: a real IMDB batch (72% padding) showing batch-norm values drifting with padding while layer-norm values stay put, the masked-attention Extra (padding rows do not stay zero), and pre-norm vs post-norm.
- **Missing evidence on why BN fails in transformers beyond padding.** Padding can be masked out of batch statistics, so padding alone is not the whole reason. Shen et al. 2020 ("PowerNorm: Rethinking Batch Normalization in Transformers", ICML; not opened here, verify before citing) measured that batch statistics in NLP fluctuate strongly between batches and that BN trains worse even with padding handled. Add one Extra citing it.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: the (2, 4, 3) padded tensor as a 8 × 3 table; a highlight sweeps down columns (BN) then along rows (LN), with μ and σ updating; padding rows greyed.
2. Plotly frames (the Note's IMDB data): pad the batch to more positions; the BN value of a fixed real word slides while its LN value stays fixed.

**CampusX vs the others**
- CampusX is the only full beginner explanation; the table view (columns vs rows) is clear. StatQuest gives one line.
- **Clash 1 (StatQuest):** "they normalize the values after positional encoding". Vaswani §3.1 and §5.4: LayerNorm after each sub-layer (LayerNorm(x + Sublayer(x))); after embedding + PE they apply **dropout**, not normalisation. **Vaswani is right**; the Note (§7.2) follows the paper.
- **Clash 2 (CampusX, padding row):** CampusX says a padding row stays 0 after layer norm (0 − μ)/σ. With σ = 0 the layer uses ε, and the output is β (not 0) after γ, β; it is harmless only because padding is masked later. Minor.
- **Angle CampusX lacks:** the reason beyond padding (Shen et al. 2020); see "What the Note lacks".

**Contradictions:** the two clashes above.

### 1080 The transformer encoder

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Transformer Architecture | Part 1 Encoder Architecture", 54:58. `transcripts/D080.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 22:30–23:00 (residual connections: let self-attention "establish relationships among the input words without having to also preserve the word embedding and positional encoding information"); StatQuest matrix-math (KphmOJnLAdI) 12:00 (residual added to the attention output, as a matrix).
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 7 (MLP: per-token, added back; d_ff = 4 d_model; ~2/3 of parameters; facts in MLPs): in the 3b1b doc §4 rows for 1080.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–12:00: transformer = box with encoder and decoder; N× = 6 identical blocks (identical design, **different weights**); each block = multi-head attention + feed-forward network, wrapped in Add & Norm.
2. CampusX ~12:00–18:00: input block for "How are you": tokenise → embed (512) → add positional encoding → x₁, x₂, x₃.
3. CampusX ~18:00–28:00: multi-head attention → z₁…z₃ (512 each); residual: add x; layer norm per vector. Why norm: attention outputs have no fixed range.
4. CampusX ~30:00–37:00: FFN: 512 → 2048 (ReLU) → 512 (linear); W₁ 512 × 2048, b₁ 2048, W₂ 2048 × 512, b₂ 512; a 3 × 512 matrix in, 3 × 512 out; then Add & Norm again → next block.
5. StatQuest 22:30: residual connections let each sub-layer focus on one job without having to carry the original embedding + position forward.
6. 3b1b Ch 7 (doc): the MLP runs on each token separately and its output is **added** to the stream; its rows can be read as questions and its columns as directions written back.
7. CampusX ~37:00–54:00: three "why" questions: residuals (stable training as in ResNet; keep original features if attention hurts; "I don't know the correct answer"), FFN (non-linearity; Geva et al. 2021 "key-value memories", 2/3 of parameters), 6 blocks (language is complex; 6 was best empirically).

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: the Note has the full walk-through with shapes, exact parameter counts, dropout 0.1 (an Extra CampusX omits), He et al.'s degradation argument with the caution that "residuals stop vanishing gradients" needs care, Geva and 3b1b's reading of W₁/W₂, SwiGLU, and Table 3 depth results.
- StatQuest's one-sentence reason for residuals (beat 5) is a beginner-friendly addition to §7.1.
- Wording risk at Note line 270–271: "each value vector is a linear function of the input" and "the ReLU is the only activation function in the block" are true, but a reader may conclude attention is linear; the softmax weights depend non-linearly on the input. Add half a sentence.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: "How are you" as three 512-strips flowing through one block: attention mixes across rows (arrows between strips), Add & Norm, then FFN acting on each strip separately (widen to 2048, shrink to 512), Add & Norm; repeat ×6 with a parameter counter.
2. Plotly: per-sub-layer parameter bars for one block (attention 1.05M vs FFN 2.10M, the Note's counts) showing FFN ≈ 2/3.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Block walk-through:** CampusX is the clearest beginner tour with exact shapes; StatQuest omits the FFN in its main example; 3b1b covers only the decoder-only GPT stack.
- **Why residuals:** CampusX says "no one knows" and offers speculation; StatQuest offers one clear reason; the Note cites He et al. 2016 and Vaswani (who cites He). **The Note is the best-sourced.** CampusX's "no one knows" is too strong: the motivation is published (He et al. 2016, §1, §3.1; the Note §7.1 quotes it).
- **Clash (is attention linear?):** CampusX ~47:00: "all the operations [in self-attention] are linear in nature", so the FFN supplies the non-linearity. Wrong: softmax(QKᵀ/√d)V is non-linear in the input (softmax, and the product of two linear functions of x). The FFN supplies a per-token non-linearity, not the only one. **CampusX is wrong**; the Note is right but should say so (see "What the Note lacks").
- **Facts in the FFN:** CampusX, 3b1b and the Note agree (Geva et al. 2021), each saying it is not fully solved.

**Contradictions:** CampusX's "self-attention is linear" and "nobody knows why residuals" (both against published sources).

### 1081 Masked self-attention

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Masked Self Attention | Masked Multi-head Attention in Transformer", 60:54. `transcripts/D081.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest matrix-math video (KphmOJnLAdI) 15:30–19:30: decoder self-attention in training; "we don't want tokens to cheat and peek at what comes next"; add a mask matrix of 0 and −∞ to the scaled scores; softmax gives SOS 100% on itself.
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 6 11:05 (mask to −∞, every position predicts in training): in the 3b1b doc §4 ("Masking with −∞: ours stronger") and §6 item 2 (grid of dots, used in the Note).

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–08:00: the key sentence: "the transformer decoder is autoregressive at inference time and non-autoregressive at training time". Autoregressive = each new value conditioned on the previous ones (stock price Wed 29, Thu 30 → Fri). The LSTM seq2seq decoder already worked this way.
2. CampusX ~15:00–28:00: assume autoregressive in both: inference on "I am fine" → <start> → मैं → (wrong) word → … ; training on "How are you" with **teacher forcing**: the next input is always the true word from the data, whatever the model output.
3. CampusX ~28:00–33:00: autoregressive training is slow: a 300-word target means 301 runs of the decoder per example. But with teacher forcing every step's input is already known → run all steps at once.
4. CampusX ~33:00–45:00: running "आप कैसे हैं" through self-attention in one go lets "आप" mix in "कैसे" and "हैं" (0.8/0.1/0.1), words that do not exist yet at inference: **data leakage** ("cheating").
5. CampusX ~45:00–57:00: inside self-attention the culprits are w₁₂, w₁₃, w₂₃; add a mask matrix with −∞ above the diagonal to the scaled scores; softmax(−∞) = 0; each word uses only itself and earlier words. Parallel and no leakage: "best of both worlds".
6. StatQuest matrix math 17:30–19:30: the same with numbers (SOS: 100% on itself, 0% on "vamos").
7. 3b1b Ch 6 11:05: the masked triangle on a grid of dots; every position predicts its next token in the same pass.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: the Note has teacher forcing, a hand-worked row, the whole matrix, the 3b1b dot grid, checks against Keras' causal mask, a test that the future has no influence, a test that one pass equals many steps, a timing measurement, and the GPT link.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim (the Note's grid of dots): the score grid fills, then the upper triangle turns to −∞ and its dots vanish; row softmax renormalises each row.
2. Plotly frames: "one pass equals many steps": the outputs of the step-by-step loop appear one row at a time next to the one-pass outputs, matching to rounding (the Note's §7.3 data).

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Why mask:** CampusX's slow-then-leaky-then-mask argument is the clearest *why* (no other source explains the training/inference split as carefully); StatQuest gives the clearest numbers; 3b1b the clearest picture.
- **Clash (can a model generate all words at once?):** CampusX ~15:00: "these types of models don't exist … there is no other option" than autoregressive generation. Non-autoregressive generation exists: Gu et al. 2018, "Non-Autoregressive Neural Machine Translation" (ICLR; not opened here, verify before citing), generates all target words in parallel (with a quality cost). **CampusX overstates**; "standard text generators are autoregressive" is the defensible claim. The Note does not repeat the claim.

**Contradictions:** the one above (CampusX only).

### 1082 Cross-attention

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Cross Attention | Transformer Decoder", 34:07. `transcripts/D082.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 28:30–31:00 (encoder–decoder attention: "keep track of the significant words in the input"; query from the decoder, keys and values from the encoder; separate weights) and matrix-math video 20:00–21:00 ("the same as calculating standard self-attention except that the V and K matrices" come from the encoder).
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 6 18:27: in the doc §7 item 11 (his wording omits that values also come from the encoder).

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–08:00: in the decoder diagram, the second attention gets two arrows from the encoder and one from the decoder. Example "I like eating ice cream" → "मुझे आइसक्रीम खाना पसंद है": at step 3 the next word depends on (1) what the decoder has generated (self-attention) and (2) the input sentence (cross-attention): a table of how strongly each output word relates to each input word.
2. CampusX ~08:00–15:00: differences in input: one sequence (self) vs two (cross).
3. CampusX ~15:00–22:00: processing: **queries from the output (Hindi) words, keys and values from the input (English) words**; same dot product, scaling, softmax, weighted sum.
4. StatQuest Transformer 29:00–31:00: the same flow with numbers and "don't drop the 'don't'" motivation ("Don't eat the delicious looking pizza").
5. CampusX ~22:00–27:00: output: one vector per *output* word, each a mix of the input words (e.g. हम = 50% we, 30% are, 20% friends).
6. CampusX ~27:00–31:00: it mirrors Bahdanau/Luong attention; Vaswani §3.2.3 says so ("mimics the typical encoder-decoder attention mechanisms").
7. CampusX ~31:00–34:00: uses: translation, QA, image captioning, text-to-image, text-to-speech.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: the Note has the formula, a hand example, a Keras check, the output shape, a trained model's cross-attention map, the RNN-attention comparison and uses.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: two sentences, Hindi on the left emitting blue query arrows, English on the right emitting orange keys and green values; a cross-attention grid (output rows × input columns) lights up; each Hindi word's output is drawn as a mix of English value vectors.
2. Plotly: the Note's trained model cross-attention map, with a slider over decoder layers.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **What cross-attention is for:** CampusX's "two things the next word depends on" is the clearest beginner framing; StatQuest's "don't lose the 'don't'" is a good concrete stake; 3b1b gives one sentence.
- **Clash (who gives what):** 3b1b's "key and query maps act on different data sets" leaves out values; CampusX, StatQuest, Vaswani §3.2.3 and the Note: queries from the decoder, **keys and values** from the encoder. The 3b1b doc already records this; CampusX is right.
- **Minor:** CampusX draws the weight table with input words as rows and output words as columns at ~05:00; in QKᵀ the queries (output words) are rows. Keep the Note's row convention.

**Contradictions:** none in CampusX beyond the layout note.

### 1083 The transformer decoder

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Transformer Decoder Architecture | Deep Learning", 48:26. `transcripts/D083.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 26:30–32:00 (decoder: EOS/SOS start, masked self-attention with decoder-specific weights, residuals, encoder–decoder attention, fully connected layer + softmax over the output vocabulary) and matrix-math video (KphmOJnLAdI) 15:30–23:00 (the decoder with all matrices, mask, fully connected layer 2 → 5 outputs, softmax per row).
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 5 20:22–25:42 (unembedding, logits, softmax): doc §4 ("ours weaker: not called unembedding") — now owned by Note 1088.

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–12:00: box of boxes: 6 encoder blocks, 6 decoder blocks (same design, different weights; "like iPhones with different apps"); a decoder block = masked self-attention + cross-attention + FFN, each with Add & Norm.
2. CampusX ~12:00–18:00: training pair "we are friends" → "हम दोस्त हैं"; input block: **shift right** (prepend <start>), tokenise, embed (512), add positional encoding → x₁…x₄.
3. CampusX ~18:00–24:00: masked multi-head attention (z₁ sees only <start>; z₄ sees all four); Add & Norm.
4. CampusX ~24:00–30:00: cross-attention: queries from the decoder vectors, keys and values from the encoder output; Add & Norm.
5. CampusX ~30:00–35:00: FFN 512 → 2048 (ReLU) → 512; Add & Norm; repeat for 6 blocks → final vectors.
6. CampusX ~35:00–46:00: output: linear layer with V neurons (V = Hindi vocabulary, e.g. 10 000; weights 512 × 10 000) → logits → softmax → probabilities → highest = "हम"; same for every position: हम, दोस्त, हैं, <end>.
7. StatQuest matrix math 21:30–23:00: the same output layer on numbers (2 inputs → 5 vocabulary outputs, bias, softmax per row).

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: the Note has the shift-right, the √d_model embedding scaling and tied embeddings (Extra), all sub-layers, parameter counts for 6 blocks, the output layer, and training with all positions in one pass (cross-entropy).
- The 3b1b doc's point about the output layer as "dot with every token's vector" (unembedding) is now in Note 1088; 1083 §8 should link it (check that it does).

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: the "हम दोस्त हैं" pair: the target shifts right under <start>; four vectors climb through 6 decoder blocks (masked attention triangle, cross-attention arrows from the encoder stack); at the top each vector becomes a 10 000-bar probability chart whose tallest bar is the next word.
2. Plotly: the loss per position during training for a real small model (the Note's Keras model), showing every position contributes to one loss.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Decoder walk-through:** CampusX is the clearest structural tour (shapes at every step); StatQuest is the clearest numeric one (every matrix); 3b1b covers only the output (unembedding) for GPT.
- **Clash (argmax in training):** CampusX at ~42:00–46:00 describes training as "pick the word with the highest probability" at each position. In training the loss uses the probability of the **true** next word (cross-entropy); argmax is only for reading the output (Vaswani §5; the Note §9). Minor, but a beginner may think training needs argmax.
- **StatQuest's angle CampusX lacks:** the fully connected layer is "just" a 1-layer network, and the decoder's attention weights are separate from the encoder's (StatQuest 27:30, 30:30).

**Contradictions:** the argmax wording (minor; the Note is right).

### 1084 Transformer inference

**Sources**
1. CampusX, "Transformer Inference | How Inference is done in Transformer?", 45:12. `transcripts/D084.whisper-en.txt`.
2. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 31:30–33:30: decode "vamos" from EOS, then feed "vamos" back, get EOS, stop.
3. 3Blue1Brown "Large Language Models explained briefly" and Ch 5 1:31–2:35 (predict, sample, repeat): doc §6 item 9 ("reuse the 1084 decode_anim.gif design").

**Teaching path**
1. CampusX ~00:00–08:00: setup: trained English → Hindi model; query "we are friends". The encoder works the same in training and inference; the decoder is non-autoregressive in training and autoregressive in inference.
2. CampusX ~08:00–12:00: encoder runs once → one context vector per English word.
3. CampusX ~12:00–24:00: step 1: <SOS> → embed + PE → masked self-attention (one token: attends to itself) → Add & Norm → cross-attention (one query vs keys/values of we, are, friends) → Add & Norm → FFN → Add & Norm → ×6 → linear (V logits) → softmax → "हम".
4. CampusX ~24:00–36:00: step 2: input <SOS> हम; two vectors through every layer; the mask still zeroes SOS→हम (weights 0.2 / 0 and 0.1 / 0.6 as an example); cross-attention 2 × 3 grid; at the top **only the last vector** goes to the output layer → "दोस्त".
5. CampusX ~36:00–44:00: step 3 → "हैं"; step 4 → <end>; stop. The mask stays on at inference, because training used it ("whatever you do in training, do at prediction").
6. StatQuest 31:30–33:30: the same loop on the tiny model.
7. 3b1b (doc): the same loop for an LLM with sampling instead of argmax (Note 1088).

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: the Note has training vs inference side by side, a trained model, each step with real outputs, the "vous/tu" greedy example, a check that the mask stays on, and Extras on the KV cache and beam search.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames (the Note's decode_anim.gif): one step per frame, the decoder input growing by one word, with the next-word probability bars and the chosen word.
2. Manim: recomputation vs KV cache: at each step, the earlier positions' key/value vectors shown unchanged (greyed) and only the new column computed.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **The loop:** CampusX's step-by-step tour through every sub-layer is the most complete; StatQuest's is the shortest; 3b1b's is the GPT version with sampling.
- **CampusX's angle the others lack:** the mask stays on at inference, with the reason (positions must see the same context as in training).
- **Angle CampusX lacks:** the KV cache (earlier positions never change, so their keys/values need not be recomputed) and sampling/beam search; the Note covers both in Extras.
- **Clash (minor slip):** CampusX ~20:00: "multiplying a scalar with a vector, we get a scalar again … Z1, a 512-dimensional vector": a scalar times a vector is a vector. Harmless.

**Contradictions:** none of substance.

### 1085 The transformer end to end

**Sources**
1. No single CampusX video; the Note reassembles Notes 1060–1084 (its "Built from" lists 3b1b Ch 5 and Vaswani 2017). CampusX D080–D084 together are the CampusX source.
2. StatQuest Transformer (zxQyTK8quyY) 33:30–35:30: summary of the whole model and its extras (normalisation, scaled dot product, extra layers).
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 5 1:31–5:42 (data flowing through embeddings, attention and MLP blocks to a next-token distribution): cited in the Note; doc §4 ("High-level GPT flow: 1085 encoder–decoder only").

**Teaching path**
1. Note §3: the chain of problems (RNN sequential → bottleneck → attention → no recurrence) as a story; StatQuest 34:00 gives the same in one paragraph ("word embedding, positional encoding, self-attention, encoder–decoder attention, residual connections").
2. 3b1b Ch 5 1:31–5:42: one picture of the whole flow, before any detail.
3. Note §4: one sentence through the whole model with shapes and parameter count; §5 training vs inference; §7 how the paper trained it (batching, 8 P100 GPUs, warm-up learning rate, dropout 0.1, label smoothing 0.1); §8 the paper's results (Table 2, Table 3); §9 where the idea went next.

**What the Note lacks**
- The doc notes 1085 covers the encoder–decoder flow only; the decoder-only flow is now in Note 1087, so 1085 §9 should recap and link (check that it does).
- No outside video covers the paper's training recipe (warm-up schedule, label smoothing); the Note is the only source here, from Vaswani §5 and Szegedy 2016.

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: the whole transformer as one zoom: start at 3b1b's "words → vectors → blocks → distribution" overview, zoom into an encoder block, a decoder block, and back out (the build order of Notes 1072–1084 as a map).
2. Plotly: the warm-up learning-rate schedule lrate = d^−0.5 · min(step^−0.5, step · warmup^−1.5) with warmup = 4 000, with a slider for warmup.

**CampusX vs the others**
- CampusX has no end-to-end video; its encoder (D080), decoder (D083) and inference (D084) videos together serve. StatQuest's 36-minute single video is the clearest one-sitting overview for a beginner; 3b1b's Ch 5 opening is the clearest one-picture overview.
- **Clash:** StatQuest says normalisation is applied "after positional encoding" (see 1079); Vaswani does not.

**Contradictions:** none beyond 1079's.

### 1086 Meaning as direction

**Sources**
1. No CampusX video teaches this; CampusX D072 ~08:00 gives the opposite (per-coordinate) picture (see 1072).
2. 3Blue1Brown Ch 5 "Transformers, the tech behind LLMs" (wjZofJX0v4M), 14:37–17:44: nearest neighbours of "tower"; king + (woman − man) lands "kind of" near queen, family relations work better (15:39); Germany − Japan + sushi ≈ bratwurst (16:13); dot product = alignment (16:44); cats − cat as a plurality probe (17:14–17:44). `transcripts/3b1b/ch5-gpt.txt`. Mapped in the 3b1b doc §4, §5 #2.
3. StatQuest, "Word Embedding and Word2Vec, Clearly Explained!!!" (viZrOnJclY0, 16:11), `transcripts/teaching-video/1086-statquest-word-embedding.txt`: 01:30–03:30 random numbers give "great" and "awesome" unrelated values; 04:30–09:30 a tiny network trained to predict the next word puts "Troll 2" and "Gymkata" close; 11:30–14:30 CBOW, skip-gram, 100+ dimensions, negative sampling.

**Teaching path**
1. StatQuest 01:30–03:30: why not random numbers: similar words should get similar numbers; one word may need several numbers ("great" positive vs sarcastic).
2. StatQuest 04:30–09:30: learn the numbers by predicting the next word; plot each word's two weights; after training, words used alike sit together.
3. 3b1b Ch 5 14:37: nearest neighbours of a word share meaning ("tower").
4. 3b1b Ch 5 15:09–15:39: the **difference** between two words is a direction; king + (woman − man) lands near queen, but only roughly; family relations work better.
5. 3b1b Ch 5 16:44–17:44: a dot product reads how much of a direction a word has: the plural probe scores plurals above singulars, and one < two < three < four.
6. Note §7: the same tests pass in GPT-2 small's token-embedding table.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: the Note measures every claim on GloVe-100 (cosines 0.44–0.67 for 11 of 12 gender pairs vs 99% of random pairs much lower; king → king first, queen second; plural probe on 12 held-out nouns) and repeats them on GPT-2 small.
- StatQuest's "how embeddings are learned at all" (beats 1–2) is not here; it belongs to the word-embedding Note that owns word2vec (link it; do not duplicate).

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: the GloVe pairs (man→woman, uncle→aunt, father→mother) projected to 2-d with their arrows drawn parallel; then the woman − man arrow is moved onto "uncle" and lands on "aunt" (the Note's data).
2. Plotly: the plural probe as a bar chart, singular/plural pairs side by side, with a slider adding nouns.

**CampusX vs the others**
- **Clearest:** 3b1b (direction arithmetic, plural probe) for meaning; StatQuest for how the numbers are learned. CampusX has no direction view.
- **Clash (coordinates vs directions):** CampusX D072 labels single coordinates (royalty, athletics). 3b1b and the Note: meaning lives in directions; single coordinates are generally not interpretable. **The Note is right**; this Note exists to fix that picture.
- **Clash (king − man + woman = queen):** the usual telling says it gives queen. The Note measures king itself as the nearest word and queen second; analogy tests (Mikolov et al. 2013; not opened here) exclude the three input words from the search, which hides this. 3b1b's "kind of" agrees with the Note.

**Contradictions:** the two above.

### 1087 GPT: the decoder-only transformer

**Sources**
1. No CampusX video in this range (CampusX D084 ends promising GPT and BERT later).
2. StatQuest, "Decoder-Only Transformers, ChatGPTs specific Transformer, Clearly Explained!!!" (bQ5BoolX9Ag, 36:44), `transcripts/teaching-video/1087-statquest-decoder-only-transformer.txt`: 02:00–07:00 embedding as a one-hot network; 10:00–21:00 masked self-attention on "What is StatQuest?" (scores 5.9 and −25.7 → 100% / 0%); 22:00 "original GPT … stacked 12 masked self-attention cells"; 24:30–25:00 the original GPT reuses the embedding weights "in reverse" for the output; 27:00–32:00 generation from EOS until EOS ("awesome"); 32:30–35:30 decoder-only vs normal transformer.
3. 3Blue1Brown Ch 5 (1:31–5:42 flow; 20:23–21:59 unembedding; 21:28 every position predicts) and Ch 6: in the 3b1b doc §5 #1, §7 items 2, 6, 7, 10; cited in the Note.

**Teaching path**
1. 3b1b Ch 5 1:31–5:42: the GPT flow in one picture: tokens → vectors → [attention, MLP] × L → last vector → distribution → sample → append.
2. StatQuest 32:30–35:30: what changes from the encoder–decoder: one unit, masked self-attention over prompt and answer alike, no encoder–decoder attention.
3. StatQuest 10:00–21:00: masked self-attention on numbers (each word sees itself and earlier words).
4. Note §5–6: token + **learned** position embeddings; GPT-2 block with pre-LN, residual stream, final LayerNorm, GELU.
5. StatQuest 24:30 + 3b1b 20:23–21:59: the output: dot with every token's vector; GPT ties it to the embedding matrix (StatQuest: "the same set of weights … flipped").
6. 3b1b 21:28 + StatQuest 26:00: every position predicts its next token in training; generation repeats predict → pick → append (StatQuest 27:00–32:00).
7. Note §8–9: context size; parameter count of GPT-2 small matrix by matrix (124,412,160) and GPT-3.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential; the Note checks the tying on the released GPT-2 weights and records the unresolved GPT-3 tying (Extra line 274).
- StatQuest's side-by-side summary "three big differences" (35:00) is a compact recap table the Note could use in §3 (if it is not already there).

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim: the Vaswani decoder block (3 sub-layers, post-LN) morphs into the GPT-2 block (2 sub-layers, pre-LN): cross-attention fades out, LayerNorms slide to the inputs, a final ln_f appears.
2. Plotly: GPT-2 small's parameter count as a stacked bar (token embedding, position embedding, 12 × attention, 12 × MLP, LayerNorms), with the tied unembedding drawn as "reused, 0 extra".

**CampusX vs the others**
- CampusX has no GPT video in 1001–1090; StatQuest's numeric walk-through is the clearest beginner start; 3b1b's flow picture is the clearest overview.
- **Clash 1 (StatQuest, positions):** StatQuest's decoder-only model uses sine–cosine positional encoding. GPT uses **learned** position embeddings (Radford 2018 §4.1: "learned position embeddings"; GPT-2's `wpe` table). The Note is right; StatQuest's toy is not GPT here.
- **Clash 2 (StatQuest, "12 masked self-attention cells"):** GPT-1 has 12 layers with 12 heads each (Radford 2018 §4.1, quoted from memory, check against the Note's copy: "12-layer decoder-only transformer with masked self-attention heads (768 dimensional states and 12 attention heads)"). "12 cells" blurs heads and layers.
- **Clash 3 (StatQuest, "autoregressive method"):** StatQuest 12:00 calls masked self-attention "an autoregressive method". The *model* generates autoregressively; the mask is what lets it train all positions at once without seeing the future (Note 1081). Wording only.
- **Clash 4 (StatQuest, mask at inference):** StatQuest 34:30–35:30 says a normal transformer uses masked self-attention "during training … only on the output" and a decoder-only one "all of the time, not just during training", implying the encoder–decoder drops the mask at inference. CampusX D084 ~36:00 and Note 1084 §7 keep the mask at inference, and Note 1084 checks it. **CampusX and the Note are right**.
- **3b1b vs Note (unembedding):** 3b1b counts a separate 617M W_U for GPT-3; GPT-2's weights are tied (checked in the doc); GPT-3 unresolved. The Note handles it.

**Contradictions:** the four StatQuest clashes above.

### 1088 Unembedding and sampling

**Sources**
1. No CampusX video (CampusX D083/D084 use argmax only).
2. 3Blue1Brown Ch 5 (wjZofJX0v4M) 20:23–21:59 (the last vector → 50 000 values via a matrix with one row per token), 22:31 (softmax), 24:04–25:42 (temperature T; T = 0 always picks the most predictable word, "a trite derivative of Goldilocks"; logits); "Large Language Models explained briefly" (LPZh9BOjkQs) 1:07 ("the output tends to look a lot more natural if you allow it to select less likely words"). `transcripts/3b1b/`. Doc §5 #4–5.
3. StatQuest, "Neural Networks Part 5: ArgMax and SoftMax" (KpKog-L9veg; already used for 1014): argmax for prediction, softmax for training. No StatQuest video on temperature or sampling.

**Teaching path**
1. 3b1b Ch 5 20:23–21:59: one matrix turns the last vector into one number per token: a dot product with each token's row.
2. Note §3.2: logits measure alignment once the shared direction is removed (measured on GPT-2).
3. Note §4 + 3b1b 21:28: only the last vector is used to generate, though all positions predict in training.
4. 3b1b 24:04–25:42: softmax with temperature: low T sharpens towards the top token, high T flattens; "once upon a time there was a" at several T.
5. 3b1b brief video 1:07 + Note §6: sampling instead of argmax; greedy decoding repeats itself (Holtzman et al. 2020).
6. Note §7: the logit lens: apply the unembedding to the stream after each block and watch the guess form.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential: the Note measures everything on GPT-2 small (full 50 257 logits, entropy vs T, greedy repetition, logit lens).
- StatQuest's argmax/softmax split (argmax when predicting, softmax when training) is the simplest bridge from Note 1014 to this Note; one linking sentence.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames (the Note's Figure 1 design): GPT-2 next-token bars for "Once upon a time there was a" as T slides from 0.1 to 2, with the "all others" bar.
2. Plotly heat grid: the logit lens, block × position, top token in each cell.

**CampusX vs the others**
- Only 3b1b teaches unembedding and temperature among the video sources; it is clear and visual. CampusX and StatQuest stop at argmax.
- **Clash (3b1b, separate W_U):** 3b1b treats the unembedding as its own matrix; GPT-2 ties it to the embedding (doc §7 item 2; Note 1087 §7.1). The Note is right for GPT-2.
- **Clash (CampusX D083/D084 "pick the highest"):** correct for greedy decoding, but greedy is not what chat models do by default; Holtzman et al. 2020 show maximisation leads to "bland, incoherent" or repetitive text. Not wrong, incomplete.

**Contradictions:** none beyond the tied-matrix point.

### 1089 How the MLP stores facts

**Sources**
1. CampusX D080 ~47:00–50:00 mentions Geva et al. 2021 ("feed-forward layers are key-value memories", 2/3 of parameters) in one paragraph; no full CampusX video.
2. 3Blue1Brown Ch 7 "How might LLMs store facts" (9-Jl0dxWQs8), `transcripts/3b1b/ch7-mlp-facts.txt`: 0:00 Michael Jordan → basketball; 4:49–5:53 Michael and Jordan as nearly perpendicular directions; 6:56 the MLP acts on each vector separately; 8:30–11:12 rows of W_up as questions (M·E + J·E = 2), bias −1, ReLU = AND gate; 13:16–14:19 a column of W_down adds the basketball direction; 16:29 MLPs ≈ 2/3 of GPT-3's parameters (116 billion); 17:30 neurons rarely represent one clean feature.
3. No StatQuest or Khan video on this.

**Teaching path**
1. 3b1b 0:00: the puzzle: "Michael Jordan plays the sport of ___" → basketball; where is the fact?
2. 3b1b 6:56 + Note §3: the MLP in one formula, applied to each position separately and added back.
3. 3b1b 8:30–11:12: a row asks "is this Michael AND Jordan?": dot product 2 for the full name, 1 for one name; bias −1; ReLU leaves 1 or 0: an AND gate.
4. 3b1b 13:16–14:19: the matching column of W_down writes the basketball direction into the stream.
5. Note §6–7: does GPT-2 small know sports? Switch MLPs off, see who writes the answer, find the neurons.
6. 3b1b 17:30 + Note §8: what the toy leaves out: real neurons are not clean single facts (leads to 1090).

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential; the Note tests the toy against GPT-2 small and cites Geva 2021 and Nanda et al. 2023 (which the 3b1b doc said to open before citing; the Note now cites it with a date).

**Animation ideas**
1. Manim (the Note's toy): the vector for "Michael Jordan" vs "Michael Phelps" vs "Alexis Jordan" through one row: dot products 2 / 1 / 1, minus 1, ReLU → 1 / 0 / 0; then the column adds the basketball arrow only in the first case.
2. Plotly: GPT-2 small, probability of the right sport as each layer's MLP is switched off (the Note's §7.1 data).

**CampusX vs the others**
- 3b1b's toy is far clearer than CampusX's one-paragraph mention; CampusX adds nothing here beyond the Geva citation, which it reads correctly.
- **Agreement:** CampusX (Geva), 3b1b and the Note all say the mechanism is not fully solved.
- **Clash:** none found.

**Contradictions:** none.

### 1090 Superposition

**Sources**
1. No CampusX video.
2. 3Blue1Brown Ch 7 (9-Jl0dxWQs8) 17:30–21:14: neurons rarely one clean feature; n perpendicular directions fit in n dimensions; allow "between 89 and 91 degrees" and far more fit in high dimensions; experiment with 10 000 random vectors in 100 dimensions pushed to be more perpendicular (19:06–19:37); "superposition"; sparse autoencoders (21:14). Doc §5 #11, §7 item 9.
3. No StatQuest or Khan video.

**Teaching path**
1. 3b1b 18:03: perpendicular features do not interfere, but only d fit in d dimensions.
2. 3b1b 18:34: relax to "nearly perpendicular"; in 2-d or 3-d it gains nothing.
3. 3b1b 19:06–19:37 + Note §4–5: random high-dimensional vectors are already nearly perpendicular; optimisation makes them more so.
4. Note §6: the room grows exponentially with the dimension (Johnson–Lindenstrauss, Dasgupta & Gupta 2003).
5. Note §7: GPT-2's real token vectors spread out the same way.
6. 3b1b 20:43–21:14 + Note §8: the superposition **hypothesis** (Elhage et al. 2022 toy model): sparse features stored as more directions than neurons; consequences for neurons.

**What the Note lacks**
- Nothing essential; the Note keeps the word "hypothesis" (as the doc requires) and checks 3b1b's experiment.

**Animation ideas**
1. Plotly frames: histogram of pairwise angles for 10 000 vectors in 100-d during optimisation (the Note's §5 experiment), with the 84.3°–95.7° band (5.7° bound) marked.
2. Manim: Elhage's toy: 5 features in 2 dimensions arranged as a pentagon as sparsity rises.

**CampusX vs the others**
- Only 3b1b covers this, clearly; the Note adds proofs and measurements.
- **Clash (89–91 degrees):** 3b1b describes 10 000 vectors in 100 dimensions all ending "between 89 and 91 degrees". The Note's Extra (line 107) shows a bound that rules this out: at least one pair must be 5.7° or more from perpendicular; the point (100× more directions than dimensions, all within about 16°) still holds. **The Note is right**, with a derivation.

**Contradictions:** the 89–91° claim (3b1b).
